THOR—*which he has completed with great effort, never being a partisan*

*of any faction, but drawing from the very sources of the Ethiopian people, namely*
*from Liturgies, Confessions of Faith, the* Organon <note>original: "Organis." This refers to the "Arganona Weddase" (Organ of Praise), a famous Ethiopian collection of hymns to the Virgin Mary.</note>, *and other writings*

HARA <note>Abba Gorgoryos (1595–1658), an Ethiopian monk whose collaboration with European scholars was foundational for Ethiopian studies in the West.</note>, *which we owe to* JOB LUDOLF <note>Hiob Ludolf (1624–1704), a German scholar regarded as the founder of Ethiopian studies in Europe.</note>, *a Man of immortal*
*memory and praise—by his own mind and character, but will especially*
*admire the wonderful ways of the Lord and His mercy, by which*
*He preserved a most extensive Church of Christ, as if in the very heart*
*of barbarism, and adorned it with a simplicity of faith; a Church*
*of which barely a rumor once reached our people, because it was*
*accused of the crime of heresy by opposing parties, simply because*
*it refused to depart from that unique rule of faith and fountain*
*of truth, namely, the Holy Scriptures.*

*For here we learn that the Ethiopians believe nothing in matters*

VII

-> P R E F A C E <-

If we consider this a little more deeply, it must certainly be a great comfort to our Lutheran Church—with whom they profess the same <term>equally precious faith <note>original Greek: ἰσότιμον πίστιν; a reference to 2 Peter 1:1, suggesting the Ethiopian Christians share the same foundational beliefs as the Lutherans</note></term>—that she has found so many witnesses of the truth here. Since the Most Reverend **AUTHOR** has proven all these things from the Ethiopians' own confessions of faith, using the very words of the Ethiopian vernacular—except for those passages where, due to a lack of specific Ethiopic type <note>At the time of printing, specialized fonts for Ge'ez or Amharic were rare, so the author substituted Latin translations where the characters were unavailable</note>, the Latin version has been transcribed here—we can altogether dispense with further investigation and review. We are fully persuaded that the godly, who will turn these sacred labors to their own use, will perceive remarkable fruits from them. For what remains, we pray to the Supreme Deity <note>God</note> that He may preserve this most faithful steward of His mysteries—**speaking the truth in love** <term>speaking the truth in love <note>original Greek: ἐν ἀγάπῃ ἀληθεύοντα; a reference to Ephesians 4:15</note></term>, an ornament of the learned world and especially of his own most glorious nation, and a <term>steward <note>original: promum condum; an ancient term for a household manager who distributes and stores provisions</note></term> of the more sacred doctrines and of the languages which both the East and the West have produced—for the amplification of His glory, [to the] Church...

# HISTORY OF THEOLOGY
## OF ABYSSINIA <note>Abyssinia was the historical name for the Ethiopian Empire in North-Eastern Africa.</note>

*As we are about to examine the theology of the Ethiopians*, it will be worth the effort to first provide some preliminary remarks regarding their *Name*, *Region*, and *Conversion*, for the sake of a better understanding. Once these things have been set forth, we may be able to proceed more easily and successfully into the consideration of the subject itself. Furthermore, since words are the signs of things and persons, it seems that we must consider first and foremost that the word *Ethiopian* owes its origin and meaning to the Greeks, and signifies a person with a dark and black face, a) because the appearance of these people—

---

<margin>a) For the name *Ethiopian* <note>original Latin: Aethiops</note> descends from the Greek *aitho* <note>original: αἴθω</note>, which denotes black, and is expressed as if "one who is scorched in appearance" <note>original: αἴθων τὴν ὄψιν</note>. Indeed, *aitho* is the same as "I burn," so that if you hear someone named an Ethiopian, [you hear of] a man—</margin>

<summary>This page introduces a historical and theological study of Abyssinia, beginning with the Greek etymology of the name "Ethiopian," which refers to a "scorched" or dark complexion.</summary>

<keywords>Ethiopia, Abyssinia, theology, etymology, Greek language, church history</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>The previous page likely ended with the first syllable of the word "forma" (shape/form), as this page begins with the suffix "ma."</meta>

# HISTORY OF THEOLOGY

...form, but is usually black through their entire life, so that this name has even given rise to a proverb. β) To the Hebrews they are called <term>Chush</term> <note>original Hebrew: כושׁ Chuſch</note>, a name that traces its origin to the firstborn son of *Ham* and brother of *Mizraim* <note>the biblical progenitor of the Egyptians</note>, from whom this nation undoubtedly descends. γ) Although this name does not refer so much to the nature of these peoples, it is nonetheless correctly regarded as a Proper Name, and specifically a patronymic <note>a name derived from a father or ancestor</note>. For this reason, we find the opinion of the otherwise most learned philologist <term>**SAMUEL BOCHART**</term> less convincing. δ) Bochart understood the <term>Chushites</term> <note>original Hebrew: כושימ</note> not to be our Ethiopians, whom we are now discussing, but only the Arabs—specifically the ancient *Scenitae* <note>literally "tent-dwellers"</note> and the more recent *Saracens*. However, if that were the case, how could the Prophet **JEREMIAH**, in his own time, have said: *“Can the Ethiopian change his skin?”* <note>original Hebrew: הלא יהפך כושי עורו</note> ε) especially since it is well enough established that neither the Arabs nor the Saracens were so black and dark in face, whereas regarding the Ethiopians, on the contrary, it is...

---

...you should imagine a person with a scorched face, dusky in color, or plainly black. From this, it also appears that both "red" and "white" Ethiopians are not incorrectly called so, since the former come very close to blackness, while the latter—as <term>**LUDOLF**</term> <note>Hiob Ludolf, a 17th-century scholar and father of Ethiopian studies in Europe</note> says somewhere—*rejoice in a truly unpleasant whiteness*.

β) The proverb is as follows: *An Ethiopian does not become white* <note>original Greek: Αἰθίοψ οὐ λευκαίνει</note>, or as the seventy translators <note>the Septuagint, the ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament</note> rendered that Hebrew passage from Jeremiah 13:23: *The Ethiopian will not change his skin* <note>original Greek: ὁ αἰθίοψ οὐκ ἀλλάξεται τὸ δέρμα αὐτῶ</note>. From this, a common proverb often used by the Latins for an impossible and vain task is *“to wash an Ethiopian”* <note>original Latin: Aethiopem lauare; meaning to labor in vain</note>.

γ) This is evident because the Ethiopians are called <term>Cushites</term> <note>original Hebrew: כושיים</note> in Holy Scripture itself, as can be shown from 2 Samuel 18:21, from the title of Psalm 7:1, from Amos 9:7, and Jeremiah 38:7, 10, 12.

δ) In his work *Phaleg*, Book IV, chapter 2.

ε) Chapter 13, verse 23.

<summary>This page explores the etymology of the word "Ethiopian," linking it to the biblical "Cush" and debating whether the term refers to inhabitants of Africa or Arabia. The author argues against Samuel Bochart, citing the Prophet Jeremiah to prove the term refers to people with permanently black skin.</summary>

<keywords>Ethiopia, Cush, Samuel Bochart, Jeremiah, Hiob Ludolf, Etymology, Ham, Scenitae, Saracens, Philology, Biblical Geography</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>The previous page established that the biblical name 'Cush' refers to Ethiopia, refuting the idea that it only refers to Arabia, and concluded with a discussion on the various skin tones of Ethiopians.</meta>

->### ETHIOPIANS<-

...is clearly evident. I would not want, however, to fall into the other extreme, as if no other nation besides this one was known to the ancients by the name **Cushites** <note>original Hebrew: כּוּשִׁים *Kushim*</note>. It will suffice for us to have shown that the Ethiopians were truly Cushites, and were called so by the Jews as well as by other learned men. ε) Beyond the names already reviewed, the name **Abyssinians** <note>original Latin: *Habessinorum*</note> is no less frequent. It is Arabic in origin, ζ) and while it seems they initially detested it, η) in the course of time they began to accept even that. θ) To which merit—

---

ε) For besides the **Septuagint** <note>the ancient Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible</note> translators, whose words we cited above, **Benedictus Pererius** cites **Philo, Josephus, Jerome,** and all the **Rabbis** in support of this opinion in his *Commentary on Genesis* (Book III, on Chapter II, 13, and Book XV, on Chapter X, 6). I add **Martin Luther, Avenarius, Osiander, Calovius,** and others.

ζ) For to the Arabs, <term>Habash</term> <note>original Arabic: حَبَش</note> signifies a "conglomeration" or a mixture of different peoples. Hence the terms *al-Habashah*, *ahbash*, and *hubush* <note>original Arabic: اَلْحَبَشَةُ اَحْبَاشٌ حُبُوشٌ</note> designate a crowd of people mixed from various tribes and nations, as **Jacob Golius** learnedly observes in his *Arabic Lexicon*, page 567.

η) It is easily apparent from what was said before that this name—being unpleasant in its etymology—was even more unpleasant to the Ethiopians, either because they were ignorant of what it meant, or because they understood its etymology all too well. This reason moved **Tzaga-Zaab**, the Ambassador of the King of Ethiopia to the Roman Pontiff <note>the Pope</note>, to attack **Matthew the Armenian** (the first Ethiopian Ambassador to Europe) in his *Confession of Faith* (Part I, §13) for calling his people "Abyssinians." For he says in that place: "He is not to be named, as Matthew falsely indicated, the Emperor of the Abyssinians, but of the Ethiopians."

θ) From which the celebrated **Hiob Ludolf** <note>a 17th-century German scholar regarded as the founder of Ethiopian studies</note> reports in his *Commentary* (Book I, chapter 1, number 16, page 52) that even his friend **Abba Gregory** <note>an Ethiopian monk and Ludolf's primary source of information</note> did not disdain to be called an **Abyssinian**, but often named himself **Habesha** <note>original Ethiopic: ሐበሻ</note> in his letters.

<summary>This page argues that "Cushite" is a valid biblical term for Ethiopians and explains the Arabic etymology of the name "Abyssinian," noting that it originally meant a mixture of peoples and was initially offensive to the Ethiopians.</summary>
<keywords>Cushites, Abyssinians, Habesha, etymology, Hiob Ludolf, Tzaga-Zaab, Matthew the Armenian, Arabic Lexicon, Septuagint</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nomenclature of the Ethiopian people, transitioning from the Arabic term "Abyssinian" to the native term "Ge'ez" and exploring the geographic boundaries of the kingdom.</meta>

# HISTORY OF THEOLOGY

...to [which] you might add another name, **Ge'ez** <note>original Ethiopic: ግዕዝ; the author transliterates this as *Gezim*</note>, by which the Ethiopians usually call themselves. This name indicates men who are free, as well as noble and renowned for their valor and skill in arms. ι) Therefore, in terms of its meaning, it seems to correspond with the very ancient name of the Germans, the **Franks** <note>original: *Francken*</note>, who called themselves **Franks**, or "the free," who were subject to no external power. κ) Moreover, you would not be in error if you called them **Sabeans** and **Himyarites** <note>original Latin: *Homeritas*</note>, as these are the same. λ) For our purposes in this treatise, the names **Ethiopians** and **Abyssinians** <note>original Latin: *Habessinorum*</note> are preferred, as they are the most familiar. For if we were to call them **Moors** <note>original Latin: *Mauros*</note>, as the Germans do, we would be forming an altogether too general a concept of them. μ) Regarding the **REGION**, it is most truly called **Abyssinia** <note>original Latin: *Habassia*</note> or, as they themselves are accustomed to name it, the **Kingdom of Ethiopia** <note>original Ethiopic: መንግሥተ : ኢትዮጵያ, *Manghesta Ithjopja*</note>. It lies between approximately the eighth and sixteenth degrees of northern latitude, according to the most recent—

---

ι) For the Ethiopian root **Ge'ez** <note>original: ግዕዝ or ገአዝ, *Geeza*</note> enjoys a double meaning: α) it signifies moving camp with an army, and β) gaining freedom through arms, and thus becoming free. Hence, if you say **Agazyan** <note>original: አግአዝያን, *Ag-azianos*</note>, you are naming "free men."

κ) The name **Franks** <note>original: *Francken*</note> is the same as "Free Men," from the ancient *fry* [free] and *Ancken* [Man], from which the diminutive *Aencklein* is still in use today.

λ) For the **Sabeans** and **Himyarites** are the same people from the region of the **Axumites** <note>referring to the Kingdom of Axum in Northern Ethiopia</note>, with the Red Sea lying between them, as stated by **Procopius of Gaza** in his *Commentary on 1 Kings, Chapter 10, verse 1* <note>original Latin: *Lib. III Regum*, using the Vulgate numbering where 1 Kings is the third book of Kings</note>.

μ) For the German word **Moor** <note>original: *Mohr*</note> originated from the Latin *Maurus*. However, these people are distinguished from our Ethiopians by more than one criterion, as is evident to anyone even slightly familiar with geographic history.

<summary>This page explores the etymology of the name "Ge'ez," linking the Ethiopian identity to the concept of freedom and comparing it to the Germanic Franks. It also clarifies the distinction between Ethiopians and "Moors" while defining the geographical coordinates of the Abyssinian kingdom.</summary>

<keywords>Ge'ez, Abyssinians, Franks, etymology, Sabeans, Himyarites, Kingdom of Axum, Procopius of Gaza, geography, Ethiopia</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the geographic description of Ethiopia, moving from general latitudinal coordinates to a specific list of the kingdoms that comprise the empire, while citing various historical and contemporary geographers to correct previous cartographic errors.</meta>

->ETHIOPIA<-

...[most] skillful Geographers' placement, ν) which the Fathers of the Society <note>The Jesuits, known as the *Patres Societatis Iesu*</note> have estimated as one hundred and forty leagues in its latitude. ξ) It otherwise encompasses within its circuit thirty other kingdoms, among which the most famous in former times was **Shewa** <note>original Ethiopic: ሼዋ; transliterated here as *Seva*</note>; it remains today both very extensive and extremely wealthy. The most flourishing is **Amhara** <note>original Ethiopic: አምሐራ</note>, which includes twenty-two other regions under its jurisdiction. Finally, **Tigray** <note>original Ethiopic: ትግሬ; or *Tigra*</note> is one of the principal kingdoms of Ethiopia, and it is the first encountered by those entering Ethiopia. All of these have their own Kings, or rather, as they themselves call them: **Masafint** <note>original Ethiopic: መሣፍንት; *Masafynot*</note>, that is, provincial governors or princes, all of whom, to a man, are subject to the supreme King of the Ethiopians, who therefore calls himself **Negusa Nagast** <note>original Ethiopic: ንግሥተ ነገሥት; *Nyagusa Nagasyty*</note>, that is, King... <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword *Regem*, meaning "King of Kings."</note>

---

ν) For the ancient geographers, such as **Abraham Ortelius**, **Gerard Mercator**, etc., in their geographical maps, constantly show Upper Ethiopia extending as far as the Equator <note>original Latin: *Lineam aequinoctialem*</note>. This was perhaps done because they understood that **Paolo Giovio** <note>original Latin: *Paulum Iovium*; see his *Histories*, Book 18</note> asserted that the kingdom of Scena or **Shewa** <note>original Ethiopic: ሼዋ; *Sebba*</note> faced the Antarctic pole at an elevation of 22°; however, practical application on a globe will easily show that the situation is otherwise. Or, what seems a more likely reason is that **Ptolemy** extended the sources of the Nile far beyond the Equator; perhaps these writers followed him like sheep following a ram.

ξ) These men [the Jesuits] were not unskilled in the discipline of geography; therefore, using astrolabes and other mathematical instruments, they were able to judge better the length and breadth of this kingdom, although they did not dare to designate the longitude with equal certainty. On this matter, Father **Balthasar Telles** <note>original Latin: *Balth. Tellezius*</note> deserves to be consulted in his *General History of Upper Ethiopia* <note>original Portuguese: *Hist. Geral. de Etiopia a alta*</note>.

o) The Kings of the Ethiopians do indeed have other names. π) Such traces include, for example, the **ritual of sacrifice** (common to all nations); the **Sabbath**, or the practice of hallowing the seventh day of the week; the **abstinence from eating the displaced sinew** <note>original Latin: *membri laesi comestione*; a reference to the Jewish dietary law in Genesis 32:32</note>; likewise, **abstaining from pork**; the custom of administering **circumcision** to infants as well as to adults (if they were foreigners and uncircumcised); and a mixture of other Jewish rituals. All of these were so familiar to this people that, if you except the use of sacrifices, they constantly practice the rest even today. ...out of her excessive admiration for Solomon, she also brought back much of the Religion of the Israelites with her. ...[the power] of the divine calling, which stirred up this Queen to travel so great a distance to hear the wisdom of Solomon. Indeed, I would not dare to say that the name *Seba* uniquely designates Ethiopia to ancient writers, since they also understood it as Arabia in their own way; but from this it would only follow that the **Queen of Sheba** <note>original Hebrew: מלכת שבא (Malkat Sheba)</note> ruled over both shores of the Red Sea, by which reasoning Ethiopia is again not excluded.

Through PHILIP, σ) the Supreme Deity had begun to lay again the distinguished foundations for their conversion. But, surprisingly, not even then was the most merciful GOD able to realize His desire, although there is no doubt that after the outpouring of the Holy Spirit, just as it went forth **into all the world** <note>original Greek: εἰς πᾶσαν τὴν οἰκουμένην (eis pasan tēn oikoumenēn)</note>, so too reached these people not only the sound, but also the foot of the Apostles τ). For *Frumentius*, having obtained permission to visit his homeland, which was Phoenicia, set out for Alexandria. There he recounted to the Blessed *Athanasius* <note>Athanasius the Great, the Bishop of Alexandria and a central figure in early Christian theology</note> how successfully Christianity had been planted through him. Athanasius, greatly rejoicing at this, sent *Frumentius* back again to continue the work of the Lord, having ordained him as the Bishop of Ethiopia,

---

σ) Acts Chapter 8, verses 29 and following. Regarding this history, see the Most Reverend Mr. CHRISTOPH HEINRICH ZEIBICH in the little book he calls *Philip’s first and other address* <note>original German: "Philippi erster und anderer Zuspruch"</note>, etc.

τ) I cannot indeed agree with the opinion that the Apostles themselves were the authors of those statutes sent to the Ethiopians—which the Ethiopians call **T’izazat** <note>original Ethiopic: ትእዛዛት (Commandments)</note> and the Greeks call **Diatagas** <note>original Greek: Διαταγὰς (Constitutions/Dispositions)</note>—which are mentioned in the great *Codex of Canons and Councils*. For this seems highly suspicious to me because, while the Apostles sent complete Epistles to their churches (and usually just one or another of them), these documents, by contrast, insert specific decrees from almost all of them, and not only from the Apostles, but also Apostolic...

# THE ABYSSINIANS
<page-num>11</page-num>
<header>ABYSSINIANS 11</header>

...we venerate the divine [scriptures] <note>The original Latin "dienum" likely refers to "divinum," meaning the divine writings or the Ethiopian biblical canon</note> as entirely Canonical, along with the three Ecumenical Creeds <note>The creeds of the early unified Church: the Apostles', Nicene, and Athanasian/Constantinopolitan Creeds</note>, and other decrees of the pious as well as orthodox Doctors. This continued until, by the fraud of Satan, it happened in the 6th Century that—being offended more by the **people** at the Council of Chalcedon than by its **doctrines**—they, along with the Church of Alexandria, also withdrew from the unity of the Latin as well as the Greek Church.

$\chi$) From that time on, all things seemed to fall further into decline. Although the Latins often busied themselves trying to place a barrier against this evil and to propose better things to them—so much so that very often their efforts were not without success— [the text continues]...

<column-break/>

[This section explains the arrival of the first missionaries:]
...of a Syrian merchant, who, when he landed on the shore of Ethiopia, was either seized by disease or killed in a slaughter. He had left two sons: **FRUMENTIUS**, whom the Ethiopians called **ABREHAM**, and **AEDESIUS**, whom they called Atzbeha. When they were brought to the King of the Ethiopians, they were welcomed by everyone. After the King died, they were placed in high court offices by the Queen. On that occasion, they provided help and counsel to Christians traveling to those places. While they themselves were held in great affection by the Queen and also by the King, her son, they obtained permission to summon Clerics to perform their sacred rites everywhere in that land and to build Monasteries. There, nine monks in particular—coming without a doubt from Egypt—successfully promoted that work in various Kingdoms with the help of **GOD**. You may find eulogies for these monks in the works of an Ethiopian Poet: *Eulogy VI, October 7; XIV, October 4; XXIV, December 6; XXVIII, November 3, etc.* <note>These references point to the Ethiopian Synaxarium, a calendar of saintly lives</note>

$\chi$) Namely, when in the Council of Chalcedon (*6th Century, Eutyches and his defender Dioscorus, the Patriarch of Alexandria*, had been condemned), and because the latter [Dioscorus] had been especially harshly treated, the Ethiopians took it badly—as they were always most devoted to the Patriarchs of Alexandria. Consequently, they did not merely quietly withdraw from the Latin and Greek Church, but they clearly struck that Council with an anathema <note>A formal curse or excommunication</note>. This was not so much because of a rejection of Eutychianism <note>The belief that Christ has only one nature; the author suggests the Ethiopians claim to reject this heresy even while being "monophysite"</note>—which they themselves wish to be seen as still loathing today—but because of the injury inflicted upon *Dioscorus*.

<page-num>12</page-num>
<header>HISTORY OF ABYSSINIAN THEOLOGY</header>

...the success of their efforts has already appeared; but indeed, obstacles and hindrances almost always presented themselves from both sides, so that the status of Evangelical Truth <note>The author uses "Evangelical Truth" to refer to what he considers the pure or original Christian doctrine.</note> in Ethiopia has hitherto turned out to be, if not worse, at least no better. $\psi$) Oh, that it might happen in our days that what remains of the tenets of Christianity among them might be more clearly proposed to them from the word of God, and that the errors and abuses that have gained strength might be removed, to the greatest benefit of that Church. But concerning both points, under divine auspices, we shall now speak.

---

$\psi$) The first of all Latin Legations <note>A formal diplomatic or religious mission.</note> to the Ethiopians is correctly believed to have occurred in the Year of our Lord 1177, sent by **ALEXANDER III**, Bishop of Rome, to **PRESTER JOHN** <note>original: "PRETEIANVM"; a legendary Christian king of the East or Africa whom Europeans long sought to contact.</note>. A second, and indeed more notable one, occurred toward the end of the *Council of Florence*, around the Year 1439, from the *Abyssinian Emperor* **ZERA JACOB** <note>reigned 1434–1468</note> to **EUGENIUS IV**, Bishop of Rome. At the beginning of the 16th Century, another new Legation from the Bishop of Rome, **CLEMENT VII**, occurred through **FRANCISCO ALVAREZ**, as well as **DAMIÃO DE GÓIS**. This was continued by **JULIUS III**, Supreme Pontiff, who dispatched Bishop **ANDRÉS DE OVIEDO** for their conversion. He was followed by **PEDRO PÁEZ** *of Fremona*, who, if anyone, certainly enjoyed happy success—so much so that the King ordered all Alexandrian rites to be set aside and permitted the Latins to use all theirs instead. But, while **ALFONSO MENDEZ**—a man otherwise of no small learning—abused the King's favor and took for himself a license greater than was just, he "lost both the court and his care" <note>original: "Curiam, Cariamque perdidit"; a Latin proverb playing on the words for the royal court and his affection or flock.</note> due to his excessive rigor. This resulted in the rest of the Fathers being brutally killed, while **MENDEZ** himself, withdrawing to India, exchanged life for death on June 29, 1656, in the 73rd year of his life and the 22nd of his exile. Whoever wishes to read more about these things should examine *Peter Heyling's Journey to Ethiopia* <note>original: "Petri Heylings Reiſe nach Aethiopien"; Heyling was a 17th-century Lutheran missionary.</note>.

### TOPIC I
#### ON THE
# THEOLOGY OF THE ETHIOPIANS
## IN GENERAL

Until now, we have outlined the entire matter purely historically, and briefly at that; now we turn to the explanation of the SYSTEM OF ABYSSINIAN THEOLOGY itself. If the entire framework of that system is to be presented as if in a summary <note>original Greek: ὡς ἐν συνόψει</note>, we could hardly bring forward anything more fitting, or indeed more elegant, than the very words of **CLAUDIUS**, *King of the Ethiopians*, which he uses in his Confession of Faith. <margin>α) The occasion for publishing this Confession was provided to CLAUDIUS because he noticed that the Abyssinians were being criticized for supposedly practicing certain Jewish rituals in opposition to Christian liberty. To clear himself and his people of this charge, he issued this Confession. The philologist of incomparable renown, ESDRAS EDZARDI, owned a manuscript of it along with the *Organ of Mary* <note>A collection of Ethiopian hymns and prayers dedicated to the Virgin Mary</note>; from him, the celebrated *Job Ludolf* <note>A 17th-century German scholar regarded as the founder of Ethiopian studies in Europe</note> received a transcript <note>original Greek: ἐν ἀπογράφῳ</note> of it in the year 1656 while staying in Hamburg. That it is genuine...</margin> After he [had set out] in that same [Confession] something like the Apostles' Creed (although...

...[of their] efforts some success has already appeared, but indeed, obstacles and hindrances have almost always presented themselves from both sides. <meta>catchword: Topic <note>original: "Loc."; abbreviation for "Locus," referring to "Locus I" on the following page</note></meta>

<note>ψ) It is widely believed that the first of all Latin <term>Latin: Referring to the Roman Catholic Church and the Western world</term> missions to the Ethiopians was carried out in the year 1177 A.D. by **ALEXANDER III**, Roman Pontiff, to **PRESTER JOHN** <note>original: "Preteianum"; a legendary Christian patriarch-king of the East</note>. A second, and indeed more notable one, occurred toward the end of the *Council of Florence*, around the year 1429, from the *Abyssinian Emperor* **ZARA YAQOB** to **EUGENIUS IV**, Roman Pontiff. At the beginning of the 16th century, another new mission from the Roman Pontiff, **CLEMENT VII**, took place through **FRANCISCO ALVAREZ** as well as **DAMIÃO DE GÓIS**. This was continued by **JULIUS III**, Supreme Pontiff, who dispatched Bishop **ANDRE DE OVIEDO** to convert them. He was followed by **PEDRO PÁEZ** *of Fremona*, who, if anyone ever did, certainly enjoyed happy success here—so much so that the King ordered all Alexandrian <term>Alexandrian rites: The liturgical traditions of the Coptic Church, which Ethiopia followed</term> rites to be set aside and permitted the use of all Latin ones. However, while **ALPHONSO MENDEZ** (a man of otherwise significant learning) abused the King’s favor and took a greater liberty for himself than was right, he—owing to his excessive rigor—as the proverb says, "lost both the Court and the cause" <note>original: "Curiam, Cariamque perdidit"; a Latin pun meaning he lost his standing at court and ruined the mission's purpose</note>. Thus, while the rest of the Fathers were cruelly killed, **MENDEZ** himself retreated to India. There, in the 73rd year of his life and the 22rd of his exile, on June 29, 1656, he "exchanged life for death" <note>A common Latin euphemism for dying</note>. Anyone who wishes to read more about these events should examine *Peter Heyling's Journey to Ethiopia* <note>original: "Petri Heylings Reise nach Aethiopien"</note>.</note>

<summary>This page reflects on the historical difficulties of spreading the "Gospel Truth" in Ethiopia and provides a detailed timeline of Roman Catholic missions from the 12th to the 17th century. It highlights the temporary success of Pedro Páez and the subsequent failure and expulsion of the Jesuits under Alphonso Mendez.</summary>

# TOPIC II
### CONCERNING
# HOLY SCRIPTURE

First and foremost, we must examine the PRINCIPLE OF KNOWING THEOLOGY, which is HOLY SCRIPTURE. For just as no one deserves even the name of Christian who does not fully accept the complete Codex of the Old and New Testaments, so too do the Abyssinians <note>A historical term for Ethiopians.</note>, who take the name of Christians for themselves, recognize the Word of GOD as the *rule and standard for things to be believed and things to be done*. From this, you may often read in their books mention made of the *Mazyhaf Kydusat* <note>original: መጽሐፈ፡ ቅዱሳት፡ (*Mazyhaf Kydusat*)</note> a) or Holy Book, which is the Scripture of the Old and New Testaments. ### TOPIC II. ON THE HOLY SCRIPTURE.

### TOPIC III: ON THE TRIUNE GOD.

-> **God is ONE in Essence**, **THREE in person** <note>original Greek: *hypostasei*</note>. <-

In this chapter, the Abyssinians <note>referring to the people of the Ethiopian Empire</note> also agree with us with one accord <note>original Greek: *homothymadon*</note>. For they know, believe in their hearts, and profess with their mouths—as often as they express the blessed name of God, **Amlak**, in thought and word—that God is one in substance <note>original Greek: *ousiodos*</note>, infinite, eternal, supremely good, supremely just, and therefore (which is, as it were, the source and center of all attributes) independent; furthermore, the creator of heaven and earth, the greatest sustainer, and most worthy of infinite glory, majesty, religious worship, and obedience. δ) For this word <note>*Amlak*</note> derives from the root **Malaka**, which signifies reigning and commanding all things whatsoever, yet without being subject to anything Himself.

***

δ) If you were to derive this word **AMLAK** from the Ethiopic root **Mal'a** <note>መለአ</note> (though this is a bit more distant), which signifies "full," "filling all things," "the whole," "the universe," and "that which is most perfect," you would by this reasoning attribute all perfections to God. For he who lacks even the smallest degree of good can neither be, nor be called, full or most perfect. However, it is more correctly derived from the root **Malaka** <note>መለከ</note>, as it implies an absolute lord—one who commands everyone and allows no one to command him, and so...

### TOPIC III: ON THE TRIUNE GOD

What is clearer in this regard than divine independence—indeed, the entire assembly of attributes, both **inactive and active** <note>original Greek: *anenergeton kai energetikon*; these terms distinguish between God's internal qualities, like eternity, and His external actions, like creation</note>—and the very works of God toward the outside world, which shine forth? With this agree another divine name used by the Abyssinians, **Igziabeher** <note>original: እግዚአብሔር; *Igziabyhar*</note>, that is, **God**, Thrice Greatest and Best. If you look at its etymology, it signifies "Lord of Kingdoms," and by <term>synecdoche</term> <note>a figure of speech where a part represents the whole</note>, the "Lord of the whole world." It also implies that He is, if one may speak this way, most uniquely one in essence. Yet, they firmly assert that just as He is one in respect to **essence** <note>original Greek: *ousia*</note>, so He is three in respect to **subsistence** <note>original Greek: *hypostasis*</note>, ε) and for that reason it is established...

<meta>catchword: et- (and)</meta>

***

The French express all these things in a single word: *Souverain* [Sovereign]. This is the same reason why the Ethiopians do not call their King an "Emperor" <note>original: *Imperatorem*</note>, much less use the term *Amakeh* <note>possibly a transcription or phonetic rendering of a title</note>, but instead address him only as **Negus** <note>original: ንጉሥ; *Negus*</note>, meaning "King." This is evident from the words of King **Claudius** <note>Galawdewos, Emperor of Ethiopia 1540–1559</note> cited above, who calls himself the **Negus of Ethiopia** <note>original: ንጉሠ ዘኢትዮጵያ; *Negusch Zatjupi*</note>, or "King of Ethiopia."

ε) Indeed, they might have seemed to some to **lean toward Sabellianism** <note>original Greek: *sabellianizein*; Sabellianism was an ancient heresy teaching that the Father, Son, and Spirit are merely "modes" or "faces" of one person rather than three distinct persons</note> when certain people translated their words, **"Three Persons, one God"** <note>original Ethiopic: ሠለስቱ: ገጽ: እግዚአብሔር; *Selestu Gaz Igziabeher*</note>, as "Three faces, one Lord." But even though the word **Gaz** <note>original: ገጽ</note> literally denotes the face and countenance...

### TOPIC III: ON THE TRIUNE GOD

It is also the case that in the Ethiopian Liturgy, not only does the mention occur that GOD is **one essence** <note>original Ge'ez: አሐዱ፡ ሕላዌ፡; *Ahadu Hellawē*</note>, but furthermore it is also commemorated that in that unity there is nonetheless a **Trinity** <note>original Ge'ez: ሥላሴ፡; *Sellasē*</note>. σ) And, lest perhaps someone here might think that a Platonic, or Fanatical, or any other heterodox Trinity lies hidden, it should be further known that they worship and venerate that Trinity in the Godhead which consists of the **Father** <note>original Ge'ez: አብ፡; *Abb*</note>, the **Son** <note>original Ge'ez: ወልድ፡; *Waldi*</note>, and the **Holy Spirit** <note>original Ge'ez: መንፈስ፡ ቅዱስ፡; *Manfas Kyddus*</note>. This very thing they are accustomed to express in the following formula, which is most common among them: ζ) **IN THE NAME OF THE FATHER, AND OF THE SON, AND OF THE HOLY SPIRIT, THE ONE GOD** <note>original Ge'ez: በስመ፡ አብ፡ ወወልድ ወመንፈስ፡ ቅዱስ፡ አሐዱ፡ አምላክ፡; *Ba-sema Ab wa-Wald wa-Manfas Qeddus ahadu Amlak*</note>. Although here also, as we do not deny, if anyone...

If one were to inspect the books of the Ethiopians at first glance, they could easily be persuaded that the people here err in a sufficiently gross and dangerous error; specifically, that while avoiding the rocks of **SABELLIANISM** <note>A heresy which claims that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are not distinct persons, but merely different "modes" or "faces" of a single person</note>, they have fallen into the quicksands of the **TRITHEISTS** <note>The belief that the Trinity consists of three separate gods rather than one God in three persons</note>. This is because they sometimes seem to both admit and assert a triple <gloss>essence</gloss> በአምላክነ <gloss>in the Godhead</gloss> ህልውና <gloss>Essence</gloss>. But in truth, he who consults them for the sake of explaining this matter and desires a fuller explanation of their words, will easily discover that nothing less than this fundamental error can be imputed to their Church. (For to wish to determine beyond doubt what one or another person here might think or deny would be rash.) This is because with the word ህልውና <gloss>Hyluna</gloss> <note>Ge'ez: ህልውና; *Hellawē*, usually meaning essence, being, or existence</note> there comes not only that which the Greeks express by the name *ousia* <note>original Greek: οὐσίας; meaning substance or essence</note>, but also what the same people express by the name *hypostasis* <note>original Greek: ὑποστάσεως; meaning the distinct personhood of the Father, Son, or Spirit</note>. If this is true, as it is most true, n) it is quite clear that the Abyssi-

### TOPIC III: ON THE TRIUNE GOD

This shines forth from that quite frequent <term>address (Greek: *prosphonesis*)</term>: *O Holy Trinity, whose essence is united and unique.* And by this reasoning, we hope it is sufficiently clear that the Trinity of Persons in one and indivisible essence is believed by the Abyssinians <note>A historical name for the people of the Ethiopian Empire</note>. Now, it is our intention to briefly examine the distinction between those Persons, or the *relative consideration of God*. And as it pertains to the FATHER and the SON and their mutual relationship, they constantly teach that *the Son was indeed begotten of the Father, yet the Father is not for that reason prior in existence, nor even greater in power and virtue, but is entirely* <term>consubstantial (Greek: *homoousios*, meaning of the same substance)</term>, <term>co-eternal (Greek: *homoaionios*)</term>, and <term>equal in honor (Greek: *isotimios*)</term>; however, on account of the <term>mode of subsistence (Greek: *tropon hyparxeos*, or the way in which they exist)</term>, the Son is both distinct from the Father and was sent to us by the Father in the fullness of time, and not vice versa. This very point they suggest most clearly in the following words of their Liturgy, found in the cited works of Castell and Ludolf <note>Edmund Castell and Hiob Ludolf, 17th-century scholars who were pioneers in the study of Ethiopian languages</note>, in which they address Christ himself in this way:

> *There is no priority of existence of the Father before you simply because He begot you; or more clearly: The Father did not exist before you because He begot you.* And regarding the latter assertion, they say: <note>μ</note> *Just as the eternal Father is glorified in His essence.* <note>Translated from the original Ge'ez: "Kama 'Ab zalalam sebuh we'etu ba-hellawēhu"</note>

---

<note>μ</note> In the Ethiopian Liturgy, Chapter 2, page 171, near the end.

<summary>This page explores the Ethiopian Orthodox doctrine of the Trinity, specifically arguing that the Father and Son are co-eternal and equal in essence despite the Son being "begotten." It emphasizes that the Father does not precede the Son in time or power, citing liturgical texts as evidence.</summary>

However, they constantly deny, or at least do not assert, that he proceeds from the Father and the Son from eternity, and is <term>breathed forth (Latin: *spirari*)</term>; thus, for this reason, they seem to be like the modern Hellenists <note>A term used here for Greek Orthodox Christians</note>, v) as well as the Ruthenians <note>Christians of the Slavic regions, particularly those following Eastern rites</note>—

—thenians, * and those Fathers who lived in the Greek Church before them—although it is necessary that a completely notable distinction be established between these groups. ξ) Indeed, if you consider the hatred—I might say, an almost **Vatinian** hatred <note>A classical reference to the Roman politician Vatinius, used to describe an exceptionally fierce or proverbial loathing</note>—which has existed between the Greeks and the Abyssinians <note>Ethiopian Christians</note> ever since the celebrated *Council of Chalcedon* <note>Held in 451 AD; it caused a major schism between the Roman/Byzantine state church and the "Oriental Orthodox" churches like the Ethiopian Church</note>, you would hardly believe that they would collaborate in this error. ξ) Regarding this distinction, I am pleased by the judgment expressed by that **most precise theologian** <note>original Greek: τῷ ἀκριβεστάτῳ Θεολόγῳ</note>, JOHANN MEISNER, in his *Theological Consideration of the Johannine saying* <note>referring to the "Three Witnesses" passage in 1 John 5:7</note> *Chapter XV, 16, 1654*, held at Wittenberg, specifically in Part IV, Section I, § 22. There, he observes that the Greek Doctors <note>learned teachers of the church</note> ought to be divided into three classes regarding this controversy:

**I.** encompasses those who flourished from the time of Christ until Basil the Great, and thus until the middle of the 4th century, such as *Ignatius*, *Polycarp*, *Justin*, *Irenaeus*, etc.

**II.** reaches to the 10th century, or nearly the year 1000, in which the <term>procession of the Holy Spirit from the Son (the *Filioque* clause)</term> was only secretly doubted, without public censure.

**III.** extends to our own times, in which class he places—

See *their Acts and writings, published at Wittenberg in 1584 in folio, pages 200 and following.*

o) I note here in passing that the Ethiopians commonly use the word *seratse* <note>original: ሠረፀ፡</note>—specifically in the past tense—instead of the word *wegatse* <note>original: ወፀአ</note> ("he went out" or "proceeded"). The term they use properly means *sprouted forth* or *budded forth*. In the *Liturgy* printed at Rome, one does indeed read the phrase "and from the Son," but this is found in no manuscript codex, as **Ludolf** maintains *in his Commentary, page 353.*

π) In his *History*, Book III, chapter 5, number 39, and following.

...relates regarding his **Gregory** to that matter. <margin>ρ)</margin> Thus, however, that **GREGORY**, when asked about the procession of the Holy Spirit from the Father alone, *whether this was the same and consistent view of all the Doctors of Ethiopia? He affirmed it.* I persisted: *Why then did they deny that the Holy Spirit proceeds from the Son, since they asserted that He is equally the Spirit of the Father and the Son? He requested that I first explain to him what this meant*, TO PROCEED FROM THE FATHER; *then he would state the cause of the negation, for he used the words of Holy Scripture:* "Who goes forth from the Father and takes from the Son" <note>original Ge'ez: ዘይወፅእ፡ እምአብ፡ ወይነሥእ፡ እምወልድ፡</note>.

*Who goes forth from the Father and takes from the Son*, John 15:26 and 16:14, *and one should not probe further. For it is not permitted for us, in the most abstruse mysteries of the Holy Trinity, to argue through "consequences," as they call them* <note>term: consequences (consequentiae) — logical deductions or inferences drawn from a text. The Ethiopian position described here favors "Biblicism," sticking strictly to the wording of the Bible rather than scholastic logic.</note>, *but one must stick to the very words of Scripture. As if, indeed, men were the authors of legitimate logical deductions and not God Himself! For if this is true, as it is, then deductions even in the articles* <note>The text ends mid-word: "arti-", likely "articulis fidei" or "articles of faith."</note>

---

<margin>ρ) These words of **Gregory of Amhara** <note>Abba Gorgoryos (1595–1658), the Ethiopian scholar who collaborated with Hiob Ludolf and provided the West with its first deep look into Ethiopian culture</note> seem, by the nature of the matter, to agree with the words of **THEOPHYLACT** <note>A Byzantine Archbishop of Bulgaria in the 11th century, famous for his biblical commentaries</note>, which are read in Chapter 3 of John. For after he disputed much against the Latins <note>The Roman Catholic Church, specifically regarding the Filioque</note>, he adds: "Believe that the Spirit proceeds from the Father indeed, but is bestowed upon creation through the Son, and this shall be your rule of orthodoxy." <note>original Greek: πίστευε σὺ τὸ πνεῦμα ἐκ τοῦ πατρὸς μὲን προϊέναι, δι' υἱοῦ δὲ χορηγεῖσθαι τῇ κτίσει, καὶ οὗτος ἔσται σοι κανὼν ὀρθοδοξίας.</note> Though he [Theophylact] is better than the former [Gregory] in this: he does not deny the power and strength of logical deductions.</margin>

<summary>This page records a theological debate where the Ethiopian monk Gregory defends the view that the Holy Spirit proceeds from the Father alone by appealing to a literal reading of Scripture. He rejects the use of logical "consequences" to define the Trinity, a stance the author compares to the Byzantine theologian Theophylact.</summary>

# TOPIC III: ON THE TRIUNE GOD

...in the article on GOD, they can and ought to have a place, since truth is able to produce truth. <margin>σ)</margin> But enough has been said about the denied PROCESSION of the Holy Spirit from the FATHER AND THE SON occurring from eternity. However, this must not yet be overlooked: that while we stated above that the Abyssinians <note>term: Abyssinians — an archaic term for Ethiopians, derived from the Arabic 'Habesha.'</note> think excellently regarding the personality of the Holy Spirit, we should also confirm this with at least one domestic testimony. It exists in the *Organ of Mary*. <margin>τ)</margin>

*Lest anyone should infer from the things said above that the Holy Spirit is not a perfect and distinct Person, Christ therefore said to the Apostles: "I will send you another counselor" <note>original Latin: paracletum; a Greek-derived term for the Holy Spirit as an advocate or comforter</note>. From which we recognize that the Holy Spirit exists together with the Father and the Son, and also exists in His own subsistence, or person. Not that the Spirit is partly in the Son and partly in His own person, but He is one and the same, both existing in His own person and existing with the Father...*

---

<margin>σ)</margin> Without doubt, the good Abbot **Gregory** <note>Abba Gorgoryos, the Ethiopian monk and primary source for Hiob Ludolf’s research</note> did not diligently read Chapter 14 of John and his remaining epistles, where you may see a conclusion formed about GOD more than once. As in that passage: *He who does not have the Son, does not have the Father either*, where no one fails to see that a logical conclusion is present, flowing from that reasoning because Christ says: *I and the Father are one* <note>original Greek: Ἐγὼ καὶ πατὴρ ἕν ἐσμεν</note>.

<margin>τ)</margin> Reading for Monday. It is titled *Arganonä Dəngəl* <note>original Ge'ez: አርጋኖነ፡ ድንግል፡; literally "The Organ/Harp of the Virgin," a famous 15th-century collection of Ethiopian hymns and prayers</note> or the *Musical Organ of the Blessed Virgin*, which is itself a most overflowing work in praise of the Virgin.

# TOPIC III: ON THE TRIUNE GOD

[Ludolf] refers to his own Gregory <note>This is Abba Gorgoryos (1595–1658), an Ethiopian monk and scholar who collaborated with the German Orientalist Hiob Ludolf. His insights were crucial for European understanding of Ethiopian Christianity.</note> regarding this matter. ρ) This is how that **GREGORY** responded when he was questioned about the Holy Spirit proceeding from the Father only:

*He was asked whether this was the same and constant opinion of all the Doctors of Ethiopia? He affirmed it was.* I pressed him: *Why then did they deny that the Holy Spirit proceeds from the Son, seeing as they asserted he was equally the Spirit of the Father and the Son? He asked that I should first explain to him what it meant* TO PROCEED FROM THE FATHER; *then he would state the reason for the denial, saying that he used the very words of Holy Scripture:* <note>original Ethiopic: ዘይወጽእ፡ እምኀበ፡ አብ፡ ወይነሥእ፡ እምወልድ፡</note> **"Who proceeds from the Father and takes from the Son,"** as seen in John 15:26 and 16:14, *and he would search no further.*

He argued that we are not permitted, in the most hidden mysteries of the Most Holy Trinity, to argue by "consequences" <note>In this context, "consequences" refers to logical deductions or inferences drawn from scripture rather than the literal words themselves.</note> as they call them, but rather we must cling to the very words of Scripture. As if indeed men were the authors of legitimate logical consequences and not **GOD** himself! For if this is true, as it surely is, then consequences are also valid in matters of faith—

---

ρ) These words of *Gregory the Amhara* <note>Amhara refers to the ethnic and linguistic group in Ethiopia to which Gregory belonged.</note> seem, by the nature of the matter, to agree with the words of **THEOPHYLACT** <note>Theophylact of Ohrid, an 11th-century Byzantine archbishop and biblical exegete.</note> which are read in Chapter 3 of John. For after he has disputed much against the Latins <note>The "Latins" refers to the Roman Catholic Church, specifically concerning the "Filioque" clause—the Western addition to the Creed stating the Spirit proceeds from both Father "and the Son."</note>, he adds: <note>original Greek: Πίστευε σὺ τὸ πνεῦμα ἐκ τοῦ πατρὸς μὲν προϊέναι, δι' υἱοῦ δὲ χορηγεῖσθαι τῇ κτίσει, καὶ οὗτος ἔσται σοι κανὼν ὀρθοδοξίας.</note> **"Believe that the Spirit indeed proceeds from the Father, but is granted to the creation through the Son, and this shall be for you the rule of orthodoxy."** *Yet Gregory is better than the former in this regard: he does not reject the force and strength of logical consequences.*

<summary>This page records a theological debate between European scholars and the Ethiopian monk Abba Gorgoryos regarding the "Filioque" controversy. Gregory defends the Ethiopian view by insisting on the literal words of the Gospel of John over Western logical deductions.</summary>

# TOPIC III: ON THE TRIUNE GOD

[Fa]ther and the Son. And these things, as I believe, may be sufficient concerning this most noble subject of Theology. For the Abyssinians <note>A historical term for Ethiopians.</note> have not proceeded any further here, since their entire Theology is more Catechetical <note>designed for basic religious instruction</note> than *Acroamatic* <note>original: "Acroamatica"; referring to advanced, scholarly, or esoteric lectures</note>, as will be established more fully from the briefly described chapters of the Ethiopian faith which they are accustomed to teach to children and neophytes <note>new converts</note>. These were recorded in a manuscript left by *Gregory* <note>Abba Gregory, the Ethiopian monk and scholar who collaborated with Job Ludolf.</note> to *Job Ludolf* <note>A 17th-century German scholar regarded as the founder of Ethiopian studies in the West.</note> in his *History of Ethiopia, Book III, chapter 5, numbers 89 and following*, and they sound like this:

*“Which GOD do you worship? The Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit: three persons, but one deity. Of these three Persons, which is first, and which is last; which is greater, and which is smaller? None is first, and none is last; none is greater, and none is smaller; but they are equal in all things.”*

These same points are also presented in their Liturgy, which is called the ቅዳሴ፡ ዘእግዚእትነ፡ <note>original: "Qidase ze-Egzi'etne"; translated as the "Liturgy of Our Lady."</note>, printed at Rome, where on *page 171, column b*, they teach thus:

*“We believe in the Father who sends—for He is the Father in His own person; and we believe in the Son who was sent—for He is the Son in His own person; and we believe in the Holy Spirit who descended upon the Jordan and upon the Apostles—for He is the Holy Spirit in His own person. Three names, one GOD. Not, indeed, like Abraham, who is greater than Isaac; nor like Isaac, who is greater than Jacob. The matter does not stand so. The Father is not greater than His Son because He is the Father, nor is the Son greater than the Spirit”*

<summary>This page argues that Ethiopian theology is primarily practical and catechetical rather than academic, using a question-and-answer faith summary and the "Liturgy of Our Lady" to demonstrate their belief in the absolute equality of the Trinity.</summary>

## TOPIC IV
### ON
# CREATION

<margin>
α) This word descends from the root ፈጠረ: *Fatar* <note>original: "Fatar"</note>, which signifies bringing into existence a being that did not previously exist at all, and thus creating out of nothing. It is used in this sense throughout the Ethiopian Bible, such as in Psalm 33:6, Revelation 4:11, and elsewhere, although it is occasionally used for "mediated creation" <note>Creation using existing materials rather than from nothing</note>, as in Exodus 16:24, where a worm is said to have been ተፈጥረ: *Tafatra* <note>original: "Tafatra"</note>: formed, or generated, from rot.
</margin>

We have thus far seen the "internal works of God" <term>Opera Dei ad intra: theological term for the eternal relationships within the Trinity, such as the Son being "begotten" by the Father</term>, such as generating, breathing, being begotten, and being breathed. Now we must proceed to the "external works of God" <term>Opera Dei ad extra: actions by God directed toward the world outside of Himself</term>. Since creation is the first of all these, the Ethiopians <note>referred to here as "Habessyni" or Abyssinians</note>—who call it ፍጥረት: *Fytraot* <note>original: "Fytraot"</note> α)—teach as follows: That this entire universe, and everything within it, was created in time out of nothing by the word of divine omnipotence; and therefore, that nothing existed from eternity, or at least could ex-

->TOPIC IV: ON CREATION<-

...could have been, since God alone is from eternity and before the ages <note>original Ethiopic: ዘለዓለም (*zala'alam*), meaning "eternal"</note>, and <note>original Ethiopic: ዘአልቦቱ ወሳኔ (*za'albotu wasāne*), meaning "infinite"</note>. This very point we find clearly and elegantly confirmed in the Liturgy of Saint Dioscorus, where immediately at the beginning the following words are read: β)

> *From age to age, and unto the ages, the Lord is in His kingdom, the Lord in His Trinity, the Lord in His divinity. Before the dawn and the daybreak, before days and nights existed, before the Angels were created, the Lord was in His kingdom. Before the heavens were spread out, and before the face of the dry land appeared, and before the grasses sprouted, the Lord was in His kingdom. Before the sun, and the moon, and the stars, and before the circuits of the luminaries, the Lord was in His kingdom. Before the animals that creep, and before the birds that fly, and before the beasts of the sea existed, the Lord was in His kingdom.*

All of these things, although they primarily <note>original Greek: πρώτως (*prōtōs*)</note> prove the eternity of God, secondarily <note>original Greek: δευτέርως (*deuterōs*)</note> they also elegantly [illustrate] the work of Creation...

---

β) This Liturgy bears the following title: *The Consecration of the Oblation*, or *The Administration of the Lord’s Supper, of Saint Dioscorus, who was the Patriarch of Alexandria.* It was found in an ancient manuscript of Ethiopian Liturgies from the collection of the distinguished Mr. Edward Pococke <note>a famous 17th-century English scholar of Oriental languages</note>. This liturgy, *printed and translated into Latin*, was added to the *Ethiopian Grammar* of Hiob Ludolf by Johann Michael Wansleben of Erfurt <note>Wansleben was a traveler and scholar who collaborated with Ludolf, the father of Ethiopian studies in Europe</note>.

### TOPIC IV: ON CREATION ###

show clearly. More about *Creation*, as far as we know, they do not teach. For just as their Philosophy—which they call <term>ርእስ (*r’əs*)<note>literally "head" or "principle," here used to refer to the fundamental principles of knowledge</note></term>—is still meager and somewhat unrefined, γ) so they avoid many questions that would illustrate this article on *Creation*. Yet, it seems this further point can be brought in their favor: that they nonetheless believe the whole Holy Trinity to be the Author of that *Creation*, or, to speak with the Philosophers, the principal efficient cause. I believe this is clear even from that saying of theirs which the previously praised <term>Castell<note>Edmund Castell (1606–1685), an English scholar who compiled a massive dictionary of seven languages</note></term> δ) cites: *The Father made, the Son joins together, and the Holy Spirit orders all things, namely, in creating.* By which words, as much as they show a diversity of actions, so too a diversity...

<sig>C 3</sig>
<meta>which-</meta>

---

γ) Their lack of refinement in Philosophy, and in the mathematical sciences specifically, is clear from the fact that they consider the idea that the earth is round and suspended freely in the middle of the air to be a notable fable; whence they must also necessarily deny that there are <term>Antipodes<note>people living on the opposite side of the globe</note></term>. In this opinion several *Fathers* <note>early Christian theologians</note> also shared, such as *Lactantius* in *Divine Institutes, book III, chapter 23*, and *Augustine* in *The City of God, chapter 9*, etc. They persuade themselves that the sun goes out and returns through a certain window when it sets or rises; however, they are not concerned with what path that same sun takes under the earth, as <term>Ludolf<note>Hiob Ludolf (1624–1704), a German scholar who is considered the founder of Ethiopian studies in Europe</note></term> reports in *History of Ethiopia, book IV, chapter 2, numbers 31 and following*.

<note>ε) To these points, I am pleased to add one further piece of evidence taken from the *Organ of Mary* <note>The *Organon Maryam* (Organ of Praise), a famous 15th-century collection of Ethiopic hymns to the Virgin Mary organized by days of the week</note>, specifically from the Monday Lesson, which begins as follows: *In the Name of GOD Almighty, who is three without Division, and one by union, equal in Essence, and equal in Divinity.* If this is so—as is most true—then the power of creating also belongs to the Son and the Holy Spirit, since "power" in the context of GOD is the divine essence itself, with which—as the Philosophers say—it is identified.</note>

also suggest [the existence of] persons really distinct from one another. But if indeed—though we do not expect it—they were to affirm regarding the Father alone that he <term>makes<note>original Ge'ez: ይግበር (*yigber*)</note></term> or <term>creates<note>original Ge'ez: ፈጠረ (*fatara*)</note></term>, while claiming that the Son only joins together those things produced from nothing by the Father, and that the Holy Spirit only organizes the things already joined, and thereby denied that the work of creation is to be ascribed absolutely equally to all three persons of the Godhead, they would be completely in error. However, the unity of essence—which was clearly asserted just a moment ago regarding the Persons in the Trinity—easily proves them to be free from such an error. ε)

<header>TOPIC V: ON THE ANGELS</header>

[-akt] <note>Continuing the term "Malakt" or "Messengers" from the previous page</note> α), or Messengers, the **Abyssinians** <note>A historical term for Ethiopians</note> name, teach, and believe them to be <term>Manfasawit<note>Ethiopic: መንፈሳዊት (*manfasāwit*)</note></term>, or spiritual beings. Among these, they believe those who persevered in the holiness created within them assist the pious and the faithful above all others; they hear their prayers and count their sighs. For this reason, the Abyssinians are also accustomed to invoke them, undoubtedly under the plausible pretext of seeking their protection.

The often-praised [Hiob] **LUDOLF** β) <note>Hiob Ludolf (1624–1704) was a preeminent German orientalist and the founder of Ethiopian studies in the West</note> judges that the reason for this practice is derived from no other source than what they learned from the **JEWS**: namely, that Angels are set over all things and individuals, without even the smallest being excepted; and likewise, that a specific guardian Angel is assigned to every mortal. The ancient pagans used to call this figure a *Tutelar Genius* <note>A protective spirit assigned to a person from birth in Roman religion</note>, and the Jews are accustomed to call it the <term>Good Inclination<note>Hebrew: יצר הטוב (*Yetzer ha-Tov*). While usually referring to an inner moral impulse, Ludolf identifies it here with the concept of a guardian spirit.</note></term>. Consequently, what began as a civil respect eventually [turned] into a clearly religious [veneration]...

α) This word <note>Malakt</note> is related to the Hebrew <note>מלאך (*mal'akh*)</note> and the Samaritan <note>ࠌࠋࠠࠀࠊ (*mal'akh*)</note>, as well as the Syriac <note>ܡܠܐܟܐ (*mal'akhā*)</note> and the Chaldean <note>מלאכא (*mal'akhā*)</note>. However, we must seek the derivation of all these from the Arabic <term>Alaka<note>أَلَكَ, meaning "to send" or "to dispatch"</note></term>, which signifies to send or to delegate. From this, the Arabic word <term>Malaka<note>مَلأَك (*malak*)</note></term>, which likewise signifies an Angel, is also derived.

...turned, they began to attribute almost divine honor to them, although I do not deny that signs of this practice are rarer among our Abyssinians <note>A historical term for Ethiopians</note>. Yet, lest I seem to have attributed anything to them without reason, it will suffice to bring forward this single point to this matter: for example, since they pursue Saint **MICHAEL** the Archangel with honor above all others and hold him in high esteem, they also desired that **12** annual festivals be sacred to him, each of which they are accustomed to celebrate with sufficient solemnity on the **12th** day of every month. γ) Furthermore, since they know that the number of good Angels is by far the greatest, they are commonly accustomed to distinguish that entire choir of theirs into **9** orders, after the manner of **DIONYSIUS THE AREOPAGITE**, δ) <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite was an influential 5th-6th century theologian whose work "The Celestial Hierarchy" defined the traditional nine-fold ranking of angels in Christian thought</note> by which—

---

γ) <margin>The very first festival of all is celebrated, as it is also the most famous of all, on the 12th day of the month of <term>Mäskäräm<note>original Ethiopic: መስከረም</note></term>, or September, which for us is September 9th; this is because they begin their year, as all Eastern peoples do, from September—perhaps because the world was created in that month, a matter about which there will perhaps be a place to discuss specifically elsewhere.</margin>

δ) <margin>For he, in *Book I, On the Celestial Hierarchy, Chapter VI*, also distinguishes the Angels into **9** Orders, and these again into three Hierarchies: *the highest, the middle, and the lowest*, or <note>original Greek: ἐπαρχίαν (eparchian)</note> supreme rule, <note>original Greek: μεσαρχίαν (mesarchian)</note> middle rule, and <note>original Greek: ὑπαρχίαν (hyparchian)</note> subordinate rule, and in each he again places 3 other Orders.</margin>

<header>TOPIC V: ON THE ANGELS</header>

The names of these orders they derive from the titles and descriptors used for them in the Holy Scriptures, so that the first class is made up of <gloss>Archangels <note>original Ethiopic: ሊቃናት፡ ወላእክታት (Likänat wä-la'iktat)</note></gloss>; the second, <gloss>Lords <note>original Ethiopic: አጋእዝት (Ag'azt); original Greek: κυριότητες</note></gloss> or **Dominions**; the third, <gloss>Principalities <note>original Ethiopic: ስልጣናት (Siltanat); original Latin: Magistratus; original Greek: Ἀρχαὶ</note></gloss>; the fourth, <gloss>Thrones <note>original Ethiopic: ወናብርት (Wänabirt)</note></gloss>; the fifth, <gloss>Powers <note>original Ethiopic: ኀይላት (Haylat); original Greek: ἐξουσίαι</note></gloss>; the sixth, <gloss>Angels <note>original Ethiopic: ወላእክታት (Wäla'iktat)</note></gloss> simply so-called, who also go by the name <gloss>Spirits <note>original Ethiopic: ወናፍስት (Wä-nafist)</note></gloss> among them; the seventh, <gloss>Princes <note>original Ethiopic: ወኩንናት (Wäkünnat)</note></gloss>; the eighth, <gloss>Seraphim <note>original Ethiopic: ሱራፌል (Surafel)</note></gloss>; and finally the ninth, <gloss>Cherubim <note>original Ethiopic: ኪሩቤል (Kirubel)</note></gloss>. And so much for the **good Angels**. Now, as for the **bad Angels**, they call them sometimes <gloss>Diabolical Spirits <note>original Ethiopic: ወናፍስት፡ መስተይም (Manfasot masteim)</note></gloss>, sometimes by the name <gloss>Satan <note>original Ethiopic: ስይጣን (Sjytan)</note></gloss>, ε) and sometimes by the name **Devil**.
<sig>C 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: Devil-</meta>

On these matters, compare also **PETER LOMBARD** <note>A 12th-century theologian whose "Sentences" became the standard textbook of medieval theology</note> in *Book IV, Sentences, Distinction 10.* However, the Abyssinians <note>Ethiopians</note> differ from the Jews, who clearly imagine ten orders; you may see these in the works of **JOHN CAMERON** <note>A 17th-century Scottish theologian</note> in his *Lectures on Matthew 18*, and **HOORNBEEK** <note>Johannes Hoornbeek, a Dutch theologian known for his polemics</note> in his *Against the Jews, page 133.*

As for those they call **Devils** <note>original: *Dijabylos*</note> 5), they correctly maintain that these were once holy and whole, existing alongside the others. At that time—as some of the **Ethiopians** <note>original: *Habessynorum*</note> claim—they formed a tenth order of Angels, whose head was <gloss>**Satanäel** <note>original Ethiopic: ሳትናኤል፡</note></gloss>. They say, however, that he and his companions rebelled against God, and that the blessed [humans] will one day succeed into his place; they claim this is the cause of the most hostile hatred of these **evil spirits** <note>original: *cacodaemonum*</note> toward humanity. 

Yet truly, what is true or false in this assertion can easily be determined with Holy Scripture as a guide. Scripture indeed asserts in *Luke 20:36* that we shall one day be <gloss>like angels <note>original Greek: ἰσαγγέλους (isangelous)</note></gloss>, but the idea that we are to fill the place of a tenth order of good Angels is said <gloss>without the support of Scripture <note>original Greek: ἄተር γραφῆς (ater graphēs)</note></gloss>. Nevertheless, these things might be more easily excused in the Ethiopians, provided they ceased being patrons of <gloss>angel worship <note>original Greek: ἀγγελοላτρίας (angelolatrias)</note></gloss>, which is the most important point of concern here. 

They recoil from Satan to such an extent that when the **Jesuit Fathers** <note>original: *Patres societatis*; referring to the Society of Jesus</note> produced a stage play in the year 1626 and represented devils on the theater stage, everyone was so terrified—

5) This name is Greek in origin and descends from the verb <gloss>to strike through <note>original Greek: διαβάλλω (diaballō)</note></gloss>, which properly means *to pierce through*, and also *to throw across*; metaphorically, however, it means *to slander* or *to accuse*. Hence <gloss>Devil <note>original Greek: Διάβολος (Diabolos)</note></gloss> signifies a **slanderer and accuser.**

However, the Ethiopians seem to me a bit too bold <note>original: *audaculi*</note> here in determining the creation of the first man, when they imagine that God mixed the four elements and formed the first man from their mixture. Concerning this matter, the Author of the *Organ* <note>original: *Organi*; likely referring to the *Arganonä Maryam* (Harp of Mary), a major work of Ethiopian Marian and creation theology</note>, *Lecture I, otherwise 2,* hands down the following, saying: *“God performed a miracle when He created our father Adam and formed him from four elements; He mixed the elements, yet in such a way that they would not disagree with one another. He mixed the first with the second, and the third with the fourth, the moist with the dry and the hot with the cold, the invisible with the visible, the tangible with the intangible.”* (As if these things could truly be mixed, which is a property only of matter.) *“He made two from the tangible and two from the intangible; He made three from the dry and one from the moist; He made three from the cold and one from the hot. The Architect knew where the inner sanctuary was to be established, and He founded the corners of the house upon the four elements. And since He knew that a clay vessel could not move itself nor speak without the mixture of the spirit proceeding from Him, He therefore breathed into his face and made him rational and self-moving.”* On this occasion, let us note at the same time that the **Abyssinians** assert at least two essential parts of man, and they have no dealings with the **Fanatics** (see the dissertation by the late <term>HENRICUS WIDEBURGIUS</term> <note>Heinrich Wideburg (1641–1692), a German Lutheran theologian who wrote on the "trichotomy" of body, soul, and spirit</note>, *On the Three Parts of Man*), who...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Topic VI (On Humanity), specifically distinguishing the Ethiopian (Abyssinian) view of the human soul and body from other philosophical and heretical systems. <page-num>46</page-num>
<header>TOPIC VI: ON HUMANITY</header>
<meta>catchword: From</meta>

...imagine three <note>referring back to the previous page's discussion on those who posit a three-part division of man: body, soul, and spirit</note>, nor do they agree with the *Platonists* ε) or *Gnostics* ϛ), who regarded the body not as an essential, constitutive part of a human being, but rather as a prison for the soul. And although they <note>the Ethiopians</note> sometimes make mention of "two souls" in their writings, it will always be clear from the entire context that they do not mean two essential and constitutive parts of man; rather, they denote at least one as essential <note>the immortal soul</note>, while by the other they signify the "vital spirits" <note>the biological life-force or breath</note>. Let us hear, for example, the *Author of the Organ* <note>original: *Auctorem Organi*; referring to the *Arganonä Maryam*</note>, in the place cited, saying:

> *Regarding what he said: "Thou shalt not kill the soul," he spoke concerning the sensitive soul. There are two souls in man: one is the Spirit of life, which proceeded from the mouth of GOD; it is not counted among the elements, nor does it ever die. The other, however, is the blood of the body, or the sensitive and vegetative soul, which has its origin from the elements, and that one is mortal.*

---

ε) See PLATO in the *Alcibiades*; PLOTINUS, *Enneads Book I, Chapter 6*; MARSILIO FICINO in his *Commentary on Plato's Symposium, Speech VI*; with whom the Socinians <note>a rationalist Christian sect</note> collude; see SOCINUS on *The First Man, Chapter 1, page 28*, and others.

ϛ) See concerning these IRENAEUS *Book I, Chapter 1, Section 10*; and likewise concerning the Valentinians <note>a Gnostic sect</note>, TERTULLIAN in *Against the Valentinians, Chapter 29*. VOSSIUS will provide more names reviewed in *On Idolatry, Book III, Chapter 40*.

# TOPIC VI: ON HUMANITY

### <note>catchword: Topic</note>

From these points, it is fully understood what their opinion is in this matter. Now, indeed, we should treat the **IMAGE OF GOD**, as that distinguished perfection created together with the first man. But truly, as far as I know from their writings, their knowledge on this point is so sterile that they know how to report little or nothing about this doctrine, or they do not seem to be particularly concerned with inquiring about it. For besides those few words of Saint **DIOSCORUS** <note>likely referring to Dioscorus I of Alexandria, a foundational figure in the Miaphysite theology followed by the Ethiopian Church</note>, which are extant in the *Liturgy*, or the offering of the *Holy Supper* <note>original: *S. Coenae*; referring to the Eucharist</note> from the beginning, in the place cited above, and they run as follows:

->Before the beginning, He created Adam in His own<-
->image and likeness; and before he transgressed<-
->the commandment, the Lord was in His kingdom.<-
<note>Translated from the original Ge'ez: እመቅደሙ፡ ይፈጥሮ፡ ለአዳም፡ በዘዚአሁ፡ አርአያ፡ ወአምሳል፡ ወእመቅመ፡ ይዐሉ፡ ትእዛዘ፡ ሀሎ፡ እግዚእ፡ በመንግሥቱ።</note>

Before man was created—namely **Adam**, who is so named *by excellence* <note>original Greek: *κατ' ἐξοχὴν* (kat' exochēn); meaning "pre-eminently" or "the man par excellence"</note>—in His image and likeness, and before he transgressed the commandment, the Lord was in His kingdom. No one would easily provide more on that matter. But since many books of the Abyssinians <note>an older term for Ethiopians</note>, many things, and many other dogmas remain hidden from us to this day, it will be necessary for us to be content with these for the present, intending to provide more when more of their writings have reached us. Moreover, in the doctrine of this article <note>the theological "article" or point concerning the Image of God</note>, we assert all the more confidently that most of the Ethiopians agree with us, as much as can be gathered from the things said above.

# TOPIC VII: ON SIN

### TOPIC VII
## ON SIN

Thus far we have dealt with man, and indeed in the *state of nature, a)* or, as some say, his *original state*; now we must proceed to the other state, which is that of *wrath and a fallen nature.* Into this state we have come through the fall and transgression of our first parents. In this way the Abyssinians <note>an older term for Ethiopians</note> establish, confess, and teach that their sin [of the first parents] is propagated to us through nature, and continues to be propagated today. Therefore they name it *Chatiat Abarue* <note>original Ge'ez: ኃጢአት፡ አበው (ḥāṭīʾat ʾabaw)</note>, or *Waras Chatiat* <note>original Ge'ez: ወራሰ፡ ኃጢአት (warāsa ḥāṭīʾat)</note>, which means *the Sin of the Fathers*, or, if you prefer, *Paternal Sin*. They call it this not only because it draws its origin from our fathers, by which very fact [it is called] *original sin*... <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "nis," which likely completes the word "originalis."</note>

---

<margin>a) Theologians commonly distinguish three states of man: the first they express with the Hebrew word *Adam* <note>אדם; generally meaning "man" or "humanity," often associated with the state of creation</note>, the second with the word *Enosh* <note>אנוሽ; implying "frail or mortal man," associated with the fall</note>, and the third with the word *Ish* <note>איש; meaning "individual man" or "husband"</note>. Others identify at least two states, others four, but in truth all these agree, as shown by the Blessed **Balthasar Meisner** <note>a prominent Lutheran theologian (1587–1626) known for his work on "Sacred Anthropology"</note> in his *Sacred Anthropology*, Decade I, Disputation I, Question I, sections 5, 6, and 7.</margin>

<margin>β) This name [for sin] descends from the root *chat'a* <note>original Ge'ez: ኀጥአ</note>, meaning *to lack* or *not to have*; from which *chati'a* <note>ኃጢአ</note> designates one who is *lacking, destitute, or unworthy*, while *chate'* <note>ኃጥእ</note> designates the *impious and sinful*; see **Job Ludolf's** *Ethiopian Lexicon*, page 224 <note>Job Ludolf (1624–1704) was a German scholar whose work founded the field of Ethiopian studies in Europe</note>.</margin>

[origi]nal originating [sin], but they also suggest that it is, as it were, the root and fountainhead of all other actual sins. In this way, they point their finger, so to speak, toward *Original Sin as it is received* <note>Latin: *Originis Originatum*; a technical term used by medieval theologians to distinguish the sin we inherit from the "originating" act committed by Adam</note>, as the Scholastics <note>medieval university theologians who used logic to explain Christian doctrine</note> like to say. γ) All of these points sufficiently show that the *Abyssinians* <note>Ethiopians</note> have no doubt regarding the fact *that lawlessness exists* <note>original Greek: τῷ, ὅτι ἐστὶν ἡ ἀνομία (tō, hoti estin hē anomia)</note>. But if anyone were to inquire further: what do they assert and teach regarding the question of *what it is* <note>original Greek: τῷ, τί ἐστι (tō, ti esti)</note> and the specific form of sin? At that point, they might seem to lack a fully sufficient knowledge of the matter, since in their own language they usually express it only through negative terms, as we have seen. However, even here you might easily expect better from the Abyssinians, if only you consider that terms which are negative in their etymology <note>word-origin</note> usually imply something positive in actual reality and by virtue of their *inherent power* <note>original Greek: δυνάμεως (dynameōs)</note>. δ) This very thing, by an exam[ple]—

---

γ) Compare **LOMBARD** <note>Peter Lombard, whose *Sentences* was the standard medieval textbook</note> *Book II, Sentences, Distinction 30*; **SCOTUS** <note>John Duns Scotus</note> *on Book IV, Distinction 14, Question 1*; **DURANDUS** <note>Durandus of Saint-Pourçain</note> *on Distinction 30, Question 1*; Thomas [Aquinas], [William of] Ockham, and others. The Blessed Balthasar *Meisner* <note>a Lutheran theologian (1587–1626)</note> in his *Anthropology, Dissertation V, Question 2, § 9*, cites eight other words indicating "Sin" among the Scholastics, but those belong more properly to the Scriptures as a whole.

δ) I can easily demonstrate this very point with an example taken from this same language and root.
