[exam]ple of the divine Attributes can be demonstrated more than sufficiently, I believe. ε) And for that reason, the Ethiopians should not be thought to regard *Sin* as a mere privation <note>Latin: *privatio*; the absence of a good that should be present</note>, or as a "Non-Entity" <note>Latin: *Non-Ente*; something that has no real existence</note>, since they know well—and they know it having been taught by their own experience—that *Sin* is remarkably active in man, in that it frequently exercises its acts within us: sometimes through *tempting* <note>original Greek: πειράζειν (peirazein)</note>, sometimes through *drawing away* <note>original Greek: ἐξέλκειν (exelkein)</note>, sometimes through *enticing* <note>original Greek: δελεάζειν (deleazein)</note>, sometimes through *conceiving* <note>original Greek: συλλαμβάνειν (syllambanein)</note>, and sometimes even plainly through *bringing forth* <note>original Greek: τίκτειν (tiktein)</note>, as *St. James* speaks in *chapter 1, verses 14 and 15*. And from this, *Actual Sin* <note>sin resulting from specific actions, rather than the state of original sin</note> must also be revisited, which among the Abyssinians <note>Ethiopians</note> goes by the name *abasa* <note>Ethiopic: አበሳ; typically meaning transgression, crime, or offense</note>. For example, when in the original sources of *Psalm 38:8*, we read: *there is no soundness in my flesh* <note>original Hebrew: אֵין־מְתֹם בִּבְשָׂרִי (ein metom bibśari)</note>, the Ethiopian translator rendered it as: *I lacked health* or *health was wanting to me* <note>original Ethiopic: ኀጥአኩ: ፍወለ: (ḫat’aku fĕwwale)</note>. In this way, both the Hebrew and the Abyssinian used a purely negative word; but who would rashly say that a "lack of health"—or a disease—does not also actually posit a contrary condition, such as disordered movement of the blood, pains, and so on, beyond the mere negation of health? And let the same judgment apply to the rest.

ε) For in this article of faith, if you consider the very center of all divine attributes—the positive as well as the negative, or more correctly, the *unactuated* <note>original Greek: ἀνενεργητῶν (anenergētōn); a technical term for attributes that describe what God is not, rather than what He does</note> attributes—which is *Independence* <note>Latin: *Independentia*; the quality of God existing of Himself without relying on any cause</note>, you will see that a term which is grammatically negative is present, and yet when considered metaphysically, it is not [mo-] <note>continued on next page as "most" or "more"</note>

<summary>The author argues that the Ethiopian church understands sin as a powerful, active force in human life, rather than just a "lack of good." that is, [it] comes from *faults, guilts, and offenses.* 5) Furthermore, these Sins are to be distinguished into *Mortal* <note>sins that sever one's relationship with God</note> and *Venial* <note>lesser sins that do not destroy the life of grace</note>, and among the former, the *Sin specifically against the Son of Man*, and likewise the *Sin against the Holy Spirit*, are to be numbered.

---

[not] only to contain immortality, infinity, indivisibility, immateriality, and other such things, but that divine eternity, omnipotence, omniscience, omnipresence, holiness, justice, goodness, majesty, life, love, and—to embrace those infinite perfections in GOD in a single word—the Divinity, or divine <term>Essence</term> <note>original Greek: οὐσία (ousia); the fundamental nature of what something is</note>, are involved in this one [concept] and necessarily established.

What more positive quality could you assign to these latter attributes? Since, once these are established, you establish the very **Essence** of GOD, but when they are removed, you also remove it. For take away the single quality of **Independence** <note>original Latin: *Independentia*</note> in GOD—would any attribute of either *infinity*, or *immortality*, or *divine power, wisdom, majesty*, etc., remain? Not at all! For GOD is *immortal* for this reason: because He does not depend [on anything else] for His being; He is *infinite* for this reason: because He allowed no limits to be placed on His essence. Why is this? Because He is *independent*; and so it is for the rest. From this one example we can learn that negative terms are to be measured not by the grammatical ending of the words alone, but by their innermost nature and constitution, because very often—though not always—they simultaneously imply something positive.

5) This noun is Plural, for in the singular it is said as <term>*abasa*</term> <note>original Ethiopic: አበሳ; meaning a crime, sin, or transgression</note>; just as it is read in *Psalm 31, 5.* <note>In many English translations, this corresponds to Psalm 32:5: "I acknowledged my sin unto thee..."</note>

<summary>This page argues that theological terms with negative prefixes (like "immortality" or "independence") actually describe the highest positive realities of God's essence. This logic is applied to the Ethiopic word for sin, "abasa," explaining that while it may appear as a "lack," it represents an active and serious transgression.</summary>

## TOPIC VIII
# ON THE LAW

All sins are known through the *Law*. Just as the Law is the will of a superior that binds an inferior to its observance, so too do the Ethiopians—who freely concede that they are inferiors subject to a certain superior, namely እግዚአብሔር <term>original Ge'ez: Egziabher; literally "Lord of the Land," the standard word for God</term>, *GOD, the Best and Greatest*—recognize that He is provoked to anger and vengeance in many ways by sins. Therefore, what is more certain than that they should confess that God, the author of that Law, has communicated it to them? Indeed, they confess this as often as they express the word ሕግ <term>original Ge'ez: Chig (transcribed here as "Chagi"); Law</term>, *a)* which denotes "the Law," or show themselves [to follow] the Law in their deeds and actions...

<note>The following text appears to be a descriptive honorific for a person or for God, possibly referencing the Emperor Claudius or a legal authority</note>
...they are accustomed to call him the "Fountain of the springing water of wisdom," and also አቡኑ <term>original Ge'ez: Abunu; "Our Father"</term>: that is, *their Father*. Both of these names of honor can be read in the *Confession of Claudius* <note>Galawdewos (reigned 1540–1559), an Ethiopian Emperor who wrote a famous defense of the Ethiopian Orthodox faith against Portuguese Jesuit influence</note>.

*a)* This name descends from the root ሕግ <term>original Ge'ez: Ch-G-G; to decree or establish</term>: which is the same as *he established, sanctioned, or brought the law*. —[we] might augur that no other method of numbering is familiar even to the **Abyssinians** <note>Ethiopian Christians</note> themselves than the one which we use, as it is also most consistent of all with Holy Scripture and the original text. θ) Truly, this **Sinaitic Law** is a perfect [rule] of actions

θ) For, a) in **Exodus 20**, **GOD** himself, establishing a distinctive sign, placed the letter *Samekh* <note>original: ס; the Hebrew letter S</note>, which signifies *setumah* <note>original: סתומה; a "closed" paragraph break in the Hebrew Bible used to indicate a minor division in the text</note>. He placed it not between the first and second commandment, according to the Calvinists' <note>Followers of John Calvin; the Reformed tradition</note> method of numbering, but between the ninth and tenth, according to our opinion. Moreover, the division made by the Holy Spirit must not be "reformed"—or I should rather say, "deformed."

But it might perhaps seem that the word *chamad* <note>original: חמד; "to covet"</note>, which is read in both the ninth and tenth commandments according to our numbering, opposes our opinion. We respond, however, that when two different things are expressed through one word, they do not imply the same thing. For who would conclude—from seeing the word *amar* <note>original: אמר; "to say" or "to speak"</note> in **Genesis 3** used regarding **GOD**, and the woman, and the serpent—that it indicates one and the same manner of speaking? Thus here too, "concupiscence" <note>intense longing or desire, often used in a theological sense for the inclination toward sin</note> occurs in both, but it is of one kind in the ninth commandment and another in the tenth. 

Wherefore the Holy Spirit in **Deuteronomy 5:18**, intending to suggest the distinction between these commandments himself, uses the word *chamad* in the ninth, but *avah* <note>original: אוה; another Hebrew term for desire or longing</note> in the tenth; by this means, He has already removed that doubt. I say nothing of other matters; here I only appeal to the consensus of the Jews and many of the [Church] Fathers. More information will be provided by **the blessed CRAMER** in his *Treatise on the Distinction of Catalogues*, **the blessed HEINRICH ECKHARD** in *A Bundle of Theological Controversies with the Calvinists*, Topic 11, Question 1, page 260, following, and **DANNHAUER**— <note>The author is citing prominent Lutheran theologians: Balthasar Cramer, Heinrich Eckhard (1580–1624), and Johann Conrad Dannhauer (1603–1666)</note>

# TOPIC VIII: ON THE LAW

"If you receive circumcision, Christ will be of no benefit to you," <note>Galatians 5:2; original Greek: ἐὰν περιτέμνησθε, χριστὸς ὑμᾶς οὐδὲν ὠφελήσει</note> and do they not, in this way, seem to violate <term>Christian liberty</term> <note>The theological concept that Christians are freed from the ritual requirements of the Old Testament Law</note>? Since, by the judgment of the same Apostle in *1 Corinthians 10:25, 27*, it is permitted for Christians to eat "anything sold in the meat market" <note>original Greek: πᾶν, τὸ ἐν μακέλλω πωλούμενον</note> and "anything set before you" <note>original Greek: καὶ πᾶν τὸ παρατιθέμενον</note>. Just as these arguments are certainly beyond any exception, so they could, on more than one count, accuse the *Abyssinians* <note>A historical name for the people of Ethiopia</note> and could disapprove of and condemn those recounted Jewish rites. 

But, lest we perhaps give any weight here to the prejudice of our emotions—and specifically to rash judgment—we believe it will be worth the effort if we bring forward the *Ethiopians* themselves, speaking on this matter and pleading their own opinion and cause. Indeed, we find all these things nowhere more accurately explained than in the above-mentioned *Confession of Claudius* <note>An important 1540 defense of the Ethiopian Orthodox faith written by Emperor Galawdewos (Claudius) to address Portuguese criticisms</note>, which, as we have already observed above, was fashioned from no other motive of wisdom than to remove from themselves the suspicion of <term>Judaism</term> <note>In this context, the practice of following Jewish law while claiming to be Christian</note> with which they were being charged. 

Indeed, after a few introductory remarks, that document states: *“But as for our celebration of the ancient day of the Sabbath, we certainly do not celebrate it like the Jews who crucified Christ, saying, ‘His blood be upon us and upon our children.’ For those Jews neither draw water, nor light a fire, nor cook a dish, nor bake bread, nor move from house to house..."*

In truth, circumcision exists among us according to the custom of the region, just like the scarring of the face in Ethiopia and Nubia, and like the piercing of the ear among the Indians. ### T o p i c  I X
# ON THE GOSPEL

Just as the *Law* is a tutor leading us to Christ (*Galatians 3:24*), so too, since neither we nor our ancestors were capable of fulfilling the Law or bearing its yoke—and thus could conceive of no hope of obtaining salvation through the Law—we have fled with a swift pace to Christ as our sanctuary. To find Him, however, it is necessary that we listen closely to His voice, which is the *Gospel*, "**the word of eternal life**" <note>original Greek: τὸ ῥῆμα ζωῆς αἰωνίȣ; John 6:68</note>. Since the **Abyssinians** <note>The historical European term for Ethiopians</note> also recognized this, from the time of their first conversion to Christ, they have clung solely to the *Gospel* as a proclamation of Grace founded upon the merit of **JESUS CHRIST**, which

### TOPIC IX: CONCERNING THE GOSPEL

...and this was the reason why all Christians have been accustomed to call themselves to this very day the <term>People of the Gospel</term> <note>original Ge'ez: ሕዝብ፡ ወንጌላውያን፡ (*Hyzybba Wanygelawjgany*)</note>, a) or the *Evangelical People*—that is, *Christians* who uniquely follow the doctrine of the Gospel. Nor is this out of place, since it befits Christians to follow the doctrine of *Moses* and the *Prophets*, and of *CHRIST* and the *Apostles*. Indeed, what else is the entirety of Holy Scripture but a letter from the most high GOD, written in the purple blood of our greatest Savior? So it is that no book of the Bible can be found where Christ is not revealed; if not <term>bodily</term> <note>original Greek: σωματικῶς; referring to a physical or literal presence</note>, then at least <term>typically and relationally</term> <note>original Greek: τυπικῶς καὶ σχετικῶς; referring to "types" or symbols that point toward Christ</note>. Even the Sainted Apostle PETER, <term>being carried along by the Holy Spirit</term> <note>original Greek: Φερόμενος ὑπὸ τῦ πνεύματος ἁγίου</note>, could not speak of the matter in any other way than to say that <term>all the prophets</term> <note>original Greek: πάντες οἱ προφῆται</note> bear witness to our Savior, as seen in *Acts 10:43*. Furthermore, it is not uncommon for the Abyssinians <note>A historical name for Ethiopians</note> also to distinguish the Gospel by the name—
<meta>catchword: name</meta>

***

a) This name <term>people</term> <note>original Ge'ez: ሕዝብ፡ (*Hizb*)</note> designates a *people, nation, tribe, or even a family*. For example, it is used when the *people of Israel* are mentioned in *Matthew 8:28* <note>While Matthew 8:28 usually refers to the region of the Gergesenes, the author likely cites a specific Ethiopic manuscript reading</note>. In other places, it denotes a sect of heretics or a school of philosophers, as in *Acts 5:17* and *15:5*, but the former meaning is more common. Moreover, that Christians are identified by the name <term>People of the Gospel</term> <note>original Ge'ez: ሕዝብ፡ ወንጌላውያን፡</note> is very clearly evident from the *Ethiopian Liturgy*, page 162, chapter 2, and other passages.

### TOPIC IX: CONCERNING THE GOSPEL

...name of a <term>spring of bubbling water</term> <note>original Ge'ez: ነቅዐ፡ ማይ፡ ዘይፈልፍል፡ (*Nakya May Zayfalfal*)</note> β), or by the name <term>Gospel</term> <note>original Ge'ez: ወንጌል፡ (*Wanygel*)</note> γ). This is truly a joyful message for sinners who believe in Christ, even though to the fleshly person, nothing worthy of joy or desire seems to be found in it. For it cannot be denied that for the most part, it deals with *hunger, poverty, spit, insults, slaughter, suffering, wounds, the torture of the cross*, and finally the most cruel *death* of our Best Savior. All these things could seem to be signs of horror rather than honor, and of sadness rather than joy. Consequently, for this reason, the Sainted PAUL himself felt it necessary to lament in *1 Corinthians 1:23* that he preached Christ crucified, <term>a stumbling block to the Jews and foolishness to the Greeks</term> <note>original Greek: Ἰουዳίοις μὲν σκάνδαλον, ἔλλησι δὲ μωρίαν</note>. But for those of us seeking deeper things, looking not so much at the shell but at the kernel—which is the infinite merit of Jesus Christ our Savior—[it is] acquired <meta>The text breaks here; the catchword indicates the next page begins with "qui"</meta>

***

β) This name is taken from *John 4:14*, where Christ says, <term>the water that I shall give him will become in him a fountain of water springing up into eternal life</term> <note>original Greek: τὸ ὕδωር, ὃ δώσω αὐτῷ, γενήσεται ἐν αὐῷ πηγὴ ὕδατος ἁልሎμένου εἰς ζωὴν αἰώνιον</note>. In the Ethiopic version, the very words cited above are found.

γ) This word also descends from the Greek <term>to bring good news</term> <note>original Greek: εὐαγγέλλειን (*euangellein*)</note>, with the letter *E* removed by <term>aphaeresis</term> <note>A linguistic term for the loss of a sound or letter at the beginning of a word; here, the Greek 'Euangelion' becomes the Ge'ez 'Wanygel'</note>.

### TOPIC IX: CONCERNING THE GOSPEL

...sought, it will easily appear to those who pay attention that all these most harsh fates of our Savior offer a joyful life to us, and present a longed-for entrance to eternal life. For the hunger of Christ is our fullness, his poverty is our riches, his wounds are our beauty and ornament, and the passion and death of Christ himself is our peace and life. Thus, a faithful person can cry out with *Isaiah*, that *Evangelist of the Old Testament*, and say: <note>original Hebrew: כִּי בַּחֲבֻרָתוֹ נִרְפָּא לָנוּ</note> *because in his wounds healing has been prepared for us*, <note>Isaiah 53:5</note>. And the *Abyssinians* <note>a historical term for Ethiopians</note> know this well; for that reason, they are also accustomed to call Holy Week <note>literally: the week of the Passover</note> not a "week of mourning," nor—as the *Germans* do—**the Silent Week** <note>original German: "die stille Woche"</note>, or **the Week of Suffering** <note>original German: "die Marter-Woche"</note>, or **the Week of Mourning** <note>original German: "die Trauer-Woche"</note>, but rather ፋሥሓ : *Fysiha*, <note>original: δ)</note> or *Joy*, because Christ...

---

<gloss>δ) This word occurs elsewhere in *Psalm 28:6* and *James 1:1*. Regarding Holy Week specifically, it is read in *4 Kings 11:6* <note>In many modern Bibles, this corresponds to 2 Kings 11:6</note>. Elsewhere, the Feast of Passover is called by the Ethiopians ፋሥሓ (*Fysiha*), which is the same as ፋስካ (*Fasika*), namely the Christian feast, if the word is used on its own. For the Jewish feast, however, they add the word አይሁድ (*Ayhud*, meaning "of the Jews"). Some scholars argue that a distinction is made in the spelling, so that the Christian feast is written as ፋስካ (*Fasika*) and the Jewish one as ፋሥሓ (*Fysiha*); however, this claim is refuted by several passages, such as *John 2:13*, *Matthew 26:17*, and *Mark 14:1, 12*.</gloss>

### T o p i c  X
## CONCERNING CHRIST

Just as the Gospel presents Christ to us as if painted before our very eyes, so from that same Gospel, anyone who wishes to be saved must come to know Christ intimately. *For this is eternal life: that they may know you, the only true God, and Jesus Christ, whom you have sent* (John 17:3); *and, as Isaiah says in chapter 53:11 <note>original: 53:12</note>, "By his knowledge my righteous servant shall justify many, and he shall blot out their iniquities."* When the Abyssinians <note>a historical term for Ethiopians</note> understood this during their conversion—that an accurate knowledge of Christology <note>original Greek: χριστολογίας, the study of the nature and person of Christ</note> was entirely required for Christianity—they always strove to preserve the doctrine they once received from the Apostles as "intact and well-maintained" <note>original Latin: *sartam tectamque*, a legal idiom meaning to keep a building in good repair; here, it refers to keeping a doctrine pure and unchanged</note>. Since they did not possess much in other articles of faith besides a few truths they held, they were all the more carefully focused on this subject. This can be seen immediately from the beginning of the *Confession of Claudius* <note>An influential 16th-century statement of faith written by Emperor Galawdewos (Claudius) of Ethiopia to explain his nation's beliefs to European Jesuits.</note>, where he recites the entire doctrine concerning Christ in these words: *“We believe in one GOD, and in his only Son JESUS CHRIST, who is his word and his power, his counsel...”*

$\gamma$) For the Abyssinians <note>A historical term for Ethiopians</note> have eight other liturgies in addition to those cited, regarding which you may consult Ludolf in his *Commentary* at the cited place.

...and $\gamma$) he will find how everything here is entirely consistent <note>original Greek: κατ' ὅλου σύμφωνα</note> with the Holy Scriptures. On this point, there is no doubt that, just as the Abyssinians were accustomed to administer the Holy Supper <note>The Eucharist or Communion</note> very frequently, so indeed, from such frequent use of those liturgies, the Clergy and the Laity alike [held] the same...

---

...was conducted. Here, in passing, we add only this: that authors are uncertain regarding its Author. Job Ludolf certainly states in his *Commentary, Book III, Chapter 4, Number 31, Page 341*: "Whether this Dioscorus is that Patriarch of Alexandria—regarded as a saint by the Ethiopians, but as a leader of heresy by the Latins <note>The Western/Catholic Church</note>—is not clear." If we may be permitted to decide anything here, while otherwise respecting the judgments of others on this matter, we pronounce that this very Dioscorus, Bishop of Alexandria, who was condemned for Eutychianism <note>The belief that Christ's divine and human natures merged into a single nature, a view rejected by the Council of Chalcedon</note> at the Council of Chalcedon, is its author. Indeed, no mention is made of any other bishop [by that name] either among the Ethiopians or among the Egyptians and others; or, if there were several, the Ethiopians would have distinguished him with a specific title <note>original Greek: διακριτικῷ</note>. 

But the clearest reason of all, in our view, is that at the end of that Liturgy, he betrayed his Eutychianism by saying: "As His Divinity became [one] with His Humanity" <note>original Ethiopic: በከመ፡ ኮነ፡ መለኮቱ፡ ዘምስለ፡ ትስብእቱ፡</note>, that is, just as His Deity (namely Christ's) is **ONE** with His Humanity. <note>The author highlights "ONE" (neuter) to imply a single nature.</note> N.B. Therefore, [they say] "not one person," <note>The author is contrasting the "one nature" view with the "one person" view.</note> which is the only orthodox position.

Consequently, they most firmly believe and teach that He is a person without parallel, **without a father** <note>original Greek: ἀπάτωρα (apatora)</note> in respect to His human nature, and **without a mother** <note>original Greek: ἀμήτωራ (ametora)</note> in respect to His divine nature, *Hebrews 7:3*. And soon: Is *JESUS CHRIST*, our Lord, a true man, or indeed *GOD*?
And the answer: *GOD* and man together, in one person, without separation, and without change, and without confusion or mixing. From which words it is further clear that the **Abyssinians** <note>term: Habessynos (Abyssinians)—a historical name for the people of the Ethiopian Empire</note> in this manner elegantly avoid, along with the **Orthodox** <note>meaning those holding the "right-teaching" or standard doctrine of the early Church councils</note>, both the Nestorian *separation* <note>original Greek: διάλυσιν (dialysin); refers to the heresy that Christ existed as two separate persons</note> and the Eutychian *confusion* <note>original Greek: σύγχυσιν (synchysin); refers to the heresy that Christ’s divine and human natures were blended into a single new nature</note>. But if you were to ask them about the manner of this union of the two natures in Christ, which was wonderfully made into one person, they themselves must confess with the Apostle from *1 Timothy 3:16*, that **beyond all controversy, great is the mystery of godliness: God was manifested in the flesh**, etc. <note>original Greek: ὅτι ὁμολογουμένως μέγα ἐστὶ τὸ τῆς εὐσεβείας μυστήሪον. ...fallen, so that they denied true humanity to our Greatest Savior, along with the **Marcionites**, **Montanists**, and other heretics. Among these must also be numbered the anonymous author of the Jacobite Epistle written to King Susneus. <note>original: "Regem Susneum." Susenyos I (reigned 1606–1632) was an Emperor of Ethiopia who briefly converted to Catholicism, sparking significant religious debate.</note> ζ) In part 5, in a clearly most impious manner, this author says: *“I am astonished that someone from the sons of LEO—namely, the monks—tells me that when Pope Leo of Rome lived in his time, he was clothed in afterbirth.* <note>original: "fecundinis" (likely a variation of *secundinis*, meaning the placenta or afterbirth). The author is using graphic language to mock the human birth of Christ or the Chalcedonian emphasis on His flesh.</note> *Do you attribute Divinity to Christ? And I said, I attribute it to him most of all. He said...”*

---

...to explain the doctrine of the personal union and communion <note>term: personal union (unio personalis)—the theological concept of how the divine and human natures are joined in the one person of Jesus Christ.</note>. For just as:

I) No human art can produce a single blended mass from fire and water, so too the divine and human natures of Christ were not changed, through the union, into a single new essence, as the **Eutychians** <note>Followers of Eutyches, who argued that Christ's humanity was absorbed into His divinity like a drop of honey in the ocean.</note> used to say. 

II) Just as water can, through union with fire, acquire the properties and qualities of fire—such as heat, burning, etc.—which nevertheless seem to be opposite to the nature of water (which is both wet and cold), while the substance of the water remains otherwise intact; so too the human nature of Christ truly and really received the properties of the Divine nature, yet with its own humanity preserved. 

III) Finally, just as water can take on the properties of fire, but fire cannot take on the properties of water, so too the divine nature does not take on human weaknesses.

ζ) It is unknown who the author of that letter was, though every page shows that he was a Jacobite. <note>term: Jacobite—a member of the Syrian Orthodox Church or related traditions that rejected the Council of Chalcedon.</note> The goal, however...

<summary>This page critiques an anonymous Jacobite author's attack on Pope Leo I and provides a three-part analogy using fire and water to explain the relationship between Christ's divine and human natures.</summary>

When the author cited earlier of the *Letter to R. ### TOPIC 10: ON CHRIST

[page] 80

*...a thing of two, for their natures are neither confounded nor mixed in their being, and because they are the same thing; we call them by the name of union, etc. that is:* <note>original Portuguese: "cousa de duas, nam se confundindo nem misturando suas naturezas em seu ser et por serem a mesma cousa; as chamamos com nome da uniam"</note> When the Romans say that in Christ there are two natures, namely Divinity and Humanity, we ourselves have known this very thing from the beginning even until today. *For we believe that the same Christ, our Lord, is perfect GOD and perfect man—perfect man in His humanity, and perfect GOD in His Divinity.* Indeed, since these natures of His do not exist separately or distinct from one another. *(For His Divinity is united with the flesh and does not exist separately. And His flesh is joined with the Divinity and does not subsist by itself without the Divinity.) Therefore we do not say that they are two, for it is one thing* (They would have spoken more correctly here had they said **One Person** <note>original Latin: "Vnus". The author is critiquing the use of the neuter "one thing" (unum) instead of the masculine "one person" (unus), which was a major point of grammatical and theological contention between Western and Eastern churches</note>, for then they would not be believed to stand on the very border of Eutychianism <note>A heresy that claims Christ's humanity was absorbed into His divinity</note>.) *because one Thing was made from two,* (and here again they would have spoken more correctly had they said **one Person**,) *yet in such a way—NOTE WELL—that the natures were neither confounded nor mixed in their being; and since it is the same thing, we use the name of unity.* 

To these, add another testimony brought forth by the Ethiopian himself, the author of the *Organon* <term>Organon: Likely referring to the *Arganona Weddase* (Harp of Praise), a celebrated collection of Ethiopian hymns and prayers</term>, who sets out his faith in the following words: *I believe in You, Lord, that You are the firstborn of GOD according to Your Essence, and the firstborn of the Virgin according to Your incarnation. I believe in You...*

<meta>catchword: Do-</meta>

<summary>This page explores the nuances of Ethiopian Christology, quoting their defense that while they emphasize 'one nature,' they explicitly deny the mixing or confusion of Christ's divinity and humanity. The author provides a critical commentary, suggesting that their terminology narrowly avoids heresy by failing to distinguish between 'nature' and 'person.'</summary>

### TOPIC 10: ON CHRIST

[page] 81
F
<meta>catchword LO-</meta>

*"Lord, because you are the Son of the Father according to your Divinity, and the Son of Man according to your humanity."* <note>original Latin: "Domine, quod tu es Filius Patris secundum Diuinitatem tuam, et Filius hominis secundum humanitatem tuam."</note> ξ) But the clearest confession of all is that which **Gregory the Amhara** <note>Also known as Abba Gregory (1596–1658), an Ethiopian monk and scholar who was the primary source for Hiob Ludolf’s research.</note> presents in the work of **Ludolf**, o) saying: *"Jesus Christ, after His incarnation, is perfect GOD and perfect man in **one person**, and shall be for ever and ever, and that without separation, without alteration, and without confusion."* And by this account, we have explained that doctrine—easily the most difficult in all of theology, yet no less noble—regarding the **hypostatic union** <term>The union of divine and human natures in the one person of Jesus Christ</term> of the two natures in Christ, as it is practiced among the Ethiopians. Now indeed, we ought to proceed to the *communication of idioms* <term>original Latin: "communicationem Idiomatum". A theological concept explaining how the properties of both the divine and human natures are shared by the one person of Christ</term>, which follows from that union; however, our knowledge of the **Abyssinians** <note>An older term for Ethiopians</note> is so sparse here that almost no evidence of the "three types of communications" <note>In Western scholastic theology, these are categories (genera) used to define how Christ's attributes are shared between his natures</note> or other related matters can be found among them.

# TOPIC 10: ON CHRIST

<meta>catchword: Do-</meta>

*...a thing made of two, neither confounding nor mixing their natures in their being, and since they are the same thing, we call them by the name of union, etc.* <note>original: "consa de duas, nam se confundindo nem misturando suas naturezas em seu ser et por serem a mesma consa; as chamamos com nome da uniam, etc." — This introductory sentence is in an early modern Romance dialect, likely Portuguese, serving as the thesis for the Latin explanation that follows.</note> That is to say: When the Romans <note>Referring to the Roman Catholic Church and the Western theological tradition.</note> say that in Christ there are two natures, namely Divinity and Humanity, we ourselves have known that very thing from the beginning until the present day. For we believe that the same Christ, our Lord, is perfect **GOD**, and perfect man; perfect man in his humanity, and perfect **GOD** in his Divinity. However, since these natures of his do not exist separately or distinct from one another—for his Divinity is united with the flesh and does not exist separately, and his flesh is joined with the Divinity and does not subsist by itself without the Divinity—therefore we do not say that there are two [things], for he is one. <note>The Latin text here suggests a correction: "They would have more correctly said 'One [Person]' here, so that they would not even be thought to stand on the threshold of Eutychianism." Eutychianism was a 5th-century belief, later deemed a heresy, which argued that Christ's human nature was absorbed into his divine nature.</note> Because one Thing was made from two (and here it would be more correct to say one Person), yet in such a way—NB <note>Abbreviation for the Latin "Nota Bene," meaning "Note Well."</note>—that the natures were not confounded or mixed in their being; and because it is the same thing, we use the name of unity. To these, add another testimony brought forth by the Ethiopian himself, the Author of the Organon <note>Likely referring to Abba Giyorgis of Sagla, a 14th-century Ethiopian saint and author of the "Arganonä Maryam" or "Harp of Mary," a major liturgical work often called the "Organ of Praise."</note>, who explains his faith in the following words: "I believe in you, Lord, that you are the firstborn of **GOD** as regarding your Essence, and the firstborn of the Virgin as regarding your incarnation. I believe in you..."*

"...O Lord, that you are the Son of the Father according to your Divinity, and the Son of Man according to your humanity." <note>original Latin: "Domine, quod tu es Filius Patris secundum Diuinitatem tuam, et Filius hominis secundum humanitatem tuam." TOPIC 11: ON JUSTIFICATION

# Topic XI
# ON JUSTIFICATION

However, all this knowledge of Christology <note>original Greek: *christologia*; the theological study of the person and work of Jesus Christ</note> would benefit the Abyssinians <note>A historical term for Ethiopians.</note> little, unless they also applied to themselves through a true and firm *faith* that which Christ earned for us by his Birth, Circumcision, Life, Passion, Death, Resurrection, and Ascension into heaven. For "his FAITH is credited as righteousness to the one who trusts in him who justifies the ungodly," *Romans 4:5* <note>original Greek: *pisteuonti gar epi ton dikaiounta ton asebē logizetai hē PISTIS autou eis dikaiosynēn*</note>. When the Abyssinians recognized this very Pauline oracle <note>Referring to the writings of the Apostle Paul as a divine revelation or "oracle."</note>, they proceeded from knowledge of Christ to assent, and from this to trust, endowed with the most certain hope that "the Name of JESUS is the name above every name, nor is there any other name under heaven given to men, in which we must be saved," *Acts 4:12* <note>original Latin: *Nomen IESVS esse nomen super omne nomen, neque aliud nomen sub coelo datum hominibus, in quo nos oporteat salvos fieri*</note>. Therefore, they lay hold of this one [Christ] in the full assurance of faith <note>original Greek: *en plērophoria tēs pisteōs*</note>. In him alone they hope for the forgiveness of sins and eternal salvation. As a testimony of this faith of theirs, they call themselves *Krestiyanat* <note>Ge'ez: ክርስቲያናት</note> and also *Krestosan* <note>Ge'ez: ክርስቶሳን</note>, that is, Christians—named, of course, after their head and their life, which is Christ. They also call themselves *Mesihawiyan* <note>Ge'ez: መሲሐውያን</note>, "Messianics," from *Mesih* <note>Ge'ez: መሲሕ</note>, the Messiah. They do not, however, call themselves "Messiahs" or "Christs" themselves—which is the madness and spirit<insert>ual pride</insert> of modern fanatics...

# Topic XI
## ON JUSTIFICATION

But all that knowledge of **Christology** <note>The branch of theology relating to the person, nature, and role of Christ.</note> would benefit the Abyssinians <note>A historical term for Ethiopians.</note> very little, unless they also applied to themselves, through a true and firm *faith*, that which Christ earned for us by his Birth, Circumcision, Life, Passion, Death, Resurrection, and his Ascension into the heavens. Since the Abyssinians were familiar with this Pauline decree, they proceeded from a mere knowledge of Christ to a formal assent, and from there to a personal trust, being endowed with the most certain hope that **the name of JESUS is the name above every name, and that there is no other name under heaven given to men by which we must be saved** <note>Acts 4:12</note>.

Therefore, they take hold of this one name **in the full assurance of faith** <note>original Greek: *ἐν πληροφορίᾳ τῆς πίσεως*</note>. In this name alone they hope for the remission of sins and eternal salvation. *(which was)* by the Holy Spirit when he was born of the Virgin, so that he might fulfill your will, and establish for you a Holy people. # TOPIC XII: ON SANCTIFICATION

->of the truth,<- as PAUL speaks in *Ephesians 4:24* <note>original Greek: τῆς ἀληθείας</note>. For although the reborn and justified know—as we proved above in the section *On the Law*—that *even the works of the holiest people are imperfect, and even to those wishing to do good, evil still lies close at hand, Romans 7:21*, they nonetheless always strive *to crucify the flesh with its passions and desires, Galatians 5:24* <note>original Greek: σταυρῶσαι τὴν σάρκα σὺν τοῖς παθήμασι καὶ ταῖς ἐπιθυμίαις</note>. The *Abyssinians* <note>A historical term for Ethiopians.</note> also know that this very thing must be done; and although they do it most eagerly, yet—miraculously—they do not easily slide into spiritual pride and a confidence resting on their own works through such rigorous discipline <term>original Greek: *askesis*, referring to spiritual training or asceticism</term>. β)

# TOPIC XII: ON SANCTIFICATION

->F 4<-

...so that they fall into spiritual pride, which could easily be proven by the examples of the *Pharisees, Messalians, Gnostics, Novatians, Donatists,* and today's *Fanatics* <note>In this context, "Fanatics" refers to radical sectarian groups who believed they could achieve sinless perfection on earth.</note>, if all the historical annals did not already say as much, and the sad experience of our own time did not demonstrate it. Nevertheless, the Abyssinians, although truly devoted to the pursuit of piety, never seem to have descended so far into these deviations, as is evident from the fact that no one has been able to bring any charge against those perfections.

<column-break/>

On the contrary, they most willingly admit that the "perfection of the way" <note>original: *perfectio viae*; a theological term referring to the limited spiritual growth possible during earthly life, as opposed to the "perfection of the homeland" (*perfectio patriae*) in heaven.</note> is imperfect. As much as human frailty allows, they also devote themselves to good works: to fasts, prayers, the frequent use of the Holy Lord’s Supper, the constant reading of the Holy Scriptures, and finally, the willing attendance at public worship. In these and other virtues most worthy of the Christian name, they seem to have surpassed many European customs. 

Lest we be thought to have said this perhaps without merit, and because we cannot provide this testimony in their own words—as they are not accustomed to commend themselves with their own praise—we shall now bring forward the Jesuit Fathers themselves as eyewitnesses <note>original Greek: αὐτόπτας</note> speaking on this matter. Among these, the primary place will easily be held by the confession of Father BALTHAZAR TELLEZ <note>A 17th-century Portuguese Jesuit whose "History of Ethiopia" (1660) was a primary source for European scholars.</note>, which he writes as follows:

...expressed [the matter in this way]: <margin>γ) In *History of Ethiopia*, Book I, chapter XV, page 39.</margin> *“Nearly all have a good understanding and are well-disposed. They are not cruel or bloodthirsty; they easily forgive injuries. There are not many quarrels among them, and those that occur are very rarely resolved by the ‘tribunal of the sword’ <note>original Portuguese: *tribunal da Espada*; a reference to settling disputes through lethal duels or private warfare, which the author contrasts with Abyssinian methods.</note>. They do so without further stays, replies, rejoinders, appeals, or grievances; in this way, they avoid the noise of courtroom pettifoggers <note>original: *rabulas forenses*; a derogatory term for legal hacks or lawyers who exploit the law for profit.</note> and the expenses of the scribes’ pens.”*

They are naturally devoted to equity and justice; once the beating stops, they immediately take arbitrators or approach the local Lord, and propose their cause verbally, without the convolutions of our paper-based lawsuits, and, hearing the sentence, they [acquiesce...]”*

# TOPIC XII: ON SANCTIFICATION 90

*...our Europe the holy faith still remains uncorrupted. — They are very devoted to penance, and no matter how much their <term>Confessor<gloss>A priest who hears confessions and assigns spiritual discipline</gloss></term> imposes, they always say it is still too little, and that they wish to do more. ## Topic XIII
# ON PRAYERS

Moreover, it is no small part of piety and *Holiness* to worship GOD with *Prayers*. Therefore, since we have seen thus far that the Abyssinians <note>The historical name for the people of the Ethiopian Empire</note> burn with no small zeal for piety <note>original Greek: εὐσεβείας (*eusebeias*)</note>, it is easy to conclude that they also deserve no small praise regarding this chief part of Divine worship, which must now be briefly examined. They are commonly accustomed to call their prayers ጸሎታት <note>Ethiopic: *tsalotat*</note>, α) or *Zalotaty*, which they are wont to pour out to the Supreme Deity both within private and public walls. For it must not be thought that they hold the same opinion as the *Indifferentists* <note>Those who believe all religions are equally valid or that religious differences do not matter</note>, β) or the *Quietists* <note>A 17th-century mystical movement that emphasized internal passivity and "quiet" over vocal prayer or external religious works</note>,

---

α) In the singular they are called ጸሎት <note>Ethiopic: *tsalot*</note>. This noun is derived from the root ጸለየ <note>Ethiopic: *tsallaya*</note>, which means the same as *he prayed* or *he poured out prayers*, just as the same word is used in **Mark 1:35** regarding the Most Excellent Savior. Elsewhere it also means *to vow*, as is clear from **Jonah Chapter 3:10**, but this is a secondary meaning.

β) This is, as it were, characteristic of a "fourth way," just as Indifferentists, or men of every religion [who think all faiths are equal],

<meta>catchword: as/prout</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the study of prayer among the Ethiopians, arguing that their religious zeal is expressed through constant private and public devotion. The author provides a linguistic breakdown of the Ethiopic term for prayer and distinguishes their active worship from European movements like Quietism.</summary>

[Quiet]ists, γ) and *Fanatics* of every kind, it is a perverse custom nowadays to abhor performing sacred rites in the common assembly of the faithful and in the place designated for that purpose. In contrast, the *Abyssinians* have their own Oratories and their own Temples, which they call either መጽለዪ <note>Ethiopic: *maṣallayi*</note> — or *Myzylajy* — δ) or መቅደስ <note>Ethiopic: *maqdas*</note> —
<meta>catchword: or</meta>

just as they truly have no religion, so also they care for the worship of no congregation. It is horrifying what is reported concerning <term>C. STURMIO</term> <note>Likely referring to a member of the Sturm family or a specific local figure known for religious indifference</note> in that common verse:

-> - - - *And for ten years* <-
-> *STURMIUS was in the temple only three or four times.* <-

But it is even more horrifying that <term>ARNOLDUS</term> <note>Gottfried Arnold (1666–1714), a radical Pietist historian who criticized organized church structures</note> sings the same tune <note>literally "dances the same dance"</note> and, in his thesis, plainly rejects public worship. Thus he speaks in the works of the *Most Reverend Mr. Erdmann Neumeister in Priestly Lips, page 183*: "Experienced Christians, as living temples, would certainly be free from the external nature of the Church." <note>original German: *Erfahrne Chriſten, als lebendige Tempel, wären allerdings frey von dem äuſſerlichen Kirchen Weſen.*</note>

γ) Regarding the *Quietists* <note>A 17th-century mystical movement emphasizing internal stillness over outward religious observance</note>, the matter is clear. For this is, as it were, their <term>fundamental error</term> <note>original Greek: πρῶτον ψεῦδος (*proton pseudos*)</note>: that they abstain from all external actions. For this reason, they were condemned in the Bull of *Innocent XI* issued against *Miguel de Molinos in 1687*, in *Propositions 5, 14, 15, 16, 20, 21, 23, 24, etc.*, which assert the same things.

δ) That word descends from the root ጸለየ <note>Ethiopic: *tsallaya*</note> cited above. In the Ethiopic *Organon* <note>a liturgical collection</note> of *Ieesi*, it is used for Chapels, such as the nobles of Ethiopia usually have near their houses, but intended only for the sake of praying.

or *Makydaſy* <note>original: መቅደስ, *maqdas*</note>, ε) or sometimes ቤተ: ክርስቲያን: <note>original: *Betä Krǝstiyan*</note> or ክርስቲያን: *Bet Cristos*, or *Christijany*, ζ) i.e. the **House of Christ**, or **Christian House**, or even now called ካኒሳ: *Kanisa*, η) in which they are accustomed to gather daily at fixed hours to serve GOD with hymns and prayers, as well as the reading and hearing of the scriptures. η) Indeed, if anyone here carefully weighs the report of <term>HIOB LUDOLF</term> <note>Hiob Ludolf (1624–1704) was a German orientalist and the founder of Ethiopian studies in Europe.</note>—

# TOPIC XIII: ON PRAYERS
<meta>catchword: li-</meta>

...considers, in which he observes, θ) that the **Ethiopians**, and specifically the enemies of the Fathers of the Society <note>The "Fathers of the Society" refers to the Jesuits, who were active in Ethiopia in the 16th and 17th centuries, often facing local resistance.</note>, addressed the **Lord's Prayer** as nothing other than ጸሎተ: ርክየት: *Zalot Rykety*, that is, **Magic Prayers** <note>original Latin: *Orationes Magicas*</note>. Based on this, one might easily form a negative opinion of the <term>Abyssinians</term> <note>Abyssinia is an archaic name for Ethiopia, derived from the Arabic 'Habash'.</note> regarding this matter. But indeed, anyone who investigates the matter more deeply will find that one should not judge them too harshly, even based on these reports. For, as the same author observes in that same passage, this occurred because the Jesuit Fathers presented the **Lord's Prayer** and the **Angelic Salutation** <note>The "Hail Mary" (Ave Maria).</note> to the Ethiopians in Latin, expressed in Ethiopian letters in roughly this manner:

<note>The following is the Latin "Our Father" written phonetically in Ge'ez (Ethiopian) script.</note>
**Our Father, who art in heaven,**
**Hallowed be thy name, thy**
**Kingdom come. Thy will**
**be done, as in heaven and**
**on earth, etc.**

Which, if you read them according to the nature of those letters, sound so corrupted: *Pater noster, cui is in Kelis. Santifiketur nomen tuum. Adiweniaty Regnum tuum. Fiaty wolunytas tua, sikuty iny Kelo, ety iny tyeryra.* etc. These sounds, when read and naturally pronounced by an **Ethiopian**, would hardly seem like Latin to anyone, even if...

θ) *In his Commentary on the History of Ethiopia, Book III, chapter X, number 110, page 493.*

<meta>This page continues the discussion of linguistic barriers between the Jesuit missionaries and the Ethiopian people. The previous page demonstrated how the Latin "Our Father," when transcribed phonetically into Ethiopian Ge'ez script, became unintelligible noise to the locals. This page explains why they refused to use such prayers and describes their own rich traditions of prayer in their native tongue.</meta>

<page-num>95</page-num>

-> # TOPIC XIII: ON PRAYERS <-

...even if one were exceptionally skilled in that language. 

What wonder is it, then, if the **Ethiopians**, who were not at all learned in Latin, refused to recite in their prayers things they did not understand? For it is necessary that our prayer be *in spirit and in truth* (John 4:24). Otherwise, they are accustomed to use the **Lord’s Prayer** <note>The "Our Father."</note> very frequently while praying. Yet they are not content with this single formula of prayer prescribed to us by the **Greatest and Best Savior**, but they also add other prayers pertaining to the state of the Church or their Commonwealth. Among these is a particularly elegant prayer which they use during the administration of the **Lord’s Supper** <note>The Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note>; its beginning is as follows: 

*Governor of Souls, Leader of the Just, and Glory of the Saints, etc.* <note>original Ge’ez: ሐጼር፡ ነፍሳተ፡ ለጻድቃን፡ ወለቅዱሳን፡ ስብሐት፡ (*Haser nafsata la-sadqan wa-la-qeddusan sebhat*)</note>

Indeed, they even begin and end their common meals—both midday and evening—with prayer. They do this to such an extent that they spend more time *praying* than *banqueting* on such occasions, 1) because

---

1) For **Gregory of Amhara** <note>Abba Gorgoryos (1595–1658), an Ethiopian monk and scholar who was a primary source for European knowledge of Ethiopia.</note> reports in **Ludolf's** *History of Ethiopia, Book II, chapter XII, section 18*, that among them the entire **Psalter** <note>The Book of Psalms.</note> is often recited during frequent banquets without any delay in the feasting. It is pleasing to cite his own words, which flow as follows: *“This, however,” he says, “must not be covered by a veil of silence...”*

<gloss>
u) ...that they begin and end their feasts with prayers. Some of our own **Courtiers** <note>original Latin: *Aulicorum*</note> (Note: Take notice!) should be ashamed of this, as they can scarcely wait for a brief blessing; yet here, the entire **Psalter** <note>The Book of Psalms.</note> is often recited at crowded banquets without delaying the meal. For the person whose duty it is distributes the Psalms among all those present; the **KING** (Note: Take notice!) himself, and those who are regulars, already know their assigned portion; the longer psalms are divided among several people, while some shorter ones are recited by just one individual. In this way, the entire Psalter is recited by various voices in a very short space of time.
</gloss>

<gloss>
x) It is pleasing to also cite the words of **LUDOLF** <note>Hiob Ludolf (1624–1704), a pioneering German scholar who founded Ethiopian studies in Europe.</note> here, from the cited work, Book IV, chapter 4, section 8, and following: "Their diet," he says, "is not only cheap and meager, but also entirely foreign to our customs, for they greedily eat raw or semi-cooked beef." **FRANCISCO ALVAREZ** <note>A Portuguese priest who wrote the first detailed European account of Ethiopia in the 1520s.</note> records an example regarding the Governor of Angot (the **Angot Ras**), who entertained **Rodrigo de Lima**, the Portuguese Legate, with such a feast, though the Legate was greatly nauseated. Indeed, gall <note>Bile from the gallbladder, used in a traditional sauce called *lebu* or *trefe*.</note> is added as a seasoning; their taste is so distorted that it tastes as sweet as honey to them. What is even worse is that they take the eaten and semi-digested herbs from the stomachs of slaughtered oxen—well seasoned with pepper and salt—and eat them like the finest mustard <note>original Latin: *sinapi*. This refers to a dish made from the contents of the rumen, known as *piles*.</note>, as if the health-giving properties of herbs were better recognized by Ethiopian oxen than by humans. These are his words; and lest you think...
</gloss>

λ) In the singular, they call it ስብሐት፡ <note>original: *Sabhat*</note> which is also used in *Exodus 16* regarding **Moses** singing, and it signifies a Song of Praise. For the root word, ሰብሐ፡ <note>original: *Sabha*</note> denotes the same as to extol with praises, to celebrate, and specifically to **God** the Greatest and Best, just as that word is found used once and again regarding God in *Psalm 147*.

μ) This name denotes any *Hymn* or musical poem, because its root is ኀለየ፡ <note>original: *Halaya*</note> that is, *He sang* or *He chanted*; this occurs in *Psalm 101:1*, and is used in *Revelation 14:3* regarding the song of the 144,000 singing before the Lamb.

ν) This name designates a *Song* that is not simple, but rather one adorned with musical instruments; for this reason, since they knew that **David** was a መዘምር፡ <note>original: *Mezemir*, meaning a musician and singer</note>, they called all his Psalms መዛሙራት፡ <note>original: *Mazmuraty*</note>, which is also read in the *Organon* <note>The *Arganona Weddase* or "Harp of Praise," a classic Ethiopian collection of hymns to the Virgin Mary.</note> 150, 3.

<meta>This page continues the critique of those who neglect formal prayer and liturgy. <header>TOPIC XIII: ON PRAYERS</header>

the Fanatics of our time, ($\xi$) <note>original: *Fanaticos*</note> even though in truth [one should pray] with **psalms and hymns and spiritual songs**, <note>original: *ψαλμοῖς καὶ ὕμνοις, καὶ ᾠδαῖς πνευματικαῖς*</note>

$\xi$) What would I wish for more than if, both elsewhere and here as well, I could exempt that infamous **ARNOLD** <note>Gottfried Arnold (1666–1714) was a radical Pietist historian whose "Impartial History of the Church and Heretics" defended many groups the orthodox church labeled as heretics.</note> from the class of those people, as one who was both born and educated in the bosom of the Evangelical Church; but the fact that he himself was the sole maker and supporter of so many errors makes it impossible for me to do so. For, lest he should perhaps seem to have deserted the crowd of Fanatics here as well, in his *Theological Counsels and Responses*, page 255, this impious Pietist speaks impiously: "If you wish to toil with **long prayers or songs**, in the opinion of serving **God** thereby, you will—**note well**—not achieve it; if you wish to place it in other customary exercises," (he alludes without doubt to the use of the **Lord’s Sacred Supper**, which he himself—oh, the crime!—had not received for seven years, always chirping that fanatic rhythm: "**I eat my God in every piece of bread,**") <note>This reflects a radical "spiritualist" view that rejected the physical sacrament of Communion in favor of a constant internal state.</note> "**how is God served by that? It is all His anyway.**" Oh, what an excellent argument (if it please the gods!) <note>A sarcastic classical idiom used to highlight the absurdity of an opponent's claim.</note> to cloak his wickedness. To which also a man of the same reputation, **GICHTEL**, <note>Johann Georg Gichtel (1638–1710), a mystic and follower of Jakob Böhme who advocated for a "priesthood of Melchizedek" and total withdrawal from organized church life.</note> presses his thumb in approval, <note>original: *pollicem premit*; a Latin idiom derived from the Roman Colosseum indicating favor or agreement.</note> who in his *Theological Letters*, Part V, number 124, page 569, writes thus: "**It disturbs me and all of us, even the loud singing.**" To such an extent, clearly, the piety of **Paul** and of the **genuine** <note>original: *γνησίως*</note> **Lutherans** is one thing, and that of the **Pietists** is another. Over such blindness, what truly pious person would not groan and say: "**Enlighten them, O Lord, lest they fall asleep (through such foul errors) in death, lest the enemy (of piety, Satan) say, 'I have prevailed!'**" <note>A paraphrase of Psalm 13:3-4.</note>

<summary>The author identifies radical Pietists Gottfried Arnold and Johann Georg Gichtel as "Fanatics" for their rejection of communal prayer, hymns, and the Eucharist. He contrasts their private mysticism with the biblical injunctions for vocal worship and the liturgical practices of the Ethiopian and Lutheran churches.</summary>

spiritual <note>This completes the phrase "spiritual songs" (πνευματικαῖς) from the previous page.</note> songs, (o) they have been accustomed to adorn their sacred rites according to the command of the Apostle, to which also sometimes a musical harmony is added. In this practice, however, they do not rest merely in the sound of drums, but to show their joy, with which they are moved toward sacred matters and the worship of **GOD**, they are additionally accustomed to clap their hands, which they call *Tafha* (ጠፍሕ). <note>The transcription "Fasycha" appears to be a misreading or variant of the Ethiopic root for clapping or striking.</note>

is said, and to dance <note>original: *tripudiare*, referring to a rhythmic stepping or religious dance</note> with the feet, which they call *Atshaha* (አጸሐስ). <note>original: *Azybaſa*. φ) The author of the **ORGANON** in that passage deals with the praises of the Blessed Virgin Mary, where the cited words are read. He does the same elsewhere, saying: *I clap with my hands to sing psalms to you, and I dance with my feet to celebrate you, as if with a shout of joy.*

ψ) If anyone investigates this rite more deeply and consults the ancient customs of the Hebrews at the same time, he will easily discover that this rite, along with the others, flowed down to the **Abyssinians** <note>An old term for the people of Ethiopia.</note> from the Jews. For when they [the Jews] are about to recite the *Trisagion* <note>The "Thrice Holy" hymn (Greek: τρισάγιον), based on Isaiah 6:3.</note> in the daily morning prayer in this manner—*Holy, Holy, Holy is the **LORD OF HOSTS*** <note>Hebrew: קָדוֹשׁ קָדוֹשׁ קָדוֹשׁ יְהוָה צְבָאוֹת (*Kadosh, Kadosh, Kadosh, YHWH Tzevaot*).</note>—they lift themselves from the earth three times and as if they are leaping, which the Hebrews call *gul* <note>Hebrew: גּוּל (*gul* or *gil*), meaning to rejoice or leap for joy.</note>, while the Greeks call it—

replied to him. ω) "Would he not also marvel at David, of whom it is said in *2 Samuel 6:5: David and all the house of Israel played before the Lord on all sorts of instruments made of fir wood, and on harps, and lyres, and tambourines, and sistrums, and cymbals; and in verse 14: and David danced with all his might before the Lord; and in verse 16: Michal, the daughter of Saul, saw King David leaping and dancing before the Lord, and she despised him in her heart.*" It is truly not believable that David alone danced in this way, or that he introduced a new custom, but rather that many danced with him, and that such was the ancient custom of that nation. From these things, it can be gathered that the Ethiopians do not side with our Pietists <note>A 17th-century movement within Lutheranism that emphasized individual piety and often viewed traditional forms of entertainment, like dancing, as sinful.</note> even on this point, who wish every kind of dance to be banished to the Garamantes. <note>An ancient tribal civilization in the Sahara; used here as a metaphor for the ends of the earth or total exile.</note> α) For indeed, they are said "to exult," <note>original Greek: *agalliasthai*</note> of which mention is also made in *Joel 2:23*, and *Psalm 32:11*, and elsewhere. it may seem that the *Abyssinians* <note>A historical name for the people of Ethiopia.</note> flee in prayer not only to GOD, but also to the deceased *Saints*, the *Blessed Virgin Mary*, and the good *Angels*, and that they venerate and adore them. Indeed, they often carry names constructed from the name of the Blessed Mary ($\beta$), and they celebrate clearly as many annual feast days for the Holy Archangel Michael as they have designated for honoring the Nativity <note>Christmas</note> of Christ our Savior ($\gamma$); indeed, they have dedicated to the Blessed Virgin a number clearly exceeding both of these, containing 32 feast days ($\delta$). From which it could be gathered, that Ma-

->G 4<-

$\beta$) There are many such names, of which it is pleasing to bring forward only a few, such as: *Maryam Senait* <note>original: ማርያም፡ ሠናይት</note>, that is, Mary is his beauty. *Maryam Mogas* <note>original: ማርያም፡ ሞገስ</note>, that is, Mary is his grace. *Maryam Kibra* <note>original: ማርያም፡ ክብረ</note>, that is, Mary is his glory. *Za-Maryam* <note>original: ዘማርያም</note>, that is, Servant, or, one devoted to Mary. *Za-Dengel* <note>original: ዘድንግል</note>, that is, Servant of the Virgin. *Za-Mala’ak* <note>original: ዘመልአክ</note>, that is, Servant of the Angel. *Mahtsante Dengel* <note>original: ማኅፀንተ፡ ድንግል</note>, that is, Entrusted to the Virgin. *Zar’a Yohannes* <note>original: ዘርአ፡ ዮሐንስ</note>, that is, Seed of John, etc.

$\gamma$) Namely, the Ethiopians are accustomed to celebrate the Nativity of Christ every month, and specifically on the 29th day of each month, and thus they celebrate 12 Christmas festivals throughout the year; they also have just as many feasts sacred to the Archangel Michael, which they are accustomed to celebrate on the 12th day of any month.

$\delta$) This was also the reason why their King *Zar'a Ya'eqob* <note>reigned 1434–1468; a major reformer of the Ethiopian Church known for increasing the number of festivals dedicated to Mary</note> in a Letter to the Ethiopian Monks in Jeru- <note>The text likely continues "Jerusalem."</note>

### TOPIC XIII: ON PRAYERS

...of Michael, and other Saints, that there is among them if not a greater, then at least an equal veneration as for Christ, and that *service* <note>original Greek: δουλεία (douleia), often distinguished in theology from higher worship</note> and *worship* <note>original Greek: λατρεία (latreia), worship reserved for God alone</note>—which belong to God alone (ε)—are bestowed upon the creature in place of the Creator. But it must be maintained that the Abyssinians <note>A historical term for people from the Ethiopian Highlands</note> themselves do not so much invoke the dead Saints to bring them aid, as they honor them with praises and greetings that are sometimes excessively superlative (ζ). From which also the Fes-

> *...those living in Jerusalem, in their writings, urged them among other things to celebrate annually all the Feasts of our Lady Mary, which are thirty-two in number in the book of her miracles.*

ε) For that *service* and *worship* are synonyms in the style of the Holy Spirit is most clearly evident from Acts 20:19, compared with Matthew 4:10; from 1 Thessalonians 1:9, compared with Hebrews 9:14; from Galatians 4:9, compared with Ephesians 6:7, and several other passages of Scripture.

ζ) We could cite hundreds of such greeting formulas here from a single Ethiopic Organon <note>The *Organon Maryam* (Harp of Mary), a famous collection of Ethiopic hymns and prayers</note>, although it is not entirely comprised of them. ---

<margin>ζ) They are also called at other times those solemn Feasts <note>original Ge'ez: መዋዕለ፡ እመራት (Mawala Emurat)</note> "Days of Appointed Times"; but this name also fits the others, at least in its grammatical sense, because the name <note>original Ge'ez: እመር (Emur)</note> denotes something "established" and "prefixed." Thus, *Mawala Ymuraty* carries the same meaning: namely, a fixed time and day destined for divine worship. However, the name **Remembrances** <note>original Ge'ez: ተዝካራት (Tazkarat)</note> seems to descend from the Hebrew <note>original Hebrew: זכר (zakar)</note>, which denotes "to remember," from which also comes the Ethiopic root <note>original Ge'ez: ዘከረ (zakara)</note>. From this root descends the name *Tazkar*, which sometimes denotes a <note>original Greek: τεκμήሪον (tekmērion)</note>—that is, a sign or token of memory, as in the title of *Psalm 37*—and at other times denotes a Feast and solemnity celebrated in memory of someone. Compare Hiob Ludolf <note>A 17th-century German scholar regarded as the founder of Ethiopian studies</note> in his *Lexicon of the Ethiopic Language, page 364.*</margin>

<margin>η) Moreover, it cannot be denied that certain foundations of this practice were already laid in the 4th century, but it especially took on growth in the 5th century. # TOPIC XIII: ON PRAYERS

Here too is the simplicity of the Abyssinians, who both *reverently worship the saints, and treat them with almost divine veneration, and yet nonetheless sometimes pray again to the Supreme Deity for their safety, rest, and happiness.* <note>The author finds it contradictory that the Abyssinians pray TO saints as powerful figures, yet also pray FOR the saints' souls as if they still need God's mercy.</note> 1) But it is necessary that those intercessors be wretched... <note>The sentence breaks off here and continues on the next page.</note>

[Page] 388, saying: *Those who call us saint-worshipers <note>original Greek: ἁγιολάτρας</note> err most gravely. For we Greeks worship <note>original Greek: λατρεύομεν</note> only one God in Trinity, and Trinity in Unity <note>original Greek: ἕνα Θεὸν ἐν τριάδι, καὶ τριάδα ἐν μονάδι</note>, but the saints, as faithful friends of GOD, we pursue with honor no differently than a faithful friend.* If things were indeed so, we would congratulate them for having better wisdom. But if we consider this matter more deeply **considering it carefully**, it will appear that while the Greeks indeed seem to wish not to worship the saints with divine cult, they nevertheless do this with another, lesser kind [of worship]. Hence even **JEREMIAH** himself in the same *Acts*, p. 127, concedes that *invocation belongs to GOD alone, and it belongs to Him primarily and most properly. But the invocation,* he says, *which is made to the saints is not properly invocation, but through accident and through grace.* For it is not Peter or Paul who hears their invokers, but that **NB.** <note>Latin "Nota Bene": Note Well</note> grace which they possess. From which it is clear that the Greeks indeed do not wish to be categorized among the crude **saint-worshipers** <note>original Greek: ἁγιολάτρας</note>, nor yet can they be entirely excused from **saint-worship** <note>original Greek: ἁγιολατρείᾳ</note>, at least of a subtle kind.

it is necessary [to consider] those who themselves still stand in need of our intercession. For if the matter is indeed so, then the blessed dead—who are partakers of the beatific vision <note>The "beatific vision" is the ultimate direct encounter with God in heaven, which is thought to bring perfect happiness.</note>—are not yet [complete in their state]. If this were the case, the Abyssinians profit nothing at all for them with their prayers. 

However, if we do not wish to find the Abyssinians guilty of this improper terminology <note>original Greek: ἀκυρολογίας</note> and logical inconsistency <note>original Greek: ἀσυλλογισίας</note>, it seems that all their worship directed toward the deceased Saints extends not so much to a religious invocation as to a most ardent wish for them and a friendly greeting to the Blessed. Nonetheless, it would be better to abstain from all these things, as they are close incitements to saint-worship <note>original Greek: ἁγιολατρείαν</note>, especially since no command, promise, or example of this matter exists in the Canonical Scriptures.

One further point remains to be brought forward on this occasion: since the Abyssinians worship and venerate the deceased Saints more than is right, it could be asked: *Might they not also be said to be guilty of image-worship <note>original Greek: ἰκονολατρείας</note>, which is usually the daughter of saint-worship?* If we are to provide any answer to this, we judge that while traces of the worship of images are indeed rarer among them, they are not entirely absent, since images, which—

---

$\varkappa$) Even from this name and its grammatical meaning, it can be clear that the Abyssinians tolerate no images except painted ones. For they call their images *Syalit* (ሥዕላት): a name that descends from the root *sy'ala* (ሥዕለ), and denotes "to paint" and "to dye with colors." This word occurs in this sense in 1 Kings 6:29 <note>The text notes the Ethiopians follow the numbering of 3 Kings</note>, where that which the blessed Great Man <note>original Latin: "Megalander," a title of honor</note> **LUTHER** rendered as "carving" <note>original German: *Schnizwerck*</note>, they [render] as *Syalit*, or "paintings." Perhaps they do this so they can better prove that solid images were not even used in the Old Testament. This, however, they do in vain, since God Himself both permitted them to be made and commanded their making (compare Exodus 25:18, Ezekiel 4:1-2, etc.). Nor do they seem to have successfully grasped the meaning of the Holy Spirit here, since the Hebrew word *pittuach* <note>original Hebrew: פתוח</note> nowhere in the Scriptures denotes a painting, but rather a sculpture or a "carved image" (compare 2 Chronicles 2:14, Zechariah 3:9, Exodus 28:11, Psalm 74:6, etc.). Thus, here too, the version of the blessed Luther is as conformed as possible to the original text.

$\lambda$) Hiob Ludolf <note>A 17th-century German scholar regarded as the founder of Ethiopian studies in Europe</note> reports on this matter in his *Commentary*...

## Topic XIV
# ON BAPTISM

**T**he Abyssinians <note>A historical name for Ethiopians</note> acknowledge two Sacraments of the New Testament (for whatever objections could be raised here regarding their practice of circumcision have already been examined above in the Topic on the Law), the first of which, by reason of its order, is the **Sacrament of Baptism**. It is called *Tymykaty* <note>original Ge'ez: ጥምቀት, pronounced Timkat</note> by them; which name, just as it descends from the root *Atymak* <note>original Ge'ez: አጥመቀ, pronounced Atamaqa</note>, denoting "to baptize" and consequently "to make a Christian," so by this very fact the Abyssinians suggest at first glance that **Baptism is entirely the Sacrament of Initiation**, which they are accustomed to administer to infants and adults alike. As for the rites by which it is performed, we shall bring forward regarding this matter not our own words, but those of the so often and highly praised **JOB LUDOLF** <note>Job Ludolf (1624–1704) was a German scholar considered the founder of Ethiopian studies in Europe</note>, who writes: *a*) *"The priest [performs] the Baptism of adults (who*

---

<margin>Book III, chapter 6, page 372: stating that when the Fathers of the Society <note>The Jesuits, who were expelled from Ethiopia in the 1630s</note> were departing into exile, they [the Ethiopians] broke and crushed all signs, even the most venerable ones of Saint Mary; for this reason, they even customarily called them specifically *Taotaty* <note>original Ge'ez: ጣዖታት</note>, or "Idols."</margin>

*a) In the History of Ethiopia, Book III, chapter VI, number XXXI, and following.*

<header>TOPIC XIV: ON BAPTISM</header>
<page-num>111</page-num>

(who are frequent there due to the conversion of the Heathens), being about to perform the rite, he begins with a reading from Psalm 51, and once the incense has been offered, he asks for the names of those to be baptized. Then, after several prayers have been recited and while the Deacon frequently exhorts the Hearers <note>Also known as catechumens; those receiving instruction before baptism</note> to pray, he anoints various parts of the body with holy oil and places his hand upon the heads of each individual. 

When this is done, the neophytes <note>New converts</note> raise their right hands and, facing the west, renounce Satan as the prince of darkness; soon, turning toward the east as if toward the sun of righteousness, they raise their right hands high and pledge a vow <note>original: "sacramentum"; here referring to a solemn oath of loyalty similar to a soldier's pledge</note> to Christ, and they profess the Creed of the Christian faith led by the Priest, and answer that they believe when he questions them. After these things, the anointing is repeated, and several Pericopes <note>Selected passages of Scripture read during a service</note> from the Gospel of John, the Acts of the Apostles, and the Pauline Epistles are read, with the usual Liturgy added; finally, the oil is poured into the baptismal water and spread in the shape of a cross. 

After many prayers are said again, the priest descends into a pool hollowed out for this purpose before the doors of the sacred building, and three times he immerses the whole body of those led to him by the Deacon, and baptizes them in the **NAME OF GOD THE FATHER, AND OF THE SON, AND OF THE HOLY SPIRIT.** Men are present to assist the men, and women the women, who help them as they come up out of the river or pool, and by lifting them up they "receive" them; for this reason, they were called *susceptores* or *susceptrices* <note>original Latin terms meaning "receivers"; the historical roots of the terms for godfathers and godmothers</note> by the ancients. Thus washed and anointed again as if newborn, they are clothed in a white tunic <note>original: "interula"</note>, to [signify] the purity of the soul...

They are clothed, and having been dressed in a red garment in memory of the salvation won by the blood of Christ, they are then for the first time introduced into the Church, where, mingled with the Christians, they become participants in the Holy Supper <note>The Eucharist or Holy Communion</note>. To those departing, milk and honey are given, and with a hand placed upon the head, they are dismissed with a blessing: GO IN PEACE, SONS OF BAPTISM. For the Abyssinians <note>Habessyni; a historical term for Ethiopians</note> frequently call Christians "Sons of Baptism" <note>original Ethiopic: ወለድ፡ ጥምቀት፡ (Wälädä T'əmqät)</note>.

Almost all of these things agree with the rites of the ancients. *We immerse three times*, (says Tertullian <note>A prolific early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155 – c. 220 AD)</note> in *On the Soldier's Crown* <note>original Latin: "de Corona Militis"</note>), *and then we taste the fellowship of the milk and honey we have received.* These rites are observed toward adults. However, the procedure for baptizing infants is much shorter; nevertheless, in former times, males were not baptized before the 40th day, and females not before the 80th day, unless the danger of death was imminent. Now, however, Baptism is hastened, especially if the illness of the infants requires it.

The godparents <note>original Latin: "Susceptores"</note> respond for them to the Priest's questioning; however, they are **NB. not totally immersed** <note>NB: Latin abbreviation for "Nota Bene" or "Note Well"</note> in the water, but are sprinkled <note>The practice of aspersion</note> and washed with it, and this occurs at the threshold of the sacred building, as if a place in the Church were not owed to them before Baptism. *So says he* <note>Referring to the author being cited, likely Job Ludolf, whose work on Ethiopia is the source of this description</note>.

From these things it can be seen that they not only teach, along with us, that both immersion and sprinkling are free and lawful by their own practice, but also, by such a standing of infants outside the Church, they suggest that those infants are not holy by their own nature, nor [holy] by birth because of their parents...

### TOPIC XIV: ON BAPTISM

"One baptism for the remission of sins"
<note>Original Ge'ez: በአሐቲ፡ ጥምቀት፡ ለስርየት፡ ኀጢአት፡ (*ba-aḥatti ṭemqat la-śeryata ḫaṭī'at*). This is the concluding phrase of the section on baptism in the Nicene Creed, used by both the Ethiopian and Western churches.</note>

that is, *And we believe in one Baptism for the remission of sins.* Since this was the case, the *Fathers of the Society* <note>The Jesuits, a Catholic religious order that attempted to bring the Ethiopian Church under the authority of the Pope in the 16th and 17th centuries.</note> could not help but recognize those who had been baptized by their own Deacons as being truly baptized. <note>n)</note> For this reason, even *FRANCISCO ÁLVARES* <note>A Portuguese priest and explorer who lived in Ethiopia from 1520 to 1526. But if he is to be so greatly trusted, what should we then say about the annual reports of the *Fathers of the Society* who wrote back to Europe? *NB. Note that various baptismal formulas used by the Abyssinian <note>A historical name for Ethiopians.</note> Clerics were partly unusual, partly even foolish, and plainly altered the essence of baptism—for example: "I baptize you in the name of the Most Holy Trinity," or...*

### TOPIC XIV: ON BAPTISM

->among<-

...those of age, in memory of the Baptism of the Best Savior <note>Referring to Jesus Christ.</note>—which many of the ancients hold for certain to have happened on that day—the King with the leaders of his court, the Metropolitan <note>The *Abuna*, or head of the Ethiopian Church.</note> with the Clergy, nobles with commoners, old and young, descend naked (with very few exceptions among the most prominent) into rivers or ponds before sunrise. There, by immersing themselves, they spend a joyful day, as **Ludolf** λ) [describes].

<margin>
κ) [The months consist] of only 30 days, as with other Eastern peoples. Since these, when calculated, did not fully correspond to the solar cycle, they added additional days called "epagomenal" <note>Days added to a calendar to make it match the solar year; in Ethiopia, these five or six days form a "thirteenth month."</note> or, as they call them, **Pagumen** <note>original Ge'ez: ጳጉሜን : Pāgumēn</note>. These are 12 in number <note>The text specifies 12, though typically there are 5 or 6; this may refer to a specific historical calculation or a transcription of the 12-month system.</note>, but thus their year is again about 6 or 7 days longer than ours, which causes that discrepancy in counting. β) They begin the start of the year not with us from the Feast of the Circumcision of Christ <note>January 1st.</note>, but from August 29 according to the Julian Calendar, following the custom of all Eastern Patriarchs. The structure of their year was examined in a very learned Mathematical Dissertation by the Magnificent Lord Dr. **Heinrich Klausing**, once also our most esteemed teacher.
</margin>

Whether a solid argument can be sought that the world was truly created in autumn, we ourselves shall consider at another time, "if the Lord wills and we live" <note>original Greek: ἐὰν ὁ Κύριος θελήσει καὶ ζήσωμεν</note>, in a special Exercise: *On the Time of the World's Creation*, or **Bereshit** <note>original Hebrew: בראשית , meaning "In the beginning," the first word of the Book of Genesis.</note>.

λ) Book III of the *History*, chapter VI, number 43, and following. <note>A reference to Hiob Ludolf’s *Historia Aethiopica*, the primary 17th-century European source on Ethiopia.</note>

For they do not recite the words of the **Creed** <note>Specifically the Apostles' Creed, used as a confession of faith during baptism.</note>—which rivals our own—nor do they use **Exorcism**, nor do they pour oil into the water in the shape of a cross <note>A standard liturgical practice in many ancient Christian traditions where blessed oil (chrism) is added to the font.</note>, nor are they led by the Ministers of the Church into a **Baptistery**.
