<meta>catchword: exci-</meta>

> Abyssinians, and [regarding] that Sacrament, to speak in the language of the schools, as a **property of the fourth kind** <term>A "property of the fourth kind" (*proprium quarto modo*) is a technical term from scholastic logic. It refers to a characteristic that is unique to a specific subject, belongs to every instance of it, and is always present—making it an essential, defining feature.</term>, which applies to every instance, only, and always to Baptism: that immediately after its administration, the baptized are also admitted to the partaking of the **Lord’s Holy Supper** <note>The Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note>. From this it has happened that, because they did not want baptized infants to be deprived of the Holy Eucharist, yet knowing they were unable to actually eat bread, they dip particles of the sacred bread—which they call **Qwerban** <note>original Ge'ez: ቈርባን (Qwerban), meaning "oblation" or "sacrifice," referring to the Eucharistic bread.</note>—into blessed wine and moisten their lips with that wine, as will be reported more fully in the following section. But indeed, this "property," or inseparable consequence of Baptism, is clearly absent here [in the Epiphany ritual]; on the contrary, it seems to us that on this day alone, all those who are healthy at least abstain from the Lord’s Supper, as they go off to feast immediately after emerging from the baths and spend that entire day joyfully amidst their cups.

ν) And this reason is of the utmost strength for clearing the **Ethiopians** from the error of the **Anabaptists** <note>A term (meaning "re-baptizers") used here as a theological accusation; the author is defending the Ethiopians against the charge that their annual ritual is a heretical "second baptism."</note> which has been charged against them. For we conclude thus: if that washing customary among the Abyssinians every year were held to be a Sacrament, it would follow that they either establish two Baptisms, or if only one, that they also treat the **Neophytes** <note>New converts to the faith.</note> and those who are essentially **Proselytes**—whom they call **Gabar** <note>original Ge'ez: ገበር (Gabar), meaning "servant" or "worker," used here to refer to converts or those undergoing religious instruction.</note> (which word indeed, both in its letters and its signifi-
<meta>catchword: catione</meta>

<header>TOPIC XV: ON THE HOLY LORD’S SUPPER</header>

# Topic XV
## ON THE HOLY LORD’S SUPPER.

The other Sacrament of the New Testament is the Lord’s Supper, or, as it is called by the **Abyssinians** <note>A historical term for Ethiopians.</note>, ምስጢር: **Mestir** <note>original Ge'ez: ምስጢር; transcribed in the text as *Myſtiry*</note>, a) i. e. **Secret**, or

contrary <note>This completes the word *contrarium* from the previous page, finishing the argument on baptism.</note>, for if this rite were held as a Sacrament by the **Abyssinians**, the Deacons and Subdeacons would also descend with them into the lake to do the same thing, knowing that the Sacraments are to be used by the whole Church, and thus by all who are called Saints; but they do none of these things, as we have said before. If we consider all these things together, we finally arrive at the opinion that the same should be thought of this rite as of the rite of immersion (which in German is called **Baden** [Bathing], in Slavic **Kupati**, and in Hungarian **Fereszteni**) which is customarily practiced in our homeland on the second day of Easter; or of the rite of marking with ashes, commonly called **Einscherung** <note>The ritual of Ash Wednesday.</note>, as is the custom among the **Latins** <note>Roman Catholics</note>, although the Latins attribute more virtue to this than those **Abyssinians** certainly do.

a) This word, as is easily apparent to anyone, does not acknowledge an Ethiopic root, but arose from the Greek *mysterion* <note>original Greek: μυστήριον; meaning mystery or sacrament</note>. For it happened that they, along with other nations—for example, the **Syrians**, **Copts**, and others—borrowed many words from the Greeks. And regarding the **Copts**, we have given more than one example in our *Harmony of Eastern Languages* <note>original Latin: *Harmonia LL. OO.* (Linguarum Orientalium)</note>; while concerning the Abyssinians...

Although the most ingenious **Drusius** <note>Johannes Drusius (1550–1616), a Protestant Old Testament scholar.</note> not incorrectly establishes that the Greek word descends from the **Hebrew**, *mistur* <note>Hebrew: מסתור</note>, which is from *satar* <note>Hebrew: סתר</note>, *he hid, he concealed*; from this it also designates among the **Ethiopians** sacred and hidden things, such as the **Sacred Supper**. For besides this, I do not remember it occurring elsewhere in this sense except in the **Council of Ancyra** <note>A church council held in 314 AD.</note>, where it is said ተወንጭፎ : ቍርባን : <note>Ge'ez: *Tawanchifo Qurban*</note> for the blessed bread and wine. For it commonly denotes any gift offered to **GOD**, as is clear from **Matthew 8:4, and 12:7, and elsewhere.**

# TOPIC XV: ON THE HOLY LORD’S SUPPER

recognizing themselves as transgressors of all the commandments, they carry and bear this one thing in their mouths: አበስነ፡ አበስነ፡ *Abasyny! Abasyny! We have sinned! We have sinned!* <note>Ethiopic: *Abasne*, meaning "we have sinned."</note> Perhaps for this reason they imitate that saying of the prodigal son, who, returning to his father, cried out with a loud voice, *Father, I have sinned against heaven and in your sight,* δ) <note>original Greek: Πάτερ ἥμαρτον εἰς τὸν ὀρανόν καὶ ἐνώπιόν σου</note> hoping that they will most certainly obtain the remission of their sins, just as that repentant one did. Then, just as they never appear in their temples without offering their little gifts, so also in this solemnity especially, they are accustomed to declare themselves generous toward the ministers of the divine word, by offering them *incense and bread.*

δ) *Luke 15:18.* Furthermore, if you should ask from where that prodigal son learned this formula, then we respond: from that rite of the Jews which those offering sacrifices used when they laid their hands upon the victim. For so <term>R. MOSES MAIMONIDES</term> <note>Rabbi Moses ben Maimon (1138–1204), a preeminent Jewish scholar and philosopher</note> in *Work of the Sacrifices, chapter 3* <note>original title: *Maaseh Korbanot*</note>, after he had reviewed the manner of imposing one's sins upon the victim, attaches the following formula accepted by those confessing:

> <note>original Hebrew: אנא השם חטאתי עויתי פשעתי כן וק ; וחוזרתי בת תשובה וזו כפרתי</note> *I beseech You, O Lord! I have sinned, I have been perverse, I have rebelled, I have conducted myself in this and that manner, but now I return to repentance, and let this be my atonement.*

That is: *I pray, Lord! I have sinned, I have failed, I have rebelled, I have conducted myself in this and that manner, but now I return to repentance, and let this be my expiation.* These things certainly illustrate the formula of the Abyssinians <note>A historical term for Ethiopians</note> in no small measure.

acknowledging themselves as transgressors of all the commandments, they carry and bear that single phrase in their mouths: **We have sinned! We have sinned!** <note>original Ge'ez: አበስነ፡ አበስነ፡ (*Abasyny! Abasyny!*)</note>; perhaps for this reason they imitate that [saying] of the prodigal son, who, returning to his father, cried out with a loud voice, **Father, I have sinned against heaven and before you** <note>original Greek: Πάτερ ἥμαρτον εἰς τὸν ἐρανὸν καὶ ἐνώπιόν σου</note>, δ) hoping that they will most certainly obtain the remission of their sins, just as that repentant son did. Furthermore, just as they never appear in their temples without offering their small gifts, so also in this solemnity especially, they are accustomed to declare themselves generous toward the ministers of the divine word by offering to them ***incense, and***

# TOPIC XV: ON THE HOLY LORD’S SUPPER

...so much so, that a poor Abyssinian woman once brought a small cat for catching mice (which that region does not lack), saying that she had nothing else to offer except that kitten, which was of a very good breed. In addition to these offerings, they are devoted to fasts—which they call *Som* <gloss>original: ጾም</gloss> or *Azywamy*— ζ) for that entire day on which they participate in the holy communion <term>Synaxis: A Greek term for the gathering of the faithful for the Eucharist</term>, to such an extent that they do not even take food toward evening if it is a day of fasting; η) indeed, so great is the observance of fasts that it verges almost on superstition. θ) In this regard, on the other hand, it seems to us they should be criticized...

---

ζ) This name descends from the Ethiopic root *Soma* <gloss>original: ጾመ</gloss>, which is the same as "to fast." In the past tense it is *Soma* <gloss>original: ጾመ</gloss>, meaning "he was fasting," from which *Som* <gloss>original: ጾም</gloss> designates a "Fast," and *Soma'i* <gloss>original: ጾማዒ</gloss> designates a "Faster." This root also seems to have arisen from the Hebrew *Tsum* <gloss>original: צום</gloss>, which likewise means to fast, and from which the Arabic *Sawm* <gloss>original: صوم</gloss>, designating the same thing, also originated.

η) We have these details from **LUDOLF** <note>Job Ludolf (1624–1704), a leading European scholar of Ethiopian history and language</note>, who in his *History of Ethiopia, Book III, ch. 6, § 83*, says: *They celebrate the communion fasting, even around evening, if it be a fasting day.* However, **ALVAREZ** <note>Francisco Álvares, a Portuguese missionary who traveled to Ethiopia in the 1520s</note> seems to contradict this in his *Acts of the Embassy of Rodrigo de Lima, ch. 13*, where he denies that *the Ethiopians take the Holy Communion on a fasting day.*

θ) The Jesuit Fathers themselves acknowledged this in their *Annual Report of 1624, p. 29*, writing: *And fasting in this country is held in such observance, rigor, and credit, that they would commit any great sin rather than vio—* <note>The original Italian text is cut off: "più tosto che vio- [lare]" meaning "rather than violate it."</note>

<meta>This page continues the theological discussion regarding the practices of the Ethiopian Church (Abyssinians). # TOPIC XV: ON THE HOLY LORD’S SUPPER

...should be criticized, not only because they immediately admit infants to the practice of the **Holy Supper**, but even when they wish to offer it to them, they [touch] the lips of the infants with nothing but consecrated wine...

<note>original Italian: "Onde si sono ritrovati alcuni, liqualiani haunno voluto morire che bever un mezzo bicchiere d’ aqua in dì di digiuno."</note>
Wherefore some have been found who preferred to die rather than drink half a glass of water on a fasting day. That is to say: **Fasts** in this region are held in such observance, vigor, and esteem that they would rather commit any great sin than violate them. Therefore, some were found who even preferred to die than to drink half a cup of water on a fast day. Compare these things with those read in chapter X, page 38, face B, and elsewhere. Furthermore, that rite of fasting on the **fourth** and **sixth** <term>Feria: The Latin term for a weekday; here referring to Wednesday and Friday</term> day of the week seems to have arisen from the custom of the Jews, who also used to fast **twice** <note>original Greek: Δὶς</note> [a week] as the Pharisee admits in Luke 18:12, specifically **on the second and fifth days** <note>original Hebrew: בשני ובחמישי</note> <gloss>Monday and Thursday</gloss> according to the witness of **Maimonides** <note>Moses Maimonides (1138–1204), a preeminent medieval Jewish philosopher and scholar</note> in the book *On Fasts* <note>original Hebrew: תעניות — Taanit</note> chapter 1, § 5. However, the Christians changed these days, as is clear from the **Apostolic Canon** <note>A set of early Christian decrees on church order</note> beginning "The fasts..." <note>original Greek: Αἱ δὲ νησῆαι</note>, which sounds like this in Latin: *Do not fast with the hypocrites*, that is, with the Jews who pretended to be Christians, for they fast on the second and fifth day of the week. Therefore, the Christians chose the fourth and sixth—or the day of **Mercury** ☿ <gloss>Wednesday</gloss> and **Venus** ♀ <gloss>Friday</gloss> according to our naming—and this because on the fourth day the Jews entered into the plan to seize Christ, and on the sixth day they actually delivered Christ to death, as this reasoning can be read in **Epiphanius**, **Augustine** (Volume 2, Works, Letter 86), and **Jerome** (Letter to Licinius, *Concerning Consecration*, Distinction 3, chapter "On Fasting" 16).

1) Compare Hiob Ludolf in *History*, Book III, chapter 6, note 53; but indeed, the Ethiopians are not the only ones who do this, since the Maronites, Nestorians, Jacobites, and Georgians also have this established as a custom, according to the witness of Gabriel Sionita, a Maronite, in Berthold Nihus's *On Eastern Communion Under One Species*, note 2, page 1667. # TOPIC XV: ON THE HOLY LORD’S SUPPER

...diluted, nor artificially made, following the example of Christ who instituted it—which ought to be like a law to us—they produce and administer it. ϰ) Indeed, an objection could be raised in support of their opinion: since their region is not wine-bearing, and yet that raisin-wine <term>passum: a sweet wine made from dried grapes</term> seems to have an analogy <note>original Greek: ἀναλογίαν</note> with our wine, those people, being placed in a situation of such necessity, might be able to use it without violating the essence of the Sacrament or their own conscience—

---

ϰ) We certainly should not fall into the opinion that they do not have vines and vineyards in Ethiopia, for they have very excellent ones indeed; however, they cannot produce wine, partly due to the excessive sweetness of the grapes, which keeps the juice expressed from them turbid and unrefined, and partly due to the excessive heat of the sun, which causes the young juice <term>mustum: unfermented grape juice</term> to turn sour quickly. Regarding this matter, **DR. MARTIN HARTMANN**, *formerly a Physician of Weimar* <note>original Latin: Vinariensis</note>, being consulted, replied as follows: *“Not only because of the heat, but because of the excessive sweetness of the grapes, fermentation cannot take place properly. For legitimate fermentation requires not only material suitable for fermenting (that is, particles that are neither too raw nor too ripe), but also tempered air—namely, neither too hot nor too cold. The former accelerates fermentations, while the latter prevents them entirely.”* Consult, if you like, **THOMAS WILLIS**, *an English Physician, in his Treatise on Fermentation, insofar as it is carried out in artificial processes, chapter 6, throughout.*

It includes scientific commentary from 17th-century physicians Martin Hartmann and Thomas Willis regarding the chemical requirements for wine-making.</summary>

<margin>z)</margin> We should not, of course, adopt the opinion that they lack vines and vineyards in Ethiopia; they certainly have the most excellent ones. However, they are unable to produce wine, partly due to the excessive sweetness of the grapes—which keeps the juice pressed from them cloudy and unrefined—and partly due to the extreme heat of the sun, which causes the **must** <gloss>original: *mustum*; unfermented grape juice</gloss> to sour quickly. For proper fermentation requires not only a material fit for fermenting (that is, particles that are neither too raw nor too ripe), but also temperate air—specifically, neither too hot nor too cold. The former hastens fermentations too much, while the latter hinders them entirely.”* Compare, if you like, the work of **Thomas Willis**, an English physician, in his *Treatise on Fermentation*, specifically regarding artificial processes, chapter 6, in its entirety.

-># TOPIC 15: ON THE HOLY LORD’S SUPPER<-

...-ce to do. To this, however, we respond more correctly that they do not act rightly, since the Sacraments cannot be changed at will by the one *administering* or *using* them, but can only be arranged and ordered by **GOD**, the sole *institutor*. Not to mention that this "necessity" is not so great that the rule of Christ—"drink the wine" <note>original Greek: πίετε τὸν οἶνον</note> and specifically "the fruit of the vine" <note>original Greek: γέννημα τῆς ἀμπέλου</note>—should in any way suffer an exception here. They could procure wine either from other parts of Africa or from our own Europe, especially in our own time, when Europeans themselves frequently undertake voyages to their lands. Or, if we were to imagine that every opportunity for obtaining wine were lacking to them, <margin>λ)</margin> we nevertheless think it would be better for them to abstain from the administration of the Holy Lord's Supper itself than—

<margin>λ) Indeed, even if they were deprived of this opportunity, they could still prepare wine themselves. If they were to prepare somewhat deeper caves or wine cellars in the very high mountains they possess, or at least store their wine in those very high temples they have carved into the rock, there is no doubt that the **must** <gloss>original: *mustum*; unfermented grape juice</gloss> could be clarified in the temperate air. The ancient Ethiopians perhaps used this method, since **Lucan** himself testifies in his *History*, Book 10, that the finest wine was once prepared in the city of **Meroë**, the capital of Ethiopia.</margin>

...to do. To which, however, we more correctly respond that they do not act correctly, since the Sacraments cannot be changed at will by the *one administering* or *using* them, but can only be arranged and ordained by God alone, the *institutor*. 

Not to mention that this necessity is not so great that Christ’s rule—"drink the wine" <note>original Greek: πίετε τὸν οἶνον</note>, and specifically the "fruit of the vine" <note>original Greek: γέννημα τῆς ἀμπέλου</note>—could in any way allow for an exception here. For wine could be procured by them either from elsewhere in Africa or even from our own Europe, especially in our own time, when Europeans themselves frequently make voyages to their lands. ---

λ) Indeed, even if they were deprived of this opportunity, they could still prepare wine themselves. If they only prepared wine cellars or caverns somewhat deeper in the very high mountains they possess, or at least placed their wine in the very high temples they have excavated in the cliffs, there is no doubt that the young juice <term>mustum: unfermented grape juice</term> could be clarified <note>original Latin: defaecari; refers to the settling of sediment during fermentation</note> in the tempered air. Perhaps the ancient Ethiopians used this method, since **LUCAN** himself testifies in his *History, Book 10* that excellent wine was once prepared in the city of Meroë, the capital of Ethiopia.

->### TOPIC 15: ON THE HOLY LORD’S SUPPER<-

-sion, we cannot fail to also bring forward a few points concerning the other part of the earthly material—namely, the bread—from the practice of the Abyssinians, so that it may be clear what they believe and do in this regard. Therefore, they use bread made from wheat, or *sərnay* [ስርናይ], ξ) baked or cooked over fire on that very day when the Holy Lord’s Supper is administered...

In this regard, it is also indeed surprising that the Roman Pope just mentioned [Julius III], in the decree *On Consecration* <term>De Consecratione; a specific section of Canon Law</term>, Distinction II, Canon *Cum omne crimen*, disapproved of the methods of the Copts in Egypt and the Christians of St. Thomas in India (who squeeze wine out of a cloth soaked in wine), yet he himself permitted, in a case of necessity, a cluster of grapes to be squeezed into the chalice and mixed with water. This is found in the cited Canon, whose words sound as follows: *But if it be necessary, let the cluster be squeezed into the chalice, and water be mixed in.* <note>original Latin: *Sed si necesse sit, Botrus in calice comprimatur, et aqua misceatur*</note> Pope Innocent VIII granted this same concession in the 15th century to the Norwegians, who were using unfermented grape juice <term>mustum; young, unfermented wine</term> instead of wine in the Holy Supper. This is attested by Volaterranus <note>Raffaele Maffei (1451–1522), a humanist scholar and geographer</note> in Book VII of his *Geography*, and cited from him by the late Christoph Richter (formerly a minister of the church at Gukmire in the County of Vas <note>Castrum Ferreum</note> in Lower Hungary) in his *Disputation on the Lord’s Supper held at Wittenberg* under the presidency of the great Abraham Calovius <note>A leading 17th-century Lutheran theologian</note> on Thursday <note>indicated by the astrological symbol for Jupiter, ♃</note>, 1673, second problem, section 6.

ξ) <margin>This name [sərnay] does not have a recognized root among the Abyssinians, nor, as far as we know, is the root *sh-r-n* [שָׁרָן] known among the Hebrews. However, it seems to us that it might not be incorrectly derived from the region of Sharon [שָׁרוֹן], which was otherwise very fertile, as mentioned in 1 Chronicles 5:16 and 27:29, among other places.</margin>

...they are accustomed to prepare the dough, ο) which they mark with a cross-shaped pattern using a specific instrument made for that very purpose. This bread is usually leavened, but on Maundy Thursday <note>original: *feria quinta magnae hebdomadis*, the Thursday of Holy Week</note>, in memory of the unleavened bread <note>original Greek: ἀζύμων, *azymōn*</note> of Christ, they use unleavened bread, π) pieces of which the Priest himself distributes with his hands to the communicants, much as is usually the practice among the *Reformed* <note>The author refers to the Protestant Reformed tradition where the minister places the bread in the hands of the faithful, rather than placing a wafer on the tongue as in the Roman Catholic tradition of the time</note>...
<meta>catchword: ap-</meta>

> ...of which mention is made, so named because perhaps the most precious and best [wheat] was produced there, just as we see that this name was given to the best wine; for this reason, the Jews called the best wine *Sharonite Wine* <note>original Hebrew: יין השרוני, *yayin ha-sharonit*</note>, to which, because of its excellence, they mixed two parts water, as is found in the Talmudic tractates *Shabbos* folio 77 and *Nidda* folio 19. The Ethiopians indeed—or at least many of them—recognize these canons as true and genuine, perhaps deceived by the inscription, which reads as follows: *These are the Canons of the Apostolic Fathers, which they established for the ordering of the Christian Church.* Meanwhile, however, it is surprising that they do not venerate them with the same religious devotion as the other Books of the Apostles, but regard them only as deuterocanonical <term>deuterocanonical: books or texts considered to be of secondary authority, or added to the canon later than the primary scriptures</term>. For this reason, they do not include them in the Order of the Books of the New Testament, but only place them in the first position among the Eight Books of the various Canons of the Councils. Although, even this should not have been believed or granted. 

But what wonder is it that the Ethiopians err somewhat here, when even the otherwise Great Theologian of his time, **JOHN DAMASCENE** <note>A prominent 8th-century monk and scholar known for "The Orthodox Faith"</note>, in *On the Orthodox Faith, Book IV, chapter 18*, joined these canons to the fourteen Epistles of Paul and the Revelation of John, writing: *Fourteen epistles of Paul the Apostle, the Revelation of John the Evangelist, the canons of the holy Apostles through Clement.* <note>original Greek: Πάυλου Ἀποστόλου ἐπιστολαὶ δεκατέσσαρες, Ἀποκάλυψις Ιωάννου Εὐαγγελιστοῦ, κάνoνες τῶν ἁγίων Ἀποστόλων διὰ Κλήμεντος</note> 

But how much both lean on a "fig-tree" argument <note>original Latin: *ficulneo argumento*; a classical idiom referring to something brittle, weak, or unreliable, much like the wood of a fig tree</note>, has been shown by the most learned of the Pontifical <note>Roman Catholic</note> Doctors, such as **Albaspinaeus**, Bishop of Orléans; **Gratian** in *Distinction XVI, Canon I*; and the Lord **Pope Gelasius**, in the Council held at Rome with seventy Bishops, chapter 3, distinction 15; and others. # TOPIC XV: ON THE HOLY SUPPER OF THE LORD

Although, to anyone scrutinizing the inner workings of Ethiopian matters, they might seem to lean toward the other extreme, and thus incline toward excess along with the Latins <note>The Roman Catholic Church</note>, and at least many of them seem to establish transubstantiation <note>The doctrine that the bread and wine literally turn into the substance of Christ's body and blood</note>. This is primarily because in their own Eucharistic Liturgies they frequently use the words ገብረ: *Gabara*, "To make," ሠራዕ: *Sara’a*, "To prepare," ኰነ: *Kona*, "To become," መየጠ: *Mayata*, "To convert," and ወተወለጠ: *Watawalata*, "To be changed." However, they seem to us, in the truth of the matter, to think more correctly than they speak; and thus the same thing has happened to them as happened to some of the early Church Fathers who wrote before the controversies <meta>catchword: con-</meta> arose.

And especially in England, they seem to approach very closely to our opinion <note>The Lutheran view</note>, when—as the celebrated **SEBASTIAN EDZARD** <note>A prominent Lutheran theologian from Hamburg (1672–1736) who defended the Lutheran view of the Eucharist</note> reports in his *Dissertation on Fundamental Dissent*, pages 28 and following—the Priest in the English Liturgy, when proffering the bread and wine, uses this form: *The Body of our Lord Jesus Christ which was given for thee, preserve thy Body and Soul unto everlasting life.* That is: *The Body of our Lord Jesus Christ, which was handed over for you, keep your body and soul unto eternal life,* and they say the same in the administration of the chalice. But we would wish that, to avoid all suspicion, they would use not the prepositive article *The*, but rather the demonstrative pronoun *This* (*Hoc*), just as our best Savior said: *This is my Body, Matthew 26:26, etc.* <note>original Greek: "τοῦτό ἐστι τὸ Σῶμά μου"; Lutherans emphasized the word "This" to insist on the "Real Presence" of Christ's body in the specific bread held by the minister.</note>

# TOPIC XV: ON THE HOLY SUPPER OF THE LORD

...they spoke more freely regarding the controversies, but in truth, through such terms they understood nothing except a **moral change** <term>moral change: A shift in the use, purpose, or spiritual significance of an object (making it holy) rather than a change in its physical essence or "substance."</term> of the bread, by which means, from common bread and wine, they became the true vehicles of the body and blood of Christ. $\psi$) <note>This symbol refers to the supporting citation from the Church Fathers at the bottom of the page.</note> For in their own time, they did not foresee that any Christian would allow himself to be carried away to the point of asserting that the bread—the essence of which remains intact before, during, and after consecration—is changed and transformed into the Body; or that the wine, while its own properties and qualities remain intact, is changed into the very blood of Christ. 

In fact, it is apparent from this that the *Abyssinians* <note>Ethiopian Christians</note> agree with us on this point: for if these simple people, who are untrained in such controversies, wished to establish such a **transubstantiation** <note>The doctrine that the bread and wine literally become the physical substance of Christ's body and blood, leaving only the "accidents" or appearance of bread and wine.</note>, they would necessarily have to treat the consecrated bread and wine with a specific sacred and divine worship <note>Known as the Adoration of the Host</note>, which is an absolutely necessary and inseparable consequence of admitting transubstantiation. However, you will find none of these things among them, to such an extent that not even a greeting...

---

$\psi$) We could illustrate this matter with the examples of **John of Damascus**, **Cyril**, and **Augustine**, but it shall suffice to bring forward only **JUSTIN MARTYR** <note>An early Christian apologist (c. 100–165 AD) often cited to show how the early church understood the Eucharist before medieval dogmas were defined.</note> speaking on this matter in his *Apology*, section 118: <note>original Greek: "γὰρ ὡς κοινὸν ἄρτον οὐδὲ κοινὸν πόμα ταῦτα λαμβάνομεν, ἀλλὰ ὃν τρό-"</note> "For we do not receive these as common bread or a common drink, but in the [same] man-..." <note>The Greek text cuts off mid-word; the catchword "pon" completes "tropon," meaning "manner" or "way."</note>

# TOPIC XV: ON THE HOLY SUPPER OF THE LORD

...attribute salvation to the bread and wine, as they occasionally do to other created things; much less will you read anywhere, so far as we know, of a formal adoration of them. This very point can be proven by a most elegant example involving **GREGORY OF AMHARA** <note>An Ethiopian monk (1595–1658) who was the primary source of information for the scholar Job Ludolf regarding Ethiopian culture and theology.</note>.
<margin>χ) *In History of Ethiopia, Book III, chapter 5, § 48, and following.*</margin>
For when he was asked by the blessed **JOB LUDOLF** χ) *What he thought about the real presence—*

-> For we do not receive these as common bread or common drink; but just as Jesus Christ our Savior, having been made flesh by the Word of God, had both flesh and blood for our salvation, so also we have been taught that the food which has been blessed by the prayer of the word that comes from Him—by which our blood and flesh are nourished through a **change** <note>original Greek: μεταβολὴν (*metabolēn*). The author highlights this to show it refers to how the food nourishes us, not necessarily a change in the bread's essence.</note>—is the flesh and blood of that Jesus who was made flesh. <-

These are his words, where he even employs the term **change** <note>original: τῆς μεταβολῆς (*tēs metabolēs*)</note>, which, since it denotes a transformation, seems to correspond to the Ethiopian word **was changed** <note>original Ge'ez: ወተወልጠ (*wa-tawalṭa*)</note>. But who does not see that he is speaking of a change that is *not Physical and Essential*, but rather *moral and accidental*? <term>Moral and accidental change: In theology, this refers to a change in the use, status, or spiritual power of an object (like bread becoming a sacrament) without its physical "substance" or "essence" being destroyed or replaced.</term> For if he had wished to imply the former, he would have used the term **transubstantiation** <note>original Greek: μετουσιώσεως (*metousiōseōs*), literally a "change of essence."</note> rather than simply "change," since the latter is also valid for a moral change, as is clear from *Acts 28:6* <note>In this Bible verse, the people of Malta "changed" their minds about Paul; it uses the same root word for change without implying a physical transformation of their bodies.</note> and other passages of Scripture. However, we do not deny that here the good Father <note>Referring to Justin Martyr, the 2nd-century Christian author being quoted.</note> spoke somewhat incautiously, as was common before the Great Controversies began; for the bread does not become the flesh of Christ in the same way that Christ became flesh, nor do we become flesh by eating the flesh of Christ.

### TOPIC XV: ON THE HOLY SUPPER OF THE LORD 147

[Gregorius] <note>The text begins mid-word, completing the name of Gregorius from the previous page.</note> himself explained his own opinion and that of his people; it is probable that he said nothing out of flattery or pretense, as he always remained so tenacious of the doctrine and rites of his fatherland that he did not even wish to die outside of his own country.

Just as the Copts <note>Egyptian Christians.</note> in the consecration of the Holy Supper use the word *Pteshebshentoy* <note>Original Greek transcription: ΠΤΕΨΨΠΣΝΤΟΙ. A term used in Coptic liturgies to denote change or transformation.</note>, so the Ethiopians use the word *Wamithu* <note>original Ge'ez: ወሚቱ (*Wamithu*), meaning "he turned" or "he changed."</note> and *Transfer*. As far as the cited Egyptian word is concerned, **KIRCHER** <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a famous Jesuit scholar and polymath.</note> has not yet proven that it is used uniquely for an essential change <note>That is, Transubstantiation, the physical change of substance.</note>, nor do we have time to inquire into it now. Furthermore, we oppose **KIRCHER** with a member of his own order, **TELLEZ** <note>Balthazar Telles (1596–1675), a Jesuit historian who wrote about Ethiopia.</note>, who asserts that the Ethiopians are so far removed from Transubstantiation that he even determines they do not have a true consecration. For he claims they are accustomed to say over the bread, *This cup is my blood.* *These words (in the judgment of Tellez) do not have the valid power of consecrating.* For the Doctors of the Roman Church are of the opinion that he who says such words accomplishes nothing, for only those words, *This is my body*, work true Transubstantiation. If these things are so, I rightly conclude against Kircher that no one can convict the Abyssinians <note>A historical term for Ethiopians.</note> of this error. Compare also **JOB LUDOLF** <note>A German scholar regarded as the founder of Ethiopian studies in Europe.</note> in the *History of Ethiopia*, Book III, chapter 5, number 56.

### TOPIC XV: ON THE HOLY SUPPER OF THE LORD 149

...to be administered; γ) that they believed and, in actual practice and continuous usage, have administered it, which is demonstrated even by the previously cited words—those which all are accustomed to proclaim with one accord <note>original Greek: ὁμοθυማδὸን (*homothymadon*)</note> after partaking of the Lord's banquet, as the Deacon leads the prayer,
K 3
<meta>catchword: are accustomed to,</meta>

---

γ) What need is there for many words on this matter, when no Church besides the Latin [Roman Catholic] one can be shown to have ever withheld the cup from the laity? To be sure, we are not unaware that as early as the 4th century after the birth of Christ, the foundations for this perverse custom were laid, when bread dipped in wine was offered to "clinics" <term>Those confined to bed by illness; from the Greek *kline*, "bed"</term> and those sick unto death, because, on account of the gravity of their illness, they were not strong enough to consume both species <term>The "species" refers to the individual elements of bread and wine</term> separately. This error seems to have arisen from a misunderstanding of Canon 12 and 15 of the Council of Nicaea, where the Holy Supper of the Lord is called the "most necessary provision for the journey" <note>original Greek: ἀναγκαιότατον ἐφόδιοን (*anankaiotaton ephodion*)</note> or "most necessary viaticum" <term>Provisions for a journey; here, the final Eucharist given to someone before death to prepare their soul for the journey to the afterlife</term>. From this, some concluded that this Sacrament was absolutely necessary for attaining salvation. This custom, however, was condemned both by Julius I, Pope of Rome (in *On Consecration*, Distinction II, Canon *Commune*), and by the Third Council of Braga (Canon 1). But these condemnations were in vain until, at the time of the Council of Constance <note>1414–1418; the council that officially banned the laity from receiving the wine</note>, the cup was completely snatched away from the laity. This was the cup which the Cardinal of Saint Angelo—according to the testimony of Pietro Soave <note>The pseudonym of Paolo Sarpi (1552–1623), a famous historian of the Reformation and the Council of Trent</note> in his *History of the Council of Trent*, Book 5, page 537—had called "a cup (I shudder to say it) filled with poison" <note>original Latin: *toxico refertum*</note>. This act was borne with great distress by all, particularly by the estates of the Kingdom of Bohemia <note>A reference to the Hussites, who fought for the right of the laity to receive the cup, a practice known as Utraquism</note>, who for that reason published a double Apology, and even by arms declared—
<meta>catchword: declared—</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the Ethiopian Church, like all non-Roman churches, has always provided both the bread and wine to the laity, tracing the Roman restriction of the cup back to early practices for the dying and the subsequent controversy in Bohemia.</summary>

### TOPIC XV: ON THE HOLY SUPPER OF THE LORD 150

...prevail. But so that no defense is lacking here either, we will provide the very words of **Basilides** <note>Also known as Fasilides (reigned 1632–1667), the Emperor of Ethiopia who expelled the Jesuits.</note>, King of the Ethiopians, in his letter given to **Alfonso Mendez**, the Patriarch, which sound as follows on this matter: *“That was not the reason why our people went to war with you (namely, that two natures are recognized in Christ...”* <note>The King is arguing that the bloody rebellion against the Jesuits was not merely over the theology of Christ's nature, but over the forced changes to their ancient religious customs, such as the use of the cup in the Eucharist.</note>

...they declared how much they abominate this “theft of the cup” <note>original Greek: καλικοκλεψίαν (*kalikoklepsian*)</note>, so much so that in the Hussite War <note>A 15th-century conflict in Bohemia sparked by the execution of Jan Hus; a central demand was "Utraquism," or receiving both bread and wine.</note> up to 100,000 men on one side perished, because it was a war started on account of the cup being taken away. Hence, as the celebrated **Wolffius** reports from Dubravius in *Memorable Lessons*, Century 15, page 781, the Hussites held a cup on their banner as a symbol. From this it is clear what cause compelled them to take up arms: namely, to assert the institution of their Savior, Jesus Christ. 

And it is certainly horrendous, adds the same author, that wretched Christians still have to fight and shed blood for that which Christ freely gave and commanded them. Likewise, the Abyssinians <note>A historical name for Ethiopians.</note> bore the attempted removal of the cup by the Fathers <note>Refers to the Jesuit missionaries.</note> with great difficulty; since they would not concede to it at all, the Fathers incurred hatred among them. Wherefore specifically **Father Tellez**, in Book 4, Chapter 12, page 334, inveighs against **Luis de Urreta** for representing, *“The Abyssinians as a quintessence of Catholics, whereas they are a primary substance of schismatics”* <note>original Portuguese: *Os Abexins huam quinta essencia de Catholicos, sendo elles huam primeira substancia de schismaticos*</note>. That is to say, Urreta viewed the Ethiopians as the very “fifth essence” <term>In ancient philosophy, the purest, most perfect element; a "quintessence"</term> of Catholics, while Tellez viewed them as the "prime matter" <term>The formless, basic substance from which things are made; here used to mean they are the fundamental source of rebellion</term> of schismatics. This judgment was certainly passed for the reason that they refused to abstain from the use of the cup.

### TOPIC XV: ON THE HOLY LORD’S SUPPER

...will be given to them, as they are convinced, from such frequent participation in it? Then indeed we shall say nothing besides the point if we respond that they do this very thing for a two-fold reason: namely, on one hand *for the remission of their sins,* and on the other hand also *for the confirmation of their faith.* For this reason, all those communicating are accustomed to pray that they may be made worthy and duly prepared guests of Christ, receiving this Sacrament not unto judgment, but unto salvation. Whence in the *Eucharistic Prayer of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ,* <note>The author refers to the Anaphora, the central prayer of the Ethiopian liturgy</note> which we discussed above, after they have recited the words of institution <note>The words spoken by Jesus at the Last Supper</note>, they confess that they approach these most holy feasts in the hope of obtaining the remission of sins. Indeed, the words flow in this way, which we will not find it a burden to include here along with the words of institution:

*On that night in which they betrayed him, he took bread into his holy, blessed, and immaculate hands, gave thanks, blessed, broke it, and gave it to his disciples, saying: Take, eat; this bread is my body, which is broken for you for the remission of sins, and when you do this, do it in remembrance of me. Likewise also the cup of wine, after they had supped, mixing it, giving thanks, blessing, and sanctifying it, you gave to them your true blood, which was poured out* FOR OUR SINS.

## ON THE CHURCH

# TOPIC XVI: ON THE CHURCH

[The Church] is called Christian, and also <note>original Ge'ez: መኅበረ፡ ፫ አስማት፡</note> *Mahabara Schalschat Asmaty,* ϛ) i.e. the *Assembly of the Three Names*. These people [the Ethiopians], along with the orthodox, teach, confess, and believe that there is truly given not only an invisible Church of the elect, but also a visible Church of the called, and that neither of the two can cease to exist in the kingdom of grace even for a moment. For concerning the invisible Church, which is known only to God the <term>searcher of hearts</term> <note>original Greek: καρδιογνώσῃ</note>, they declare their faith and mind as often as they recite the Nicene Creed, where they confess together with us that they believe in <note>original Ge'ez: አሐቲ፡ ቅድስት፡ ቤተ፡ ክርስቲያን፡ ዘኵሉ፡ ጉባኤ፡ እንተ፡ ክርስቲያናት፡</note> the *One Holy Catholic and Apostolic Church*. For the words of the Creed flow in their sequence as follows:

> And we believe in one holy Apostolic Church, which

just as he who is not inside a house is exposed to all storms, so he who is not established within the house of the Church is forced to perish eternally.

---

ϛ) This name is also used to distinguish the Church in the *Ethiopic Organon, Lesson III* <note>The *Organon Maryam* or *Organ of Mary*, a prominent collection of Ethiopian hymns and prayers</note>; it is an elegant title of praise, by which it is indicated that the Church belongs to God the Father through calling, and to His Son through His mission. To God the Son through redemption, and to God the Holy Spirit through sanctification and confirmation in the faith. Nevertheless, to avoid the mark of <term>Sabellianism</term> <note>A historical heresy that claimed the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit were merely different "names" or "modes" of one person rather than three distinct persons</note>, it would be more correct to say "of the three *Persons*."

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Topic XVI (On the Church), further explaining the distinction between the visible and invisible Church. # TOPIC XVI: ON THE CHURCH
<meta>catchword: hoe- (this)</meta>

is above all congregations. γ) By this reasoning they very clearly distinguish the invisible congregation of *Believers*, which must be believed and cannot be seen or touched, from the visible congregation, which is gathered for GOD through the Word and Sacraments. This visible Church consists of both good and bad men, of the pious and hypocrites, of believers and unbelievers, or at least of <note>original Greek: προσκαιροῖς</note> *temporary followers* <note>Those whose faith is only for a season, especially in times of prosperity</note>. It is like a *field* bearing wheat as well as weeds <note>original Latin: zizaniam; often translated as "tares" or "darnel"</note>, *Matthew 13:24*; like a *threshing floor* <note>original: Areae</note> holding both wheat and chaff, *Matthew 3:12*; like a *net* containing good fish as well as rotten ones, *Matthew 13:47*; like a *household* rejoicing in both lazy and faithful servants, *Matthew 25:45*; like a *garden* nourishing sterile as well as fruitful trees, *Isaiah 67:3, Psalm 1:3, Matthew 3:10, Luke 13:7*; and like a *sheepfold*, containing sheep...

---

γ) They also seem to have drawn this doctrine not only from the Holy Scriptures—which frequently distinguish between the *militant and the triumphant* Church, as is clear from *Revelation 2:26–27, 4:4, 7, 9* and *Hebrews 12:22*, compared with *Ephesians 6:10–12* and *1 Peter 5:8–9*—but also from the very <term>Canons of the Apostles</term> <note>A collection of ancient ecclesiastical decrees once attributed to the Apostles, held in high regard by the Ethiopian and Eastern churches</note>, which they hold in high regard. There, at the very beginning and as if in a preface, they say that they received a command from Christ: *To divide the world among themselves, so that each of them might receive his own place according to their number, and ordain wholesome institutions.* Upon these (namely, the statutes) it is fitting to establish the foundations of the Christian Church—behold the <term>Church Militant</term> <note>The Church on earth, currently "at war" against sin and the devil</note>—and so that they might act in this same Church just as it is done in the heavens—behold the <term>Church Triumphant</term> <note>The Church in heaven, having achieved victory and eternal peace</note>.

<note>Vocabulary: Church, invisible congregation, visible congregation, church militant, church triumphant, Canons of the Apostles</note>

...and embracing the goats, <note>Original Greek letter: δ</note> by which reasoning they are far more wisely discerning than either the ancient *Novatians* <note>Original Greek letter: ε</note> and *Cathars* <note>Original Greek letter: ς</note> or even our own [adversaries]...

δ) *John 10:1 ff. For now, the Church is visibly gathered from the number of both, but is invisibly discerned by God the judge.”* <note>original: "A bonis malisque haec uita communiter dicitur. Nunc Ecclesia ex utrorumque numero uisibiliter congregatur, sed DEO inuisibiliter iudicante discernitur."</note>

ε) The *Novatians* arose from Novatus, an African priest. <note>The author likely conflates Novatus of Carthage and Novatian of Rome, a common occurrence in older histories.</note> Among other monstrous opinions which they fostered, they also fought for this one: *“That the Church, even in these lands, possesses only pure and uncontaminated members.”* From this <term>primary error</term> <note>original Greek: πρῶτῳ ψεύδει</note> flowed another: *“that for those who have once lapsed, no further grace is given to return again into the covenant entered with God.”* <note>The "lapsed" were Christians who renounced their faith under Roman persecution; the Novatians refused to readmit them to the Church.</note> More on these people is provided from the Church Fathers by Augustine in *On Heresies, 38*, and from our own writers, among others, the blessed Lucas Osiander in his *Third Century [of Church History], folio 65.*

ς) The *Cathars* <note>From the Greek word for "Pure."</note> are indeed the very same as the Novatians, but they mixed in several other errors and heresies. Thus, under this name, the *Meletians* (arising from Meletius the Egyptian, Bishop in the Thebaid, around 320 AD), the *Donatists* (arising in the 4th Century from Donatus the African, around 332 AD), and other men of this same "brand" are not undeservedly included. They were also called *Gazari* by the unlearned—perhaps more correctly than *Cathars*, or "The Pure," since that name did not belong to them except <term>by way of irony</term>. <note>original Greek: κατ' ἀντίφρασιν; literally "by antiphrasis," a rhetorical device where a word is used to mean its opposite.</note>

<summary>This page argues against the "Novatian" and "Cathar" view that the earthly Church consists only of the pure, citing Gregory the Great to support the idea that the Church is currently a mixed body of good and bad people.</summary>

...stroyed, but for which *there shall be no end* <note>original Ge’ez: አልቦ፡ ማሕለቅት፡ (*albo mahlaqt*); original Latin: *non erit finis*</note>. From these points, we hope it can be established for everyone that the *Abyssinians* <note>A historical term for the people of the Ethiopian Empire.</note> neither relegate the initial blessedness of eternal life to this current life, nor do they embrace or defend Chiliasm <term>Chiliasm: From the Greek word for "one thousand," the belief that Christ will return to rule a physical, earthly kingdom for 1,000 years before the final judgment.</term>, as they are entirely ignorant of it. But indeed, just as by this reasoning the *Abyssinians* avoid the Scylla <note>Scylla and Charybdis are monsters from Greek mythology representing two equal dangers on either side of a narrow passage. Here, the author uses them to describe avoiding one theological error (Perfectionism) without falling into another (Perpetual Doubt).</note> of *Perfectionism* <term>Perfectionism: The theological claim that humans can reach a state of sinless perfection during their earthly life.</term>, they also carefully guard against falling into or striking against the Charybdis and whirlpools of perpetual doubt concerning their salvation. On this point, they concede that a justified person is not only able to be certain of their salvation, but they themselves possess full [assurance]... <note>The text here breaks off with the Greek prefix πλη- (*plē-*), likely referring to *plerophoria*, meaning "full assurance" or "certainty" of faith.</note>

> [He] was [crucified], and rose from the dead on the third day, just as it is written in Holy Scripture. He ascended with glory into the heavens, and sits at the right hand of his Father. He will come again in glory to judge the living and the dead, and there will be no end to his kingdom. And we believe in the Holy Spirit, the Lord and Giver of Life, who proceeded from the Father. We worship him, we glorify him with the Father and the Son, who spoke through the Prophets. And we believe in one holy Apostolic Church, which is above all congregations. And we believe in one Baptism for the remission of sins, and we hope for the resurrection of the dead, and the life which is to come forever. Amen.

If these words are compared with the Nicene Creed <note>The foundational statement of Christian belief adopted in 325 AD and expanded in 381 AD; it is shared by almost all Christian denominations.</note>, it will be apparent that it is exactly the same, with only a few words changed.

# TOPIC XVI: ON THE CHURCH

...embracing the **full assurance** <note>original Greek: πληροφορίαν (*plērophorian*)</note> of faith, they believe firmly and without doubt that they and all the faithful, as such, are blessed—namely in hope, and indeed truly, no less than in reality, if you look at the blessedness of this life in hope. μ) From this doctrine it has come about that even their King **ZERA-IAACOB** <note>Zera Yaqob (reigned 1434–1468), a significant Ethiopian Emperor known for his deep theological interests and reforms.</note> [addressed] the Monks living at that time in Jerusalem,

---

μ) Here, these phrases must be properly distinguished: "I have a hope of being saved," and "I am saved, but in hope." The former implies mere potential—either remote, as among those who are unregenerate *negatively* <note>Those who have not yet heard the Gospel.</note>, or *proximate*, as among those who are unregenerate *privatively* <note>Those who have heard the Gospel but have not yet accepted it.</note>. All such hope does not yet deserve to be called **full assurance and confidence** <note>original Greek: πληροφορία καὶ πεποίθησις (*plērophoria kai pepoithēsis*)</note>. 

But, if I say, "I am saved, but in hope," I mean that I have indeed already received all the rights and, as it were, the certificates of the kingdom of heaven, and that there is no condemnation in me, because I am in Christ Jesus through faith, and Christ is in me through grace and **mystic union** <term>Mystic union: A theological concept describing the spiritual oneness between a believer and Jesus Christ.</term>. Meanwhile, however, it does not follow that I perceive the beginning of actual heavenly blessedness, since the *blessedness of the kingdom of grace* <note>The spiritual state of a believer on earth.</note> is one thing, and that of the *kingdom of glory* <note>The state of the blessed in heaven.</note> is another. 

Furthermore, someone can say that they are blessed *in reality* <note>original Latin: *in re*</note>, because it is true, but not in such a way that all hope is entirely excluded. For example, a son who is a minor, designated as the heir of a kingdom, is truly such, and thus in reality, but also in hope. For, if like **Rehoboam** <note>The son of Solomon whose harsh rule caused the kingdom of Israel to split; see 1 Kings 12.</note> he wishes to oppress his subjects, he does not attain the keys of the kingdom; so too one might not wrongly say the same thing happens here.

<summary>This page discusses the Ethiopian theological concept of "assurance of faith," arguing that a believer can be certain of their salvation "in hope" while still on earth. It distinguishes this certain hope from a mere possibility of salvation using legal and monarchical analogies.</summary>

**Note Well.** *Blessed are we! who have seen the mystery of your Son, and have been made partakers of the offering of his body and blood upon the holy table. And from these things it is already somewhat clear what the Abyssinians <note>Abyssinians: A historical term for Ethiopians.</note> also feel and teach correctly in this article.* <note>original Latin: "Beatos! qui uidimus mysterium Filii tui... Habessyni in hoc quoque articulo fentiant doceantque recte."</note>

-># TOPIC XVII<-
->## ON THE<-
-># POWER OF THE KEYS<-

To the Church of GOD, which we have considered up to this point, the thrice-best and greatest GOD has granted the *Keys of the kingdom of heaven*. The Ethiopian would say, መራኁተ፡ ሰማያት፡ $\alpha$) *Marahuty Samájaty* <note>original Ethiopic: "The Keys of Heaven"</note>, or, the *Power of forgiving and retaining sins*, which the Ethiopians also—

---

$\alpha$) Indeed, this name—as we do not deny—we have not yet found accepted in this same sense in the books of the Abyssinians encountered by us thus far; rather, the phrase ሥልጣነ፡ ይስረይ፡ ኀጢአት፡ <note>original Ethiopic: "shiltane yisrey hati'at"</note> i.e., *The authority to forgive sins*, is familiar and customary to them, as will become clear from the passage to be cited shortly. The wretched sinner, thinking he must obey, openly confesses to cattle-rustling <term>cattle-rustling: The original Latin *abigeatum* refers specifically to the theft of livestock, a serious crime in agrarian societies.</term>. By chance, the owner of the cattle was present; rejoicing that he had caught the thief, he immediately summoned the defendant to court. δ) It is fitting to include the words of Saint Lactantius <note>Lactantius (c. 250–325 AD) was an early Christian author and advisor to Constantine the Great, known as the "Christian Cicero" for his elegant style.</note> on this matter, speaking thus of the superstitious who try to defend their religion with arms: *“Why then,”* he says, *“do they rage, and while they wish to diminish their folly, they increase it? For they feel that nothing in human affairs is more excellent than religion, but just as in religion itself, so they are deceived in the manner of its defense. For religion is to be defended not by killing but by dying, not by cruelty but by patience, not by crime but by [faith]...* <note>The note ends mid-sentence; the full thought from Lactantius's *Divine Institutes* concludes that religion is defended by faith, not by the sword.</note>

# TOPIC XVII: ON THE POWER OF THE KEYS

...called those devoted to the [Alexandrian] Church, *they would dare to follow the religion or dispute concerning it. When this happened, the King was indeed deeply offended by this audacity of the Metropolitan <note>A high-ranking bishop with authority over a province; in this context, the head of the Ethiopian Church.</note>, yet he did not dare to punish it. Instead, he only issued another edict granting everyone the freedom to embrace the religion of the Fathers—now established through disputes—but the undaunted Metropolitan once again struck everyone who asserted two natures in Christ <note>This refers to the "Chalcedonian" belief that Christ has both a divine and human nature, which the Ethiopian Church rejected in favor of Miaphysitism (one nature).</note> with a new anathema <note>A formal curse or decree of excommunication that excludes a person from the Christian community.</note>.*

A similar example of excommunication, though used less appropriately, occurred at the same time in Ethiopia. For since the nobles sided with the Metropolitan and abandoned the King, Zadenghel <note>Emperor Za Dengel of Ethiopia (reigned 1603–1604), who attempted to convert to Catholicism, leading to his downfall.</note> planned to overwhelm them while they were off guard. However, ABUNA PETROS, the Alexandrian Metropolitan and the chief instrument of the rebellion, in an example unheard of in Ethiopia and contrary to both divine and human laws, released the rebels from their oath of allegiance made to their legitimate King through a detestable excommunication. x) With these two examples and a third...

---

x) From this single example, the abuse of excommunication is evident, as is the frequency with which the Patriarchs of Alexandria <note>The heads of the Coptic Church in Egypt, who historically appointed the bishops for Ethiopia.</note> have been moved by a lofty spirit and pride when they sought supreme power and license over sovereigns. Their pride also seems to have been the cause of that most terrible desolation... <meta>catchword: made by the Turks (continues in the insert on the next page)</meta>

### TOPIC XVII: ON THE POWER OF THE KEYS

To these, in our own time, there seem to have been added from the <term>legal experts</term> <note>original: *ICtis*, an abbreviation for *Iurisconsultis*, meaning jurists or legal scholars</note> a most celebrated pair: one from the Academy of Halle, **Christian Thomasius** in his **Thoughts on the Question of How Far a Preacher, etc. p. 38**, the other from the Academy of Leipzig, **Gottlieb Gerhard Titius**. Their doubts and arguments were refuted long ago by **Theodore Beza** and **John Brown**, and within our own memory by the late Doctor **Johannes Fecht** in his *Treatise on Excommunication*, by **C. Beerensprung** **in the Lawful Exclusion of the Openly Unrepentant Sinner from the Confessional, 1705**, and by **Christoph Matthäus Pfaff** in his *Treatise on the Origin of Ecclesiastical Law* p. 231 and following.

For if, in order to remove the abuse of the best things, the things themselves had to be removed, then surely men ought not to use the sun, nor the world, nor the Word of God—no, not even the most holy name of God himself (though no Christian would even think of such a thing)—since all these things are also exposed to manifold and detestable abuses at times. Therefore, let that license of <term>Cato the Censor</term> <note>Marcus Porcius Cato (234–149 BC), a Roman statesman famous for his strict morality and desire to banish any foreign influence he deemed corrupting</note> to banish even good things because of their abuse be dismissed, since here this principle should rather prevail: *Let the abuse perish, let the use remain.* And: *Abuse does not abolish use.* Would that in our own age...

Nevertheless, when necessity demands that such things be done, then even here the Medical Canon <note>a fundamental rule of medicine</note>—understood theologically—will hold true: ***It must be cut away with the sword, lest the healthy part be drawn in*** <note>original Latin: *Enfe recidendum, ne pars fincera trahatur*. This is a classical proverb, famously used by the poet Ovid, suggesting that a diseased limb must be amputated to save the rest of the body</note>. In this same sense, the best Savior himself orders the offender to be warned once and then a second time, and finally, if he has not listened to the Church, to be removed, in the words: **let him be to you as a Gentile and a tax collector** <note>original Greek: ἔστωσοι ὥσπερ ὁ ἐθνικὸς καὶ ὁ τελώνης</note>, *Matthew 18:17.*

The words of the late **Johann Karl Naevius** therefore please us; in his book *On the Law of Clerics*, after he cited the *Electoral Institutes on Excommunication*, he concludes with these words: **"From this it appears,"** he says, **"that the Ban <note>Excommunication</note> has been mostly retained by the Church for fear of contagion, so that the other members might not be infected and offended, etc."** <note>original German: "Daraus erscheint... daß der Bann größtentheils bey der Kirchen ob metum contagii behalten, damit die andern Glieder nicht angesteckt und geärgert werden möchten."</note>

### TOPIC XVII: ON THE POWER OF THE KEYS

here, let us examine this matter from the perspective of the Ethiopians. In this regard, if we investigate everything with sufficient diligence, we will finally be forced to admit that this particular controversy has not yet become known to them. This is because no one has been found among them who has departed from the truth of orthodox teaching <note>original Greek: *orthodidaskalias*, meaning "right teaching" or "orthodoxy"</note> which our Church has defended up to this point.

This is evident even from the *Formula for the Ordination of a Bishop*, where they pray as follows: <margin>$\mu$) This is found among the Apostolic Canons, as they are commonly called by the Abyssinians, number 21, titled: *On the Ordination of Bishops and the Rite of the Eucharist*.</margin> *“Almighty GOD, Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, our Savior: Grant, O Knower of hearts, O Father, that your servant, whom you have chosen for the Episcopate, may feed your flock and perform the priesthood before you without blame; that, ministering night and day, he may pray suppliantly and see your face, so that he may worthily offer your oblation”*—for this is what they call the **Sacred Supper** <note>Also known as the Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note>—*“of your holy Church, and in the holy Spirit of the priesthood, having* **NB.** <note>Latin *Nota Bene*: "Note well" or "Pay attention."</note> *the faculty of remitting sins according to your command, and of giving orders* <note>The power to ordain other clergy.</note> *according to your institution, and* **NB.** *of loosening every bond of iniquity according to the power which you gave to your Apostles, so that he may be acceptable to you in sincerity and a pure heart, etc.”* From which things...

->TOPIC XVIII: ON THE ECCLESIASTICAL MINISTRY<-

## Topic XVIII
### ON THE
# ECCLESIASTICAL
# MINISTRY

This Power of the Keys, which we have examined up to this point, was indeed granted by Christ to the whole Church; yet it cannot and should not be usurped by individual members of the Church in a public capacity. Rather, it must be entrusted only to specific persons, called by the common vote of the Church, who constitute the ordinary *Ministry* of GOD. This is what the divine Apostle to the Gentiles <note>St. Paul</note> calls the "ministry of the spirit" <note>Original Greek: διακονίαν τοῦ πνεύματος (*diakonian tou pneumatos*)</note> α), while the *Abyssinians* <note>An older term for Ethiopians.</note> call it *Malykyty* <note>Ethiopic: መልእክት (*mal'ekt*), meaning a mission, message, or ministry</note>. β) This very [ministry]...

> ...as an ornament, the opinion of the late and distinguished Mr. MARTIN CHLADENIUS <note>Martin Chladenius (1669–1725) was a prominent Lutheran theologian who specialized in the "Power of the Keys."</note>, who in his *Dissertation on the Nature of Evangelical Absolution* shows that a middle—and therefore the safest—position is defended by us here. He states this position consists in the fact that ministers of the word indeed enjoy a *real and effective influence in the remission of sins, and thus their absolution is exhibitive and collative* <note>Technical theological terms meaning the absolution doesn't just "announce" forgiveness, but actually "presents" and "bestows" it to the believer.</note>, *yet in such a way that the consideration of them as an instrument does not overthrow the power, but establishes it.*

α) In 2 Corinthians 3:8, and Acts 6:4, it is called the "ministry of the word" <note>Original Greek: διακονία τοῦ λόγου (*diakonia tou logou*)</note>; in Philippians 2:17, however, the "service of faith" <note>Original Greek: λειτουργία τῆς πίστεως (*leitourgia tēs pisteōs*)</note>, etc.
β) This name descends from the root *la'aka* <note>Ethiopic: ለአከ</note>, which [means]... <meta>The definition of the root verb "to send" continues on the next page.</meta>

Nevertheless, this very *Ministry* is carried out through diverse offices and various people, since GOD himself appointed *some to be apostles, some prophets, some evangelists, and some pastors and teachers, for the equipping of the saints, for the work of the ministry, and for the building up of the body of Christ* <note>Original Greek: Ephesians 4:11, 12</note>. *To delegate, to send*, and likewise *to serve*, [this root] denotes; by which means the duty of the ministers of the Church is elegantly sketched. It is necessary for them to be α) sent by GOD, or lawfully called, and β) diligent in their office, and always ready to care for souls. This root corresponds with the Hebrew *halak* <note>Original: הלך, meaning "to go" or "to walk"</note> and the Arabic *kharaja* <note>Original: خرج, usually meaning "to go out" or "to emerge"</note>, which also denotes *to send*.

# TOPIC 18: ON THE ECCLESIASTICAL MINISTRY

...tropolitan <note>Continuing from "Metropolitan" on the previous page</note>, who is also commonly called *Abuna* <note>Ethiopic: አቡነ; original Latin: *Abuna*</note>, δ) that is, **Our Father**. By ancient custom, and at the request of the King, he is consecrated to that office by the Patriarch of Alexandria and sent from Egypt to Ethiopia. *For they do not believe it is permitted for them—as **Ludolf** <note>Hiob Ludolf (1624–1704), a German scholar who founded Ethiopian studies in Europe</note> observes—to appoint someone from their own nation who is familiar with the language, laws, and customs of the country for the Patriarch to ordain.* Next in rank to him is the *Komosaty* <note>Ethiopic: ቆሞሳት; a title for a high-ranking cleric or archpriest</note>, ε) who is of the Ba-
->silica<-

***

δ) [The Patriarch of] **Alexandria**, as can be seen from the *Liturgy*, page 160, chapters 3 and 4, and page 163, chapter 2, and following.

This name *Abuna* <note>Ethiopic: አቡነ</note> coincides not only in its grammatical meaning with the Hebrew *Abuna* <note>Hebrew: אבונא; "Our Father"</note> and *Abba* or *Ab* <note>Hebrew: אבא or אב; "Father"</note>—from which almost all Eastern languages have borrowed it, as we have shown in the *Corollaries of our Harmony*—but also in terms of dignity. For the Jews name their Rabbis not only *Rabbi* <note>Hebrew: רבי; "My Master"</note> and *Mari* <note>Hebrew: מרי; "My Lord"</note>, but also *Abi* <note>Hebrew: אבי; "My Father"</note>; and just as *Rabbinu* <note>Hebrew: רבינו; "Our Master"</note> and *Abinu* <note>Hebrew: אבינו; "Our Father"</note> are used, so too *Abuna* is said. This argument was elegantly examined by the late **Ernest von Auerswald** in his *Dissertation on the Rabbinate* <note>Latin: *Dissertatio de Rabbinatu*</note>, which I myself defended as the Respondent <note>The student who publicly defended a thesis written by a professor</note>.

silica <note>Continuing from "Ba-silica" on the previous page</note>, or the **Provost of a Major Church**, whose duty it is to manage the secular affairs of the Churches and to settle the disputes of the Clergy, specifically to the extent that such jurisdiction is permitted to Clerics. The camp churches are presided over by the **Debhtera Gueta** <term>ደብተራ፡ ጌታ፡; literally 'Lord of the Tents' or 'Master of the Canons'</term>, or **Provost of the Canons**, who is followed by the **Nebraty** <term>ነብራት፡; an official 'set' or 'established' in authority</term>, 5) or their **Dean**. Regarding those exercising ecclesiastical jurisdiction, [it is said] that the next after these are the **Liqane Kahnat** <term>ሊቃነ፡ ካህናት፡; 'Chiefs of the Priests'</term>, that is, **High Priests**. This does not contradict our view; we have stated that they accompany the **Debhtera Gueta**, since the Provosts of the Canons are altogether below the **Qomosat** <term>ቆሞሳት፡; Archpriests</term> or the **Prefects of the Principal Churches**.

***

5) This name descends from the root **nabara** <term>ነበረ፡</term> which, in *Psalm 1:1*, signifies "to sit in judgment," and in *Matthew 5:15*, "to establish" or "to appoint." From this, **manbar** <term>መንበር፡</term> denotes a throne or seat (*Luke 1:12*), and likewise a tribunal or judgment seat (*Psalm 9:4*).

ζ) This name is also of Hebrew origin, where the word **kohen** <note>Hebrew: כהן</note>—as is well known—denotes a priest. It descends from an unused root **k-h-n** <note>Hebrew: כהן</note> which, in the *Piel* <note>The intensive verbal stem in Hebrew</note>, takes the form **kohen**, that is, "he acted as a priest."

the **Ethiopians** themselves also confess this, and they do not side with the **Anabaptists** or **Fanatics** <note>original: ζ) refers to the scholia at the bottom of the page</note>, who declare the civil magistrate to be a human, tyrannical institution that should be cast down from its throne. And this majesty, which Kings enjoy among mortals, even the supreme Rulers of the **Abyssinians** <note>original: *Habessynorum*, an older term for Ethiopians</note> themselves acknowledge; [they acknowledge] that they have acquired it not through the blood of their ancestors, nor through their own wisdom, virtue, or power, but that it was done by the providence and governance of the Supreme Deity, so that they were moved to the rudders of the Republics and Kingdoms subject to them. However, they are not admitted to the absolution of sinners or the administration of the sacraments <note>such as the Eucharist or Baptism</note>. Consult further on this subject the great theologian of Wittenberg, the **late GOTTLIEB WERNSDORF** in his *Dissertation on the Right of the Civil Magistrate Regarding the Calling of Church Ministers*, as well as the **late GEORG FRIEDRICH NIEHENCK** in his *Theological Dissertation on the Political Magistrate*, held in 1706.

# TOPIC XIX ON THE POLITICAL MAGISTRATE

...they might abuse [this power]. To this is referred the example of King **Susneyos** <note>Susenyos I, reigned 1606–1632; an Ethiopian Emperor who famously converted to Roman Catholicism, leading to significant civil and religious strife</note>, who, when he attempted to again allow by royal edict the Alexandrine rites <note>the traditional Coptic/Ethiopian Orthodox liturgical practices</note>—which had been abolished for the Ethiopians <note>original: *Habessynis*</note> by the public consensus of the Church—incurred the rebuke of **ALPHONSO MENDEZ**, the Patriarch appointed by the Supreme Pontiff <note>the Pope</note>. As **BALTHASAR TELLEZ** 5) reports in a *Letter given to Susneyos*, Mendez attacked the King's action with the following reasoning:

> Therefore, your Highness should have been mindful of the High Priest **AZARIAH** and his words to King **UZZIAH** <note>original: *Oziam*</note>. *It is not your office, O King* **UZZIAH!** *to burn incense to the Lord, but the office of the priests, that is, the sons of Aaron, who have been consecrated for this ministry. Depart from the sanctuary, lest you be disgraced,* $\zeta$) *because it shall not...*

5) *Book V, History of Ethiopia, chapter XXIX, page 482, column a.*
$\zeta$) *2 Chronicles XXVI, 18.* We find a parallel <note>original Greek: *ὁμώνυμον*</note> to this example also in Ecclesiastical History, where Saint Ambrose likewise ordered the Emperor **THEODOSIUS** to depart from the inner sanctuary, as we have already advised above. Now, however, it is our intention to present the same matter more clearly from **BARONIUS** <note>Cesare Baronio (1538–1607), a cardinal and historian known for his massive history of the Church</note>, who in his *Ecclesiastical Annals for the year 390, number 28,* relates the following:

### TOPIC XIX ON THE POLITICAL MAGISTRATE

...be counted to you for glory. Your Highness will also be able to recall, by the same means, the punishment which followed shortly after this King, etc.

<column-break/>

[he meant that the sanctuary] was inaccessible to all others and ought not to be touched. He [Ambrose] <note>Referring to St. Ambrose of Milan's famous confrontation with Emperor Theodosius I</note> therefore commands that he should leave and stand with the rest of the Laity. For the purple robe, he said, makes Emperors, not Priests. This admonition the most faithful Emperor likewise received willingly, and he replied that he had remained within the railings <note>original: "cancellos"; the screens separating the clergy in the chancel from the congregation</note> not induced by overconfidence of spirit, but because he had learned this custom in Constantinople <note>In the Eastern Church, Emperors often enjoyed the privilege of entering the sanctuary, a custom not permitted in the Western Church under Ambrose</note>. Thus he spoke. Where indeed the distinction between **Theodosius** and **Uzziah** lies in this: that the former [Theodosius] did not rashly approach the place of the Clergy to perform their duties, nor did he seek them out; but the latter [Uzziah] actually burned incense to the Lord. Yet such was the great mildness of that Emperor that, upon returning to Constantinople, even when bringing his gift into the sanctuary, he immediately withdrew—much to the indignation of Bishop **Nectarius** <note>Successor to Gregory Nazianzen as Archbishop of Constantinople</note>, as **Nicephorus** <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopoulos, a 14th-century church historian</note> and others observe.

## Topic XX
# ON MARRIAGE

Following the Ecclesiastical and Civil states comes the State of Marriage, or the Conjugal State, which is called *Sabsab* <note>original Ge'ez: ሰብሳብ</note> (*Sabyfaby*) by the Abyssinians, *a)* and is regarded as a state instituted by God himself. If, however, you wish to evaluate the customs commonly practiced in Abyssinia, you will be amazed that among these people—almost the only ones who bear the name of Christia—

---

*a)* This name descends from the Ethiopic <note>Also known as Ge'ez, the classical liturgical language of Ethiopia</note> root *wasaba* <note>original: ወሰበ</note>; which is not in common use, except for its derivative form, *awsaba* <note>original: አውሰበ</note>, which is "middle-voiced" <note>original Greek: μέσον; a grammatical category where the subject performs and is affected by the action</note>. When used for a man, it means *to take a wife*; conversely, for a virgin or widow, it means *to marry someone*, as is also clear from a comparison of the Ethiopic version of *Matthew 5:32*, and *22:24*, compared with *1 Corinthians 7:28*. In the first two passages, it is used for the man and is equivalent to the Greek *gameo* <note>original: γαμέω; to marry</note> and *epigambreuo* <note>original: ἐπιγαμβρεύω; to marry as next of kin or to fulfill a levirate marriage</note>, but especially to the word *gameo*, which is used for men and women alike.

δ) In the frequently praised *History*, Chapter 20.

### TOPIC XX: ON MARRIAGE

It would have been more fitting for him to instead water and preserve those things which were praiseworthy and established according to his own will and favor; for in no other way will the tenor and authority of the laws remain firm, or the vigor and prosperity of kingdoms flourish. But indeed, just as we have seen the Political Magistrate sin by *excess* until now, so, on the other hand, we find the Ecclesiastical Ministry failing somewhat in *neglect*. Since not even to the Clergy [is] Poly-

-gamy practiced among the Kings of the Ethiopians, because, along with other good customs, they drank in this error from no other source than from *Solomon*. For depraved morals and wicked customs creep along like hereditary diseases; rarely do you read that the sons of lustful parents have practiced chastity, whereas on the contrary it usually happens that chaste parents have nevertheless raised lustful sons, and gentle parents have raised cruel ones.

v) We cannot indeed deny that this passage <note>1 Timothy 3:2</note> is cited by them quite plausibly. For at the beginning occurs the word of command *must* <note>original Greek: *dei*</note>; furthermore, all those requirements which the Apostle [Paul] requires of a Bishop are stated as positive qualities, such that they truly ought to be present in a Bishop. From this, it would be easy to conclude that it is therefore necessary for a Bishop to have a wife, and specifically exactly one. But indeed, anyone who attends more accurately to the circumstances of time and place will easily perceive that the mind of the Apostle was far different from considering marriage a necessity for the office of Bishop. For if that were the case, why would he himself have remained celibate, or why would he have desired that everyone remain such—namely, celibate—as he himself was (*1 Corinthians 7:7*)? And if it had been an absolutely necessary requirement for a Bishop to marry a wife, why would he have said in verse 1 <note>The OCR reads "v. 11," but the text quotes 1 Corinthians 7:1</note> that *it is good for a man not to touch a woman*, and why in verse 34 would he have declared that *the unmarried care more for the things of the Lord than those who are married, etc.*?
