<meta>This page continues the discussion on the regulations of marriage for the clergy versus the laity. The previous page ended with the concern that if a clergyman leaves his wife, he may not be legally permitted to marry another. The catchword from the previous page is "indeed" (imo).</meta>

-> ### TOPIC XX: ON MARRIAGE <-
-> ### PAGE 202 <-

...indeed <note>Completing the sentence from the previous page: "lest they..."</note>, they rashly expose themselves to the lusts of the flesh, whereas among civil authorities <note>original: *Politicos*—referring to secular leaders or those governed by civil law rather than ecclesiastical law</note> nothing is now more frequent than to repudiate former wives and marry new ones. For when even the slightest disagreement or discord has arisen between spouses, if they cannot be reconciled immediately by the royal judges, they are released from their marriage without delay. <gloss>ξ</gloss> By this reasoning, from a single error concerning "lawful polygamy," such "daughters of vice" are born that one might call **Polygamy** prolific in a physical sense no less than a moral one. Therefore, the Clergy strongly desire to place an obstacle against this evil; just as this is entirely laudable in them, so too if the quarrels of spouses are brought before them, they truly consent to divorce only with great difficulty; indeed,

> it prohibits a Bishop from being able to marry another wife after the first has died. There are indeed others, such as the Papists <note>original: *Pontificii*—referring to the Roman Catholic Church</note> especially, who—in order to weaken the argument drawn from this passage <note>1 Timothy 3:2</note> for establishing the marriage of Priests—explain these words spiritually; but it is rightly answered that one must not withdraw from the literal sense without necessity.

<gloss>ξ</gloss> In this way they again show their agreement with the Jews, who also inclined toward divorce with ease; for which reason God, by wise counsel, commanded—besides other rites—the taking of the *bitter waters of jealousy* <note>Numbers 5:12ff—a ritual for testing the fidelity of a wife</note>, a matter which <margin>b.</margin> **Andreas Acoluthus** <note>1654–1704, a German theologian and Orientalist</note> has very fully explained in his work *On the Bitter Waters*.

<term>Coniugium</term>: Marriage; <term>Polygamia</term>: Polygamy or successive remarriage; <term>Diuortium</term>: Divorce; <term>Clerici</term>: The Clergy; <term>Iudaei</term>: The Jews; <term>Zelotypia</term>: Jealousy/the ritual test of jealousy; <term>Andreas Acoluthus</term>: Author of a notable 17th-century treatise on the biblical "Trial of the Bitter Water."

### TOPIC 20: ON MARRIAGE

On the contrary, they strive with the utmost effort to ensure that those in disagreement are reconciled and divorce is avoided. 

This very point is confirmed again by the words of the previously praised **GREGORY** <note>Likely referring to a prominent ecclesiastical authority or historical commentator mentioned in preceding pages</note> in the following passage: *“If any husband wishes to divorce <note>original: repudiare</note> his wife, he is first of all warned to remain in harmony with her. If this is not possible, they are separated from one another, not by Church judges, but by royal judges, because Church judges only decide on Church matters. However, they do not permit priests to divorce their wives.”*

But let enough be said about the doctrine and practice to clarify this point. Meanwhile, for the sake of more complete knowledge, it is pleasing to present a single detail concerning the ritual for joining <term>newlyweds</term> <note>original Latin: neonymphos</note> from **ALVAREZ** <note>Francisco Álvares (c. 1465–1540), a Portuguese missionary and explorer who provided some of the earliest European accounts of Ethiopia</note>, who was an <term>eyewitness</term> <note>original Greek: αὐτόπτης (autoptes)</note>, as he relates:

◦) *“He saw Metropolitan Mark <note>A high-ranking bishop</note> performing the marriage of a new couple before the main gate of the church in this manner: a bed <note>In this context, a ceremonial couch or seat of honor</note> was placed, on which the groom sat with the bride; the Patriarch circled them with his clergy, carrying censers and crosses; afterwards, with his hand placed on their heads, he said: ‘Take care that you diligently observe what God commands in the Holy Gospel, and reflect that you are one flesh. Therefore, remain joined in soul and heart.’ They remained in this way until the end of the **Eucharist** <note>The service of Holy Communion</note>, and when it was finished, he gave them the blessing.”* And the same is also by the testimony...

---

◦) In the *Travelogue* <note>original: Itinerario</note>, chapter 21, elsewhere 27.

### TOPIC 20: ON MARRIAGE

One may confirm this by the words of **ABBOT GREGORY** <note>Gregory (1595–1658) was an Ethiopian monk and scholar who provided much of the information used by European Orientalists of the time</note>, who speaks on the matter thus: *“They do not join the groom and bride within the Church, except only for Deacons, for whom the Alleluia is sung.”* From this, one may also observe that the Abyssinians <note>Ethiopians</note> do not regard marriage entirely as a Sacrament <note>A formal holy rite conveying divine grace, as defined in Roman Catholic or Orthodox theology</note>, since they are accustomed to performing it outside the temples themselves, as we indicated above. Regarding the other rituals which are perhaps customary for them after the priest has performed the consecration, we will provide them as recounted in the words of the same **GREGORY** in the works of **LUDOLF** <note>Hiob Ludolf (1624–1704), a German scholar known as the father of Ethiopian studies in Europe</note>:

$\pi$) *“The cutting and exchange of the hair-locks <note>original: cincinnorum</note> of the groom and bride is not in use in Ethiopia; perhaps it may be the custom of some specific province, but I do not know of it. However, wedding feasts are held, as well as dances. The groom and bride do not leave the house for ten days, rejoicing with their relatives.”* 

And we hope that these remarks on marriage may be sufficient for a fair-minded judge of these matters.

<margin>
$\pi$) *In the Commentary on the History of Ethiopia, Book III, chapter VI, no. 69, p. 430.* Pregnant women are also to be praised regarding marriage because, before the time for giving birth approaches, they confess their sins and partake in the Lord's Holy Supper <term>Eucharist</term>. They prepare themselves for their blessed departure <note>original Greek: ἀνάλυσιν (analysin), here referring to the soul's release from the body at death</note> as if they were near death, as witnessed by **DAMIANUS A GOES** <note>Damião de Góis (1502–1574), a famous Portuguese humanist and diplomat</note> in his *Treatise on the Customs of the Ethiopians, page 505.* In this way, they surpass many *European women* in their piety.
</margin>

TOPIC XXI: ON DEATH

# Topic XXI
### ON
## DEATH AND THE STATE OF THE
## SOUL AFTER DEATH

*Death*, which is the final boundary of all things, puts an end to marriages and all human affairs. α) Among the Abyssinians <note>A historical term for Ethiopians</note> it is called *mot* <term>ሞት</term>; for the godly it is a release <note>original Greek: ἀνάλυσις, meaning a loosening or departure</note>, but for the ungodly it rightfully and deservedly exists as the most fearful of all fearful things <note>original Greek: Φοβερῶν Φοβερώታτον</note>. β) Indeed, the doctrine concerning death must be treated with all the more circumspection, as much as it—

---

α) As *Horace* says in *Book I of his Epistles to Quintius*. <note>A reference to Horace's Epistles 1.16.79: "mors ultima linea rerum est"</note>
β) We are pleased here by the words of the blessed **MARTIN GEIER** <note>Martin Geier (1614–1681) was a prominent German Lutheran theologian and author of popular devotional works</note>, who compares the terrible hour of death with the siege of a city in *The Voice of Repentance, Feast of the Circumcision, p. 139*, saying,
> One can indeed speak much of sieges when one is not present there, how wretchedly things go; however, when one is present oneself, and sees everything that happens—the enemy launches an assault, breaches are shot, fire is thrown in, everything is in turmoil—there one's laughter is suppressed. But how must it be when the final assault occurs, when Death sets in? He gives you one blow after another; everything is lost; ah, then it is in a bad state.

<summary>This page introduces the theological topic of death, defining it as the end of all human affairs and contrasting its perception between the godly and the ungodly. It includes a vivid metaphor by Martin Geier comparing the process of dying to the violent siege of a city.</summary>

Just as theologians are accustomed to teach this point with great care elsewhere, it will suffice for us at present to demonstrate what the **Ethiopians** <note>original: *Abyſſini*, a historical term for people of the Ethiopian Empire</note> determine and teach on this matter.

And if one asks about the *State and Location* of souls separated from the body, then indeed everything is so entangled and confused that you would not know what you should conclude. For, as we saw above, they honor the Saints with a devotion that goes beyond mere civil respect <note>The author is distinguishing between "civil" honor given to a king or magistrate and the "religious" worship given to divine beings</note>, and yet in their Liturgies they pray more than once for those same Saints' salvation and rest.

Indeed, anyone who reads in their general Liturgy, page 160, column 3, finds that they ask, among other things, for the Lord to remember the souls of the pious, saying:

*Remember, O Lord, the souls of your servants, our father N. N. etc.* <note>original Ge'ez: ተዘከሮሙ፡ እግዚአ፡ ለነፍሳተ፡ አግብርቲከ፡ አቡነ፡ እገሌ፡; original Latin: *Recordare Domine, animarum ſeruorum tuorum Abuna N. N. etc.* Abuna is a title for an Ethiopian bishop meaning "Our Father"</note>

And again:

*Remember, O Lord, our fathers and our brothers, who have fallen asleep and rested in the orthodox faith,* <note>original Ge'ez: ተዘከር፡ እግዚአ፡ አበዊነ፡ ወአኃዊነ፡ እለ፡ ኖሙ፡ ወአዕረፉ፡ በሃይማኖት፡ ርትዕት፡; original Latin: *Memento Domine patrum noſtrorum et fratrum noſtrorum, qui obdormiuerunt, et requieuerunt, in orthodoxa fide*</note>

and so forth; such a reader might perhaps assert that they...

### TOPIC XXI: ON DEATH

[they seem] to determine, along with the **Soul-Sleepers** <note>original: *ψυχοπαννυχίταις* (Psychopannychites), a theological sect that believes the soul "sleeps" or remains unconscious between death and the final judgment</note>, γ) that the souls of those who have died in the Lord are removed from the **Beatific Vision** <note>The immediate sight of God enjoyed by the deceased in heaven</note> until the final day, perhaps sleeping the deep *Sleep of Epimenides* <note>original: *Epimenidis somnum*; a reference to a legendary Greek poet said to have slept for fifty-seven years</note>. We see that the celebrated **HIOB LUDOLF** <note>A 17th-century German scholar who was the leading European expert on Ethiopian history and the Ge'ez language</note> felt the same way about these matters; in his *Commentary* δ), after bringing forward these and many other points, he finally added:

> *I am not able to reconcile these things; for if those fathers and brothers have fallen asleep and rested in the right faith, what need is there to tirelessly weary God with prayers, that He might grant them rest? How can we seek their intercession if they themselves have need of our intercession with God, so that He may release them from—I know not what—chains, and make them rest? And, if they are not in the sight of God, how can they pray and intercede for us,*

<meta>catchword: quod</meta>

---

γ) They were also called **Arabics** elsewhere, named for the region where they spread the seeds of their errors; and likewise **Mortal-Soul Believers** <note>original: *θνητοψυχίται* (Thnetopsychites)</note>, because they believed that souls die out completely along with the bodies. However, since that error was so crude as to be easily refuted, they eventually embraced a somewhat more subtle opinion—namely, that souls are merely resting—which is why they were called **Soul-Sleepers** <note>original: *ψυχοπαννυχίται*</note>. Concerning them, it is worth reading *Nicephorus, Ecclesiastical History, Book V, Chapter 23*; they were also refuted by **John Calvin** in a specific treatise <note>Referring to Calvin's 1534 work *Psychopannychia*, his first theological publication</note>.

δ) *Book III, chapter V, number 42, § 11, page 359.*

Since the matter stands thus, what benefit returns to the soul from that rite established by our Fathers, where on the third and ninth, and likewise the fortieth day, an offering is made to GOD in the Church for the deceased?”* The Angel replied, *“GOD has permitted nothing in His Church to be done either in vain or uselessly; rather, He has granted and commanded that His heavenly and earthly mysteries be performed in His Church. For when the offering is made in the Church on the third day, the soul of the deceased receives some relief* **NB.** *from the pain it feels from the separation from the body, granted by the Angel who has it in his power—for that two-day period is permitted to the soul so that, together with the Angels who are present with it, it may descend to the earth* **NB. Therefore not into heaven,** *to wherever its spirit takes it, etc.”*</margin>

<margin>ρ) For so says **RABBI MOSES ALSHECH** <note>A prominent 16th-century Jewish mystic and biblical commentator based in Safed</note> in his Commentary.</margin>

### TOPIC XXI: ON DEATH

And in this way, finally, those conflicting opinions of the Ethiopians <note>original: *Habessynorum*</note> can be reconciled; for, as we said before, the living pray for the dead, and yet the dead are also called upon by the living. They do this perhaps under the hope and condition that they assume the blessed <note>the souls of the righteous in heaven</note> know the state of the Church Militant <term>Church Militant: The Christian community currently living on earth, viewed as being in a state of spiritual warfare against sin</term> and its members in these lands, or at least that these things become known to them through messenger Angels. Since they believe the blessed are now free from every stain <note>referring to the purification from sin after death</note>, they believe them to be all the more capable of obtain-
<meta>catchword: -ing [obtaining]</meta>

---

*...[commenting] on the Law, folio 18, near the end of Genesis 8:7, after he compared the souls of the wicked to ravens, he writes:* "But it is not so with the dove; she is a pure soul, for although she is good, she found no rest for the sole of her foot to remain in the Garden of Eden <note>Paradise</note>, etc." <note>original Hebrew: אך לא כן היונה היא נפש טורה שעם שטובה היא לא מצאה מנוח לכף רגלה לישאר בג"ע</note> This whole passage can be read in the *Select Rabbinic-Theological Works* <note>original: *Selectis Rabbinico-Theologicis*</note> of the Blessed Abicht <note>Johann Georg Abicht (1672–1740), a German Hebraist and theologian</note>, page 210. Rabbi Joseph Tsarfati <note>original: *Ioseph Tzorpath*</note> holds the same view in the book *The Hand of Joseph* <note>original Hebrew: בס' יד יוסף (*Sefer Yad Yosef*)</note>, as does the author of the *Scepter of Judah* <note>original Hebrew: שבט יהודה (*Shevet Yehuda*), a historical work by Solomon ibn Verga</note> and others. Petrus van Limborch <note>original: *Petrus a Limborch* (1633–1712), a Dutch Remonstrant theologian</note> also leans somewhat toward Jewish views in his *Christian Theology* <note>original: *Theol. Christiana*</note>, published in Amsterdam in 1686, Book VI, chapter 10, as observed by Ludolf <note>Hiob Ludolf (1624–1704), a leading scholar of Ethiopian history and language</note> in his *Commentary*, Book III, chapter 5, number 41, page 356.

σ) For an intercessor is usually called upon in those cases where the Lord clearly does not wish to hear the offending person, just as the disciples interceded with Christ for the Canaanite woman, whom the Lord himself was refusing to hear. For when indignation ceases, intercession also ceases—at least that which is done by another. Now it is clear even from this that the practice of the Abyssinians <note>Ethiopians</note> is in vain, when the living pray for the dead, and the dead are said to pray for the living. For if the living pray for the dead, they pray either with effect <note>original: *fructu*, literally "fruit"</note> or without effect; if the former, what...

The same must be thought regarding the phrase *Abraham’s bosom* <note>A biblical term (Luke 16:22) for the place where the righteous dead await judgment</note>. For this too is usually taken by some either for eternal life itself, or for some other *place* <note>original Greek: που̃ (pou), literally meaning "where" or "the where"</note> by certain others. Regarding *Abraham's bosom*, an elegant commentary by the formerly most celebrated theologian of Tübingen, the late **Lord Doctor Johann Adam Osiander**, is available, published in 1671.

### TOPIC XXI: ON DEATH

...that it might finally come to pass, by reason of instruction, that they would renounce the religious veneration of the deceased—a practice hateful to the Divine Will. The Fathers of the Society themselves discovered this belief among them, reporting that King SUSNEYOS <note>Susenyos I, Emperor of Ethiopia, who briefly converted to Catholicism</note>, who was most favorable to the Romans, once spoke as follows: <margin>$\gamma$) *In the Annual Letters written in 1621, under the title Mission to Ethiopia, p. 224.*</margin> $\gamma$) *"Woe to King ZARA YAQOB <note>The 15th-century Emperor who solidified Ethiopian Orthodox traditions</note>, who caused us to be outside the Faith of the Portuguese today; he will pay for it well there in hell where he is."* <note>original Portuguese: "O malaya en Rey ZERA - IACOB, que elle nos fez eftamos hoje fora de Fe dos Portuguefes, elle o pagarà muybem la inferno onde efta."</note> That is to say: May it go badly for King ZARA YAQOB, who caused...

### TOPIC XXI: ON DEATH

...that today we find ourselves outside the religion of the *Portuguese* <note>Referring to Roman Catholicism</note>, for which he pays a sufficient penalty in hell, where he now resides. Therefore, as I believe these points suffice for understanding what the *Ethiopians* believe concerning the state of souls after death, let me now offer a few remarks regarding the rite of burying the dead as practiced among them. 

First, they are accustomed to wash their dead thoroughly and fumigate them with fragrant incense; then they wrap them in clean clothes, or, if the deceased was of higher noble rank, they cover them all around in a bull's hide. Having been wrapped in this manner, they are placed upon a bier, and the Clerics, equipped with crosses, censers, and holy water, carry the body out—indeed, with such a swift pace that one can hardly keep up with them. 

All the deceased are carried to the churches, either to be buried within them—an honor reserved for the nobility and the Clergy—or to be interred around the church, just as is the custom among us. Before this is done, however, they read a certain passage <term>Pericope</term><note>A specific selection of text from the Bible used for public reading</note> from the *Gospel of John*, and once these rites are finished, they return to their homes. All of this **GREGORY** <note>Abba Gregory, the Ethiopian monk and scholar who was the primary source for European knowledge of Ethiopia in the 17th century</note> also confirms with his own testimony, writing: δ) *Those who have died, they bury within the church or in the courtyard.* <note>Original Ge'ez: ለዘሞቱ፡ ይቀብሩዎ፡ ውስተ፡ ቤተ፡ ክርስቲያን፡ አው፡ በአውደ፡ በዛዊ፡</note>

---

δ) Found in **JOB LUDOLF**'s *Commentary on the History of Ethiopia, Book III, chapter V, note 69, page 439.*

# T o p i c XXII
### CONCERNING
## THE RESURRECTION, THE LAST JUDGMENT, HELL, AND ETERNAL LIFE

**D**eath itself and the state of the soul after death will be followed by the *Resurrection* <note>Ge'ez: ትንሣኤ፡ Tenysae</note>, or the Resurrection of the Dead, where we shall take up again the same bodies which we had here, although they will be unequal in their qualities <note>This refers to the theological belief that while the physical body is restored, the "quality" of that body—whether glorified for the saved or wretched for the damned—will differ</note>.
<meta>catchword: Re-</meta>

---

<margin>a)</margin> The name *Resurrection* <note>ትንሣኤ፡</note> descends from the root *to lift* or *take up* <note>ነሥአ፡</note> (which is clearly related to the Hebrew *nasa* <note>נשא — to lift or carry</note>) and signi— <note>The text breaks off mid-word, likely "signifies"</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the general introduction of Topic XXII (Resurrection) into a detailed linguistic and theological analysis of the terms "Resurrection" and "Judgment," continuing the discussion of the transition from death to the final state of the soul.</meta>

TOPIC XXII: ON THE LAST JUDGMENT

## The Resurrection <note>original Hebrew: קוּם (Qum), meaning "to arise"</note> will be followed by *Judgment* <note>original Ge'ez: ኵነኔ (Kunané)</note>,
## and $\beta$) *the Last Judgment, where Christ is*
## ->The Judge<-

The term commonly signifies *taking up and receiving something,* but in other contexts it also denotes *rousing and awakening a sleeper,* as it is understood in this sense in *Matthew 8:25* and elsewhere. Likewise, it means *to erect and lift up one who is lying down,* as in *Psalm 146:6*. All of these definitions elegantly illustrate the nature of the resurrection, specifically that of the godly. For death is indeed a sleep, as *Job* says in *chapter 14:12*; hence Christ Himself says of *Lazarus*: "Our friend Lazarus has fallen asleep," <note>original Greek: Λάζαρος ὁ φίλος ἡμῶν κεκοίμηται</note> *John 11:11*. Compare also *Deuteronomy 31:16, 2 Samuel 7:12, Daniel 12:2, Matthew 9:24, 1 Corinthians 11:30, etc.* Furthermore, resurrection is also *the repeated raising of that which has fallen,* or as **SAINT DAMASCENE** <note>John of Damascus, an 8th-century monk and theologian</note> says in *On the Orthodox Faith, chapter 28*: "a second standing up of that which has fallen," <note>original Greek: δευτέρα τοῦ πεπτωκότος στάስις</note> with which the Hebrew noun *Tkumah* <note>תְּקוּମָה - standing/resurrection</note> elegantly agrees. This term is properly used for the awakening of sleepers, but is applied metaphorically to the resurrection—not only because of the comparison between death and sleep, but also because of the ease and divine power shown in raising human beings. For, as **AUGUSTINE** <note>the influential 4th-century Bishop of Hippo</note> rightly observes in *Sermon 44 on the Word of God*: "None of us wakes a sleeper in bed as easily as Christ awakens one lying in the grave."

$\beta$) The root of the word for judgment is *Din* <note>original Hebrew: דִּין</note>: *he judged*, which likewise entirely coincides with the Hebrew *Kun* <note>כּוּן</note>, from which *Konen* <note>כּוֹנֵן</note> is derived, meaning *he established, appointed, or decreed.*

<summary>This page provides a linguistic and theological analysis of the concepts of Resurrection and Judgment, using Hebrew, Greek, and Latin sources to define death as a "sleep" from which God easily awakens the faithful. It emphasizes the divine power of Christ as the Judge who establishes and decrees the final state of humanity.</summary>

<keywords>Resurrection, Last Judgment, Christ as Judge, Sleep of Death, Saint John of Damascus, Saint Augustine, Lazarus, Etymology, Hebrew philology, Patristic theology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Topic XXII, moving from the definition of Resurrection to the nature of the Last Judgment. # TOPIC XXII: ON THE LAST JUDGMENT

The **Judge** of the living and the dead will judge all human beings collectively, yet in such a way that only the ungodly come into the judgment of condemnation; from there, being cast down, they will go into *Sheol* <note>original Ge'ez: ሲኦል (Siol); original Greek: γ)</note> or *Hell*, so that they may pay eternal penalties there for their wickedness and, most especially, for their unbelief. As for this world and whatever remains in it, while it perishes through *utter destruction* <note>original Greek: πανολετρία (panolethria)</note> or *the end of the world* <note>original Ge'ez: ኅልቀት፡ ዓለም (Halkat Alam); original transliteration: δ) Halkaty Adam</note>, the godly will go into *heavenly bliss* <note>original Ge'ez: በጽሐ፡ ሰማያዊ (Batha Samayawi); original Greek: ε)</note> so that they may live and reign with GOD
<meta>catchword: in</meta>

***

γ) This term descends from the root *sa'ala* <note>original Ge'ez: ሰአለ</note>, which for the *Ethiopians* just as for the Hebrews, denotes *to ask* or *to seek*. From this comes the name *Sheol* <note>original Ge'ez: ሲኦል</note>, which commonly denotes hell—perhaps because hell is insatiable, according to that saying of *Solomon*: "Sheol and Abaddon are never satisfied" <note>original Hebrew: שאול ואבדה לא תשבענה (Sheol ve-Avaddon lo tishbe'anah); Proverbs 27:20</note>.

δ) It is also called elsewhere *Fetsame* <note>original Ge'ez: ፍጻሜ</note>, which means the *End* <note>1 Peter 1:9</note>, and also *perfection*; see the *Liturgy*, page 168, column 2.

ε) *Batha* <note>original Ge'ez: በጽሐ</note> is derived from the word meaning "he was happy." This root is unknown to the Hebrews, but if you wish to find a certain analogy, it is provided by the root *matza* <note>original Hebrew: מצא</note>, meaning "he found," "he discovered," or "he obtained." This indicates a precursor to glorious bliss; for, as the Apostle suggests, we must first *find* and *grasp* Christ before we may fully enjoy heavenly bliss itself.

<summary>This page describes the final separation of humanity at the Last Judgment, where the ungodly are sent to an "insatiable" Sheol while the godly enter heavenly bliss. The author provides deep etymological roots for these states, drawing connections between Ethiopic, Hebrew, and Greek concepts of destruction and salvation.</summary>

<keywords>Last Judgment, Sheol, Hell, Ethiopic language, Ge'ez etymology, Proverbs 27:20, destruction of the world, heavenly bliss, salvation</keywords>

### TOPIC XXII: ON THE RESURRECTION, ETC.

...into eternity. All these things, which will be no different in the future, the Ethiopians <note>The author uses "Aethiopes" and "Abyssinos" interchangeably to refer to the people of the Ethiopian Empire.</note> also believe. For, as for the *resurrection of the dead*, those who wish to adhere to Christ cannot deny this. For if, according to the sentence of the Apostle in *1 Corinthians 15:17*, "if Christ has not been raised, our faith is futile," <note>original Greek: εἰ ὁ χριστὸς οὐκ ἐγήγερται, ματαία ἡ πίσις ἡμῶν</note> is true, then it certainly follows that this is also true: "if there is no resurrection from the dead, our faith is futile." <note>original Greek: εἰ ἡ ἀνάστασις ἐκ νεκρῶν οὐκ ἐστι, ματαία ἡ πίσις ἡμῶν</note> We have seen that the *Abyssinians* hold this belief through the evidence proven thus far; therefore, it is necessary that they also believe in the *resurrection*. 

But what need is there for words when their own testimonies are present? For they profess the Resurrection in clear words in the *Ethiopian Creed*—or the *Nicene Creed*, as we have shown above—saying: "*And we hope for the resurrection and the life, which shall come for eternity, Amen.*" With these, the words of the *Confession of Claudius* <note>Referring to the "Confessio Fidei" of Emperor Galawdewos (r. 1540–1559), written to defend Ethiopian Orthodox practices against Jesuit criticisms.</note> are in friendly agreement, stated as follows: "*And we hope for the resurrection of the dead to life, which shall come for eternity, Amen.*" These statements suggest once again that the *Abyssinians* neither doubt the salvation they are to obtain in the future, nor do they, in the manner of the *Millenarians* <term>Chiliasts: those who believe in a literal 1,000-year earthly reign of Christ before the final end of the world</term>, expect a "golden age" or better times out of a clearly desperate hope. 5) Then, as to the *Final Judgment*—

---

5) For all Millenarians, the crude as well as the subtle...

<meta>catchword: —ment</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the Ethiopian Church firmly believes in the bodily resurrection of the dead, citing the Nicene Creed and the Confession of Emperor Galawdewos. It distinguishes their orthodox hope for eternal life from the "Millenarian" belief in a literal thousand-year earthly kingdom.</summary>
<keywords>Resurrection, Ethiopian Church, Nicene Creed, Confession of Claudius, Emperor Galawdewos, Chiliasts, Millenarianism, 1 Corinthians 15:17</keywords>

...as far as [it] pertains, they believe most certainly and indubitably, as befits Christians, that this will occur in the future. Furthermore, they assert that a strict account of all words, deeds, and thoughts must be rendered before Christ the Judge; and thus, while all will indeed come *into Judgment* <gloss>Ge'ez: በኵነኔ (*Bekuinane*)</gloss>, none except the impious and the unfaithful will be led *into a condemnatory judgment* <gloss>Ge'ez: በደያኒ (*Badajani*)</gloss> <note>The author distinguishes between the general act of being judged and the specific sentence of condemnation, marked here by the letter zeta (ζ) referring to the footnote below.</note>, and from there be cast down to hell with the devil and his angels. All of which...

<meta>catchword: fi</meta>

<column-break/>

...[the Millenarians] agree on this point: that the earthly kingdom will not last *forever*, as the Ethiopians say in the place cited, but only for about a thousand years. Whence also in the past, Papias and Cerinthus <note>Papias (c. 60–130 AD) and Cerinthus (c. 100 AD) were early Christian figures associated with the belief that Christ would establish a physical kingdom on earth for 1,000 years.</note> were called *Millenarians* <note>original Greek: χιλιάσαι (*chiliasai*), meaning those who count by thousands</note> because of the thousand years which they imagined, as testified by AUGUSTINE in *On Heresies, chapter VIII*, and also by EUSEBIUS in *History of the Church, Book III, chapter 39*. In our own age, the greatest patron of Chiliasm <term>Chiliasm: the theological belief in a future thousand-year golden age or reign of Christ on earth</term>, DR. WILHELM PETERSEN, among other books, did not hesitate to entitle one with this title: *Scriptural Explanation and Proof of the Thousand Years, etc.* <note>original German: "Schrifftmäßige Erklärung und Beweiß der tausend Jahre, etc." Petersen (1649–1727) was a prominent German Pietist theologian.</note>

***

ζ) We cannot indeed conceal the fact here that the word *Judgment* <gloss>Ge'ez: በኵነኔ (*Bekuinane*)</gloss> is sometimes used to denote a judgment of condemnation, even in Holy Scripture; for example, in *Mark 12:40* and *Romans 8:1*. Meanwhile, however, that occurs more rarely and not in its primary meaning, whereas the name *Condemnation* <gloss>Ge'ez: በደያኒ (*Badajani*)</gloss> always [signifies] condemna- [tion]... <meta>catchword: tionem</meta>

If we are to pause, as it were, in summary, we find it described in these words in the *Second Canon, or Statute, of the Apostles* (marked by the Greek letter eta η): <term>JOHN begins</term> <note>original: "Infit IOHANNES." Therefore, let us not show favoritism toward any person who is among us. And, if anyone says that which is not proper to say, let [the others] rebuke him for saying that which is not good. And again, John begins: There are two places, one of life, the other of death; into these two places all separation occurs. The place of life is this: that you love the Lord your GOD, who created you, with your whole heart, and praise him, because he delivered you from death. This is the first Commandment, and the second is this: that you love your neighbor as yourself; in this all the Law and the Prophets are beloved.* <note>This "Two Ways" teaching—the way of life and the way of death—is a very old Christian motif, famously found in the 'Didache' and the 'Epistle of Barnabas'.</note> Furthermore, regarding the nature of the Final Judgment—when it will occur, and with what preceding [signs]...

...denotes [condemnation] to hell, as in *Matthew 10:28, 16:26, 1 Corinthians 11:29*, and elsewhere throughout; sometimes also the actual endurance of infernal torments, as in *Revelation 20:10*; so that consequently this distinction can both stand and elegantly explain a matter that is otherwise difficult. <note>The author is likely concluding a grammatical or theological distinction between the act of judging and the sentence of hell-fire, similar to the distinction on the previous page between "Judgment" and "Condemnatory Judgment."</note>

<margin>η) This Canon is titled elsewhere as: *On the correction of him who speaks what is not proper, and on the love of GOD and the love of neighbor.*</margin>

...in what] precise year it will be, they do not search out with reckless curiosity, knowing well <note>original Greek: "ὅτι οὐχ ἡμῶν ἐσὶ γνῶναι χρόνους ἢ καιροὺς, οὓς ὁ Πατὴρ ἔθετο ἐν τῇ ἰδίᾳ ἐξουσίᾳ"</note> *that it is not for us to know the times or the seasons, which the Father has put in his own power* (θ), and such ignorance is also praiseworthy (ι). θ) *Acts 1:7*. When Saint AUGUSTINE weighed this passage deeply within his own soul in *The City of God, Book XVIII, chapter 53*, he finally wrote: *The one who says "It is not for you to know the times" looses the fingers of everyone who calculates regarding this matter.* <note>Augustine is suggesting that God’s decree "unties the fingers" of those attempting to calculate the end of the world on their hands—meaning it renders their mathematical efforts useless.</note> We could prove this point by bringing forward all possible examples, but the space of these small sheets does not allow for a thorough investigation of that matter. Therefore, we abstain from mentioning them for the present.

ι) For, as the same AUGUSTINE says elsewhere, *It is better not to know than to err; faithful ignorance is better than reckless knowledge.* Saint BERNARD <note>Bernard of Clairvaux (1090–1153), a Cistercian abbot and influential mystic.</note> agrees with this in his *Letters*, as do others. For in these matters, that saying of Socrates holds true: *Those things which are above us, and above our mind and knowledge, are nothing to us.*

ϰ) Indeed, among recent authors, we know of hardly anyone who would define it as material and physical, except for one <term>Dippel</term> <note>Johann Konrad Dippel (1673–1734) was a radical Pietist theologian and alchemist, often criticized by the orthodox for his unusual interpretations of scripture.</note>, who in his *Mystery of Creation* <note>original German: "Geheimniß der Schöpffung"</note>, page 328 of his *Works*, says that the fire of hell is **the material substance on Earth, which shall end up there on the Last Day.**

### TOPIC XXII: ON HELL

...and they do not dispute whether hell is found in the center of the earth, or in the sphere of the sun, <margin>λ</margin> or in another place, <margin>μ</margin> since this has sufficed for them

<margin>λ) That hell is in the center of the earth is asserted—besides many others whom I intentionally pass over—by the distinguished **Laurentius Surius** in *History of the year 1537*, and likewise by **Keckermann** in *System of Theology, Book II, page 288*. This was observed before us by the late Dr. **Deutschmann** in his *Positive-Polemical Theology concerning Hell, Thesis V, page 158*. Furthermore, those who placed it specifically within the orbit of the sun were refuted by the late **Johann Caspar Haferung** in his *Dissertation on Seeking the Seat of Hell in the Sun*, held at Wittenberg in 1716.</margin>

<margin>μ) It is remarkable that **Seneca** <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca, the Roman Stoic philosopher.</note> himself—whom many wish to portray as a complete Christian and, like Zwingli <note>Huldrych Zwingli, the Swiss Reformer, who famously suggested some virtuous pagans might be saved.</note>, among the blessed—denied the existence of hell. For in his *Book on Consolation, chapter XIX*, he says, <note>original: "Quae nobis inferos faciunt terribiles, fabula est."</note> "The things that make the underworld terrifying to us are but a story." But it is even more surprising that **Poiret** <note>Pierre Poiret (1646–1719) was a French mystic whose ideas were often seen as unorthodox by mainstream theologians.</note> himself, who boasts of having all wisdom in divine matters, has stumbled here as well. For in his *Method of Discovery, Part II, Section III*, after speaking of ideas and anxieties—or rather, delusions—he added: "And this indeed is that true and proper hell: the eternal fate of all who have abandoned God." Several more "monsters" produced by Poiret’s imagination were refuted by the late Dr. **Martin Chladenius**, mentioned above with praise, in his *Dissertation on Poiret’s Saints*. The once-celebrated theologian Dr. **Joachim Lange** also accomplished the same in several of his own dissertations,</margin>

<meta>catchword: prae</meta>

<summary>This page documents 18th-century academic debates regarding the physical location of Hell (debating theories of the Earth's center versus the Sun) and critiques philosophical views that treat Hell as a metaphor or a fable.</summary>

<keywords>Hell, Laurentius Surius, Keckermann, Johann Caspar Haferung, Seneca, Pierre Poiret, Martin Chladenius, Joachim Lange, afterlife, geography of the underworld</keywords>

-> # EPILOGUE <-

[...such] things they will have in their bodies? <note>original: "π) Num... talia in corporibus suis sint habituri?" This continues the list of speculative questions about the physical state of resurrected bodies.</note> π) Whether the blessed will be present in the Empyrean heaven <note>The Empyrean was considered the highest part of the heavens, often associated with pure light or fire in ancient and medieval cosmology.</note> rather than in the glorious heaven of GOD? ρ) Regarding these and similar matters, the *Abyssinians* <note>Ethiopian Christians.</note> are not troubled, but walk in the simplicity of faith, and they cannot easily be provoked into introducing various monstrous opinions.

And in this manner, by the help and grace of the supreme DIVINITY, we have brought this ETHIOPIAN THEOLOGY to its conclusion—from head to heel <note>A Latin idiom, *a capite ad calcem*, meaning from start to finish.</note>, from the beginning of knowledge to the end of *Theology*, which is Eternal Life—according to the strength of our own talent and the state of our resources, which are quite meager. It is not permitted for us *Wayfarers* <note>original: "Viatoribus." A theological term for humans still on their earthly pilgrimage, as opposed to those who have reached their destination in heaven.</note> to proceed further; and even if we wished to adopt that motto of the once most glorious Emperor *Charles V* as our own—*FURTHER BEYOND* <note>original: "PLVS VLTRA," the famous motto of the Holy Roman Emperor Charles V.</note>—we would altogether fear that the same thing might happen to us here which a learned hand [wrote] to the same *Charles* when he was vainly besieging the city of *Metz*: σ)

---

π) More could be said on these things, but being studious of brevity we pass them over; meanwhile, one may consult HENR. OPITZ’S *Dissertation on the Stature and Age of the Rising, held in 1707*.

ρ) This is the opinion of almost all *Calvinists*, and specifically of SOHNIVS in *Heidelberg Disputations VII, Th. 127*, as well as in *Volume II, p. 271*.

<summary>This page concludes the study of Ethiopian Theology, noting that the Ethiopian Church avoids speculative questions about the afterlife in favor of simple faith, and ends with the author's closing reflections on the limits of his research.</summary>
<keywords>Ethiopian Theology, Abyssinians, Epilogue, Eternal Life, Charles V, Plus Ultra, Empyrean Heaven, Heinrich Opitz, Georg Sohnius</keywords>

--- Page 263 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 230, completing a sentence about a learned man who adapted the motto of Emperor Charles V. The text concludes the author’s "Ethiopian Theology" and offers a final prayer for the reader’s transition from earthly study to heavenly vision.</meta>

<page-num>231</page-num>

-> # EPILOGUE <-

<warning>The right edge of the page is heavily worn and damaged, resulting in some loss of the paper margin, though the text remains mostly intact.</warning>

—arned man <note>The syllable "ti" completes the word "eruditi" (learned man) from the previous page.</note> ($\sigma$) had once inscribed, in the words: **YOU ARE NOT EQUAL TO MORE, NO FURTHER!** <note>original: "TV PLVRIBVS IMPAR NON VLTRA!" This is a parody of Charles V’s motto *Plus Ultra* (Further Beyond), suggesting a limit to human power or knowledge.</note> Therefore, since it is not permitted for us to proceed further at present, nor to speak of that ineffable vision of **GOD** itself, we stop here and add the finishing touch <note>original: "coronidem." In ancient manuscripts, a *coronis* was a flourishing mark at the end of a work.</note> to our **ETHIOPIAN THEOLOGY**. We pray with a humble heart to **GOD**, Thrice Best and Thrice Greatest, that—just as we trust with undoubting faith and full assurance <note>original: "$\pi\lambda\eta\rho\omicron\phi\omicron\rho\acute{\iota}\alpha$" (plerophoria), a Greek term for absolute certainty.</note> that we shall most certainly obtain this very thing—whenever it shall seem good to His divine mercy, He may lead us from the *Theology of Wayfarers* <note>The study of God possible while still on our earthly journey.</note> to the *Theology of the Blessed* <note>The full, direct knowledge of God enjoyed by those in heaven.</note>, and from being runners <note>Humans struggling toward a goal.</note> to those who have reached—

<sig>P 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: —the goal. (original: "res", likely completing "comprehensores" or those who have grasped the prize).</meta>

***

<note>$\sigma$) Whence a certain learned man, playing upon the name of the city, composed this verse:</note>

->*Stay your journey at Metz, for this Goal is given to you.*<-
<note>original: "Siste uiam Metis, haec tibi Meta datur." A Latin pun on the city of Metz and the word *Meta*, meaning a goal, boundary, or turning point in a race.</note>

Another writer, however, introduces the most glorious Emperor <note>Charles V, Holy Roman Emperor.</note> speaking thus:

->*Neither Metz, nor Maurice, nor Mauritania, nor Magdeburg,*<-
->*Nor Death—why say more? I had been successful in all things.*<-
<note>This refers to the setbacks of Charles V: the Siege of Metz, the betrayal by Maurice of Saxony, his campaigns in Africa (Mauritania), and the resistance of Magdeburg.</note>

Or as the Germans say:

->The city of Metz and the Maiden <-
->Have denied **CHARLES** the dance.<-
<note>original: "Die Mez und die Magd / Haben CARL den Tanz versagt." *Magd* (Maiden) is a pun on the city of Magdeburg. The "dance" refers to the Emperor's ability to control or lead these territories during the Protestant Reformation.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the "Ethiopian Theology" by acknowledging the limits of human understanding and contrasting earthly study (Theology of Wayfarers) with the ultimate vision of God (Theology of the Blessed). It uses historical puns regarding Emperor Charles V's military failures to illustrate that everyone eventually reaches a "limit" beyond which they cannot pass.</summary>

<keywords>Epilogue, Theology of Wayfarers, Theology of the Blessed, Charles V, Metz, Magdeburg, Plerophoria, finishing touch, heavenly vision, limits of knowledge</keywords>

# EPILOGUE

...may finally bring the work to fulfillment. This, together with us, WE VOW and EARNESTLY PRAY for *all Christians throughout the world*, but especially for **those of the household of faith**. <note>original Greek: τοῖς οἰκείοις τῆς πίστεως; a reference to Galatians 6:10, which instructs believers to do good to all, but especially to their own spiritual community.</note>

->A M E N.<-

THE LORD'S PRAYER IN 10 ORIENTAL LANGUAGES
P 5

# APPENDIX
->PRESENTING<-
## THE LORD’S PRAYER IN 10 RARER ORIENTAL LANGUAGES

### I) IN THE ETHIOPIC LANGUAGE <note>Commonly known as Ge'ez, this was the liturgical and scholarly language of the Ethiopian Empire.</note> α)

> **1** Our Father who art in heaven. **2** Hallowed be thy name. **3** Thy kingdom come. **4** Thy will be done, on earth as it is in heaven. **5** Give us this day our daily bread. <note>The original transliterated text uses "Sifajána Zalála ylatýna," referring to the sustenance needed for each day.</note> **6** And forgive us our trespasses as we forgive those who trespass against us. **7** And lead us not into temptation. **8** But deliver us and save us from all evil. **9** For thine is the kingdom and the glory forever and ever. Amen.

### II) IN THE ARMENIAN LANGUAGE β)

> **1** Our Father who art in heaven. **2** Hallowed be thy name. **3** Thy kingdom come. <note>The text for the Armenian version continues on the following page.</note>

---

α) <note>original: "IOBVS LVDOLFVS in Gramm. Aethiop. p. 11..."</note> Hiob Ludolf <note>A pioneering German orientalist (1624–1704) known as the founder of Ethiopian studies.</note> in *Ethiopic Grammar*, page 11, and in his *Commentary on the History of Ethiopia*, Book III; as also Thomas Lüdken <note>A 17th-century scholar also known as Andreas Müller, famous for his collections of the Lord's Prayer in many languages.</note> in *The Lord's Prayer in nearly a hundred versions besides the authentic.*

β) <note>original: "PETR. PAVL. in Doctr. Christ. Armen. pag. 77..."</note> Pietro Paolo <note>Likely referring to a missionary publication of the Catholic Church.</note> in *Armenian Christian Doctrine*, page 77. Compare the *Armenian Bible* printed in Amsterdam, 1666. <note>The 1666 Oskan Bible was the first complete Bible printed in Armenian.</note>

<meta>This page continues "Index II of Subjects," moving from the letter A to the letter B. # INDEX II OF SUBJECTS

### B

***Baptism*** is the Sacrament of initiation, 110.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;for adults, it is conferred by immersion, 111, following.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;for infants, by sprinkling, 112. <note>original: "aspersionem"; the practice of sprinkling or pouring water rather than full immersion.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;can it be performed by fire? 115. <note>A reference to the "baptism of fire" mentioned in the Gospels, sometimes debated as a literal or metaphorical rite.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;is it a "naked rite"? 115, following. <note>A "naked rite" refers to a ceremony that is a mere symbol without conferring spiritual grace; the author is likely arguing against this view.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;it is rightly administered by the Ethiopians, 118, following.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;unhappily repeated by the Roman missionaries, 119, following. <note>The author critizices Catholic missionaries for re-baptizing Ethiopian Christians, which was a point of great religious tension.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;by what rites is it administered among the Ethiopians? 110, following.
The efficacy of ***Baptism***, 115, following; 118.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;its constituent parts, 114, following.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;its names, 116.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;whether the mixing of oil removes its essence, 115.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;who are the "sons of baptism"? 112, 116.
Do the Ethiopians repeat ***Baptism*** every year? 120, following.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;They commemorate the [baptism] of Christ annually by immersing
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;themselves in water, ibid. <note>This refers to the Timkat festival (Epiphany), which commemorates Christ's baptism in the Jordan; Westerners often mistakenly thought Ethiopians were being re-baptized.</note>
The Holy Supper is offered to those who have been ***baptized***, 122, following. <note>The "Holy Supper" refers to the Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note>
The beginning of glorious ***blessedness*** is not granted in this life, 164, 166.
The ***blessedness*** of the faithful is of hope and of reality, 165, following. <note>original: "spei et rei"; a theological distinction between the joy of salvation experienced now through hope and the physical reality experienced in the afterlife.</note>
Are the ***blessed*** to succeed to the place of the fallen Angels? 42.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Are they participants in the beatific vision? 108. <note>The "beatific vision" is the direct self-communication of God to the individual, the ultimate goal of the soul.</note>
***Belkis***, Queen of Ethiopia, sets out to Solomon, 196. <note>Belkis is the name often given in Eastern traditions to the Queen of Sheba.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Whether she was one of Solomon's wives? ibid.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;She conceived a son by Solomon, ibid. <note>Refers to Menelik I, the legendary founder of the Solomonic dynasty in Ethiopia.</note>
